We woke as men do who have trusted their bones to open ground and a night sky that never quite stops breathing. Even here in Grand Canary, where the day comes on like warm bread drawn from an oven, the dark has its own bite: a dew that beads on hair and sleeve-cuff, and a chill that creeps up through the hips and makes the joints complain when you stand.

Down in the barranca the island was already tuning itself. Water ticked over stones with the thin, constant voice of a kitchen clock; a few early goats somewhere above knocked their bells together in sleepy argument; and from far off—too far to name—the first cart on a road gave a wooden groan that seemed to drag behind it the whole notion of commerce. Between those sounds the air held a hush of thyme and sun-heated rock waiting to happen.

For my own part, when I’ve slept out, I like to set blood moving with a run. But the middle of the island is no gentleman’s track. There wasn’t three yards of level ground to be found; every step was a negotiation with lava that bristled like broken crockery. So when Coppinger—portly, determined, and pious about his own body—began going through dumb-bell exercises with two lumps of prickly stone as if the cliff were his private gymnasium, I followed suit, heaving and stretching until the stiffness was driven from the shoulders and into sweat.

Coppinger had roughed it a good deal in his time. He was a doctor of medicine amongst other things, and he took out a new degree every other year as some men take out new boots. Health theories with him were not opinions; they were a religion, practised with an earnestness that would have been admirable if it hadn’t been so tyrannical.

There had been rain two days before, and a thin trickle still ran along the bottom of the barranca. We went down to it and washed. The water was cold enough to make the gums smart when it touched the mouth, but it carried with it that clean, stony taste that makes you feel—however briefly—that you’ve been rinsed of the town. We brushed our teeth there too. On this sort of expedition a toothbrush is the greatest luxury imaginable—more civilised, in its way, than a bed.

“Now,” said Coppinger when we had emptied our pockets, “there’s precious little grub left, and it’s none the better for being carried in a local Spanish newspaper.”

“Yours is mostly tobacco ashes.”

“It’ll get worse if we leave it. We’ve a lot more bad scrambling ahead of us.”

That was plain enough. So we sat beside the stream where the rock formed a natural table, and ate all that remained: a few crushed odds that tasted of paper, heat, and yesterday’s handling. It wasn’t food so much as a reminder of food, but it took the edge off. I could not help thinking, even as I chewed, of the fonda at Santa Brigida—ten hard miles away—and of the PATRON’S old country wine, dark and honest and smelling faintly of cask and sun. If the English hotels in Las Palmas knew what splendid vintages could be had with a little diplomacy in the mountain villages, their grand old “cellars” would be laughed out of fashion inside a week.

To tell the truth, the two mummies Coppinger had already gathered would have satisfied my small ambition. Sewn into goatskins that had gone brittle as paper, they gave off dust like puffballs when disturbed—as if the dead were trying to return to the cliff. But you know what Coppinger is. He fancied he had come upon traces of an old Guanche university, or sacred college—something of that kind—and he would not be satisfied until he had ransacked every cave that pocked the whole face of the precipice. He had plenty of stuff left for the flash apparatus, twenty-eight more films in his Kodak, and argued that we might as well do the job now as make another journey on purpose.

So he took the crowbar; I shouldered the rope; and away we went up to the ridge where the sun had baked us the day before as if we were loaves set too near the fire.

These caves were not easy to reach—otherwise they would have been looted long ago. Coppinger, who on principle makes out he knows all about these matters, said that in old Guanche days they had ladders of goatskin rope which they could haul up after them, keeping out undesirable callers. No better plan occurred to me, and the cliff certainly looked as though it had been designed to discourage curiosity.

The mouths of the caves were in a more or less level row, thirty feet below the ridge and fifty above the bottom. Spanish curiosity, I had noticed, does not go far where it cannot walk. To ladder such openings from below would have been cumbersome; a light knotted rope is easily carried. Our plan was to descend to each mouth from above, slip down to the foot of the cliff, and begin again ab initio for the next.

Coppinger was plucky enough, and he had a good head on a height, but there is no disguising that he was portly and nearer fifty than forty-five. For such work he must have been keen indeed. I went first each time, easing myself over the edge and “walking” down the face in that fly-fashion you have to adopt when the rock gives you no decent hold and your whole trust is in a thin bootlace of rope. From below there’s little help a man can offer—only his best wishes shouted up. My hands were soon cut raw; the rope bit, warmed, slipped; each grip was a bargain with gravity.

I tried to save him where I could. The first three caves were small and empty, mere dens—store-places, seemingly—and I asked him to take them for granted. But he insisted on clambering down to each one in person. When he decided that one of my “granaries” was a prison, another a pot-making factory, and another a schoolroom for young priests, he naturally said he had no reliance on my judgment. Thorough-going archaeologists have a fatal imagination, and Coppinger’s was in fighting trim.

As the day rose higher, the heat began to press with the steady weight of a hand on the back of the neck. The air in the cliff-side grew close, smelling of old bat, dry stone, and an ancient dust that puffed up at the slightest disturbance and clung to the tongue. Even the island’s sounds altered: the stream below seemed thinner and farther away; the wind in the scrub made a tired whisper rather than a song; and, once, some distant hammering drifted up from a village—metal on metal—thin as a nail and twice as unpleasant, like a promise of modernity that didn’t belong in such a place.

Coppinger began to have had enough, though he would not admit it. He was game, and he insisted on going on longer than was safe. I disliked it. The drop from the ridge was seldom less than eighty feet. But at last he was forced to give up.

I suggested marching for Santa Brigida forthwith, before hunger became a kind of rage. He wouldn’t do it.

“There are three more openings,” he said, peering along the cliff as if the rock itself owed him an answer. “And if you won’t do them, I must make another effort myself.”

He tried to make out he was conferring a favour on me by offering to accept a report from my untrained observation. I refused to see it in that light. I was tired; the sweat had soaked me through; my head ached from the sun’s violence; my hands stung as though rubbed with salt.

Coppinger might be tired, but enthusiasm held him up like a stiff drink.

“Look here,” he said, “there’s no knowing what you may find, and if you do lay hands on anything, remember it’s your own. I shall have no claim whatever.”

“Very kind of you,” I said, “but I’ve got no use for any more mummies done up in goatskin bags.”

“Bah! That’s not a burial cave up there. Don’t you know the difference yet in the openings? Be a good fellow. It doesn’t follow that because we have drawn all the rest blank, you won’t stumble on a good find for yourself.”

“Oh, very well,” I said at last, because he was set on it and I was too wearied to keep refusing. I stumbled over fallen rock, along a ledge, and scrambled up by the fissure in the cliff that saved us the two-mile detour of the first approach. I wrenched out the crowbar, jammed it into a new purchase, and went over the side again, hands smarting worse at every rope-burned grip.

Swinging into the cave mouth was awkward. The rock above overhung—or else it had broken away below, which came to the same thing. I managed it, though I landed with an ugly thump on my back and very nearly lost the rope. Had it slipped away then, Coppinger could not have flicked it into my reach from where he waited below.

At first glance I knew this cave differed from the others. Those had been mere rounded hollows, dens scooped out anyhow. This had been faced up with cutting tools: angles clean, sides smooth and flat. The walls inclined inward toward the roof in a fashion that reminded me of some architecture I had seen before but could not at once place. There were several rooms connected by passages. I was pleased—purely for the sake of labour saved—to discover that the other openings Coppinger wanted were merely windows or doorways into adjoining chambers.

I searched. I ran my fingers along the smoothed stone, feeling for scratches, for the faint rise of markings. No inscriptions. No wall-painting. Nothing but bats—dry leathered bodies that shifted and whispered above me, their small squeaks stitching the dark to the dark—and the powdery litter of ages.

To keep from seeming too quick—Coppinger always suspects a man of slurring his work if it’s done briskly—I lit a cigarette and smoked it through, the smoke hanging low and tasting of stale mineral air. Then I leaned out at the rope and shouted down what I had found.

He turned up an anxious face. “Have you searched it thoroughly?” he bawled.

“Of course I have. What do you think I’ve been doing all this time?”

“No, don’t come down yet. Wait a minute. I’m making fast the Kodak and the flashlight apparatus on the end of the rope. Pull them up and make me half a dozen exposures—there’s a good fellow.”

“Oh, all right,” I said, and hauled the gear up into the cave.

The photographs would be dull enough, but Coppinger liked dullness. He trusted it. One has to be careful of halation in such interiors. There was a ledge like a seat beside each doorway, and I lodged the camera on it for steadiness, snapping the flash from behind and above. The sudden white flare made the bat-wings beat like thrown cloth and left a hot smell in the air, sharp and metallic.

I got four chambers that way and came to one where the ledge was higher and wider. I put down the camera, wedged it level with scraps of stone, and sat to recharge the flash.

The moment my weight came onto that ledge there was a sharp crackle, like the breaking of old sugar, and down I went half a dozen inches. I was up at once, snatching the Kodak as it began to slide.

And then I confess I felt pleased. Here, at any rate, was a cupboard of sorts—a hollow hermetically sealed with cement. If they had taken the trouble to close it up so, the odds were there was something inside worth hiding.

At first there was nothing but dust and rubble. I lit a candle end, its flame breathing in the stale air, and cleared away the loose stuff. Presently my knife began to strike something that did not chip like cement. It came away in regular layers, thin sheets. When I took a fragment toward the entrance where daylight licked the darkness, I saw each layer had two faces: one side a shiny pale stuff like talc; on it smeared a coating of dark toffee-coloured matter that might have been wax. The toffee surface was worked over with a pattern—scratched lines, purposeful, repeated.

I do not pretend to knowledge in these matters. Coppinger had impressed on me often enough that the Guanche could not write, and with that in my mind I did not guess—though I might have—that the patterns were letters in some obsolete character. I only knew that anything so carefully made, so carefully hidden, was worth taking.

So I set to work quarrying it out with the heel of my boot and a pocket-knife. The sheets were more or less stuck together; I did not try to separate them. They fitted the cavity exactly, but by smashing down its front I could reach the lower edges, and then I hacked through the bottom layers until I got the bulk out in one solid piece. It measured perhaps twenty inches by fifteen by fifteen. It looked heavy, but it was not as weighty as its size promised; it handled oddly, as if the material remembered heat and held it.

When I had taken the remaining photographs, I lowered the lump down to Coppinger on the end of the rope. Then I descended myself. The cliff gave me back to sun and wind and the sound of my own breath.

The bundle lay on the ground. Coppinger was on all fours beside it, as reverent as a cook bending over a broth he has watched all night. His eyes were shining; his fingers hovered as though touch might ruin everything.

“What is it?” I asked.

“I don’t know yet,” he said, almost snarling with delight. “But it is the most valuable find ever made in the Canary Islands—and it’s yours, you unappreciative beggar; at least what there is left of it. Oh, man, man, you’ve smashed up the beginning and you’ve smashed up the end of some history that is probably priceless. It’s my fault. I ought to have known better than set an untrained man to do important exploring work.”

“If anything’s gone wrong, it’s your fault,” I said. “You told me there was no such thing as writing among these ancient Canarios. I took your word for it. For anything I knew, the stuff might have been something to eat.”

“It isn’t Guanche work at all,” he snapped. “You ought to have known that from the talc. Great heavens, man, have you no eyes? Haven’t you seen the general formation of the island? There’s no talc here.”

“I’m no geologist,” I said. “Is this imported literature, then?”

“Of course. It’s Egyptian—that’s obvious at a glance. Though how it’s got here I can’t tell yet. It isn’t stuff you can read off like a newspaper. The character’s a variant on any we’ve discovered so far. And this waxy stuff spread over the talc—it’s unique. Some sort of mineral, I think; perhaps asphalt. It doesn’t scratch like animal wax. I’ll analyse it later. Why anyone invented it and then let such a splendid notion drop out of use is a marvel. I could stay gloating over this all day.”

“Well,” I said, “if it’s all the same to you, I’d rather gloat over a meal. It’s ten miles hard going to the fonda, and I’m hungry as a hawk. Look—do you know it’s four o’clock? It takes longer than you think, climbing down to each cave and up again.”

He spread his coat on the ground and wrapped the lump with tender care, refusing to tie it with rope for fear of breaking more edges. He insisted on carrying it himself for most of the way to Santa Brigida. Only when fatigue had him within an ace of dropping did he allow me to take my turn.

“I suppose you may as well carry the stuff,” he snapped, “seeing that after all it’s your own.”

The fonda, when we reached it, smelled like salvation: garlic crushed into oil; stewed peppers; bread with a crust that crackled when broken; the faint sweet-sour of wine sleeping in wood. I ate as well as could be procured, drank a bottle of the Patron’s old Canary, and turned into bed after a final pipe, feeling the body settle into itself as if the mattress were a warm dough. Coppinger dined too, but I have reason to believe he did not sleep much. The house had its own night noises—murmured voices, a door-latch, an occasional cough—but his wakefulness seemed to sit on top of them, alert and hungry.

In the morning I found him still poring over the find, heavy-eyed, unshaven, brimming with enthusiasm as if he had been living on nothing but the idea of it.

“Do you know,” he said, “that you’ve blundered on the most valuable historical manuscript the modern world has ever seen? Of course, with your clumsy way of getting it out, you’ve done an infinity of damage. Those top sheets you shelled away and spoiled contained probably an absolutely unique account of the ancient civilisation of Yucatan.”

“Where’s that, anyway?”

“In the middle of the Gulf of Mexico. It’s all ruins to-day, but once it was a very prosperous colony of the Atlanteans.”

“Never heard of them. Oh—yes, I have, though. They were the people Herodotus wrote about, weren’t they? But I thought they were mythical.”

“They were very real,” he said, and struck the sheets lightly with his knuckles as though to make them answer, “and so was Atlantis—the continent where they lived, which lay just north of the Canaries here.”

“What’s that crocodile sort of thing with wings drawn in the margin?”

“Some beast that lived in those days. The pages are full of them. That’s a cave-tiger. And that’s some sort of colossal bat. Thank goodness the author had the sense to illustrate fully, or we should never have been able to reconstruct the tale—at any rate we could not have understood half of it. Whole species have died out since this was written, just as a whole continent has been swept away and three civilisations quenched. The worst of it is, the man was highly educated and writes a very bad fist. I’ve hammered at it all night and only managed a few sentences here and there.” He rubbed his hands, delighted. “It will take me a year’s hard work to translate properly.”

“Every man to his taste,” I said. “I’m afraid my interest wouldn’t last as long as that. But how did it get there? Did your ancient Egyptian come to Grand Canary for his lungs and write it because he felt dull in a cave?”

“I made a mistake there,” he said testily. “The author was not Egyptian. The similarity of the character misled me. The book was written by one Deucalion—priest or general, perhaps both—and he was an Atlantean. How it got there I don’t know yet. Probably that was told in the last pages, which a certain vandal smashed up with his pocket-knife in getting them away from where they were stowed.”

“That’s right,” I said. “Abuse me. Deucalion, you say? There was a Deucalion in Greek mythology—one of the two who escaped the Flood. Their Noah.”

“The swamping of Atlantis might very well correspond to the Flood.”

“Is there a Pyrrha then? She was Deucalion’s wife.”

“I haven’t come across her yet. But there’s a Phorenice, who may be the same. She seems to have been the reigning Empress, as far as I can make out at present.”

I looked more closely at the margin drawings. They were perfectly intelligible though the perspective was wrong. A cave-tiger, enormous, seemed to be trying to puff down a mammoth; elsewhere a bat like a nightmare spread its wings across the page. There was something in that crude line-work—its confidence, its untranslatable immediacy—that made me think of all the voices that vanish when a language dies: the taste-words, the sound-words, the names for winds and breads and beasts that cannot be carried whole into another tongue.

“Weird beasts,” I said. “And big. By Jove, I shouldn’t care to have lived in those days.”

“Probably they had some way of fighting them,” he said. “That will show itself as I get along with the translation.”

He looked at his watch and yawned. “I suppose I ought to be ashamed, but I haven’t been to bed. Are you going out?”

“I shall drive back to Las Palmas,” I said. “I promised a man a round at golf this afternoon.”

“Very well. See you at dinner. I hope they’ve sent back my dress shirts from the wash. O Lord! I am sleepy.”

I left him going up to bed, and went outside to order a carriage. The street had begun its day-noise: children calling, hooves striking stone, a radio somewhere making a tinny imitation of music. It struck me then how quickly a place’s true sound can be overwritten—how easily the old acoustic fabric is torn and patched with cheap modern racket, until even memory begins to hear wrong.

There we parted for a considerable time.

At the hotel in Las Palmas a cable waited for me: home for business forthwith. A Liverpool boat lay in harbour, and I just managed to catch her as she was steaming out. The boatmen made a small fortune out of my hurry.

Now Coppinger was only an hotel acquaintance. When I got back to England I was up to the eyes in work, and I’m afraid I did not think much more of him. One doesn’t, with people one meets casually abroad. It must have been at least a year later that I saw, in a paragraph of a paper, that he had given the lump of sheets to the British Museum, and that their estimated worth was ten thousand pounds at the lowest valuation.

That was a revelation. He had so repeatedly impressed upon me that the things were mine by right of discovery that I wrote him a pointed note, mentioning that he seemed to have been making rather free with my property.

Back came a stilted letter beginning, “Doctor Coppinger regrets,” and with it the English translation of the wax-upon-talc manuscript. He “quite admitted” my claim, and “trusted that the profits of publication would be sufficient reimbursement for any damage received.”

I had not expected him to take that tone, and wrote back a warm reply to that effect. The only answer I got was through a firm of solicitors, who stated that all further communications with Dr. Coppinger must be made through them.

I regret publicly the line he has taken; but since the affair has gone so far, I am disposed to follow out his proposition. Accordingly the old history is here printed. The credit—and the responsibility—of the translation rests with Dr. Coppinger; and whatever revenue accrues from readers goes to the finder of the original talc-upon-wax sheets: myself. If there is a further alteration in this arrangement, it will be announced publicly at a later date. But at present that appears most unlikely.

The public reception was done at last.

The sentence had been spoken out, the Empress Phorenice named with all the regulated ardor that ancient custom required, and the new Viceroy set in place beneath a roof heavy with age and gilding. I had, in form, delivered up the reins. Tatho had, in form, seated himself upon the snake-throne, and bent his neck to receive the chain of gems that meant supreme office. Then the hall—bright as a brazier’s mouth with gold tile and polished metal—filled with its appointed violence of sound: drums that struck like fists on a door, trumpets whose blare made the roof-beams shiver, and the roar of voices crying, as in duty bound, “Long Live Tatho!” “Flourish the Empress!”

He rose for his first progress around the council-chamber, and I followed behind him with folded arms and lowered head, as the ritual demanded. Twenty years ago I had walked this same circuit to the same clamoring music; now fortune had turned its face. It is a hard schooling, learning to carry a passionless mask when one’s place is taken before a crowd. Yet for all that etiquette forbade any cry for me, it could not forbid the eyes. Along the glittering lines of soldiers, courtiers, chiefs, and statesmen, kindness flickered—quick glances that warmed and stung in the same breath. It is not often the fallen are met with tenderness.

On these ceremonial days the people present may bring petitions; may accuse the retiring head without fear of vengeance; may even offer their own plans for future governance. Vanity may be forgiven me this once: not a voice was raised against my twenty years of rule, nor did any call for change. We made the circuit three times as the rite prescribes, and their silence was approval.

So, one behind the other—the new Viceroy and the old—we marched over the golden tiles toward the farther wall, and the great officers of state left their stations to join our train. We came to the door of the private chambers which an hour ago had been mine.

Ah, well. I had no home now in any of Yucatan’s wondrous cities. A man should be thankful enough to be permitted to sail back to Atlantis with his head still on his shoulders, and yet bitterness rose in me like smoke.

Tatho gave the formal summons, “Open ye to the Viceroy,” and slaves within swung the massive stone valves wide. He entered; I followed at his heels. The others halted at the threshold, sending valedictions like incense, and then the stone clanged shut behind us.

We passed into the inner chamber, and for the first time the court’s forced breathing was behind us. Tatho turned at once, folded his arms meekly, and bowed low before me.

“Deucalion,” he said, “believe me—I have not sought this office. It was thrust upon me. Had I not accepted, my head would have paid forfeit, and another man—your enemy—would have been sent in your place. The Empress does not permit her will to be questioned.”

“My friend,” I answered, “my brother in all but blood, there is no man living in Atlantis or her territories to whom I would rather hand over my government. For twenty years I have ruled Yucatan, and Mexico beyond—first for the old King, then for this new Empress. I know the colony as a man knows the lines of his own palm: her cities, her palaces, her pyramids, her people. I have hunted beasts and savages in the forests. I have built roads and tamed rivers to bear shipping. I have fostered arts and crafts like a merchant, and three times each day have spoken the cult of the Gods with my own lips. Through lean years and fat years I have labored for the land’s prosperity and Atlantis’ strengthening, and I have come to love these peoples like a father. To you I bequeath them, Tatho, with tender supplication for their interests.”

“It is not I who can carry on Deucalion’s work with Deucalion’s power,” he said, “but rest content that I shall do my humble best to follow in your footsteps. Believe me, I came out with a thousand regrets. I would have died sooner than take your place had I known how sharply the supplanting would trouble you.”

“We are alone here,” I said. “A man may show his natural self without tarnishing ceremony. Your coming was sudden. Till an hour ago—till you demanded audience—I thought to rule longer. Even now I do not know why I am deposed.”

“The proclamation said,” Tatho replied, “‘We relieve our well-beloved Deucalion of his present service, because we have great need of his powers at home in our kingdom of Atlantis.’”

“A mere formality,” I said.

He looked uneasily toward the hangings, as though he expected the cloth to grow ears, and drew me toward the center of the chamber. His voice sank.

“I do not think so,” he whispered. “I believe she has need of you. Troublous times are coming, and Phorenice wants the ablest men in the kingdom ready to her call.”

“You may speak openly,” I told him. “We are in the heart of the pyramid, built by a man’s length of solid stone. I oversaw the laying of every course. Besides, in Yucatan we do not make a fine art of listening at doors. We count it shame.”

He shrugged. “I act according to my education. At home, a loose tongue makes a loose head, and there are those who trade in tales. Still—what I say is this: the throne shakes, and Phorenice sees the need of sturdy props. So she has sent this proclamation.”

“But why come to me?” I demanded. “It is twenty years since I sailed to this colony, and from that day I have not returned to Atlantis once. I know little of the old country’s politics. The scraps of news that drift to us over the ocean read thinly here. Yucatan is another world, Tatho—new interests, new people, new everything. To us Atlantis is a shadow far across water. It is for this new world I have striven all these years.”

“If you have had small leisure to brood over your fatherland,” he said, “Atlantis has had leisure to admire her brilliant son. Over yonder your name carries magic. When you and I were lads, colleges taught that the men of the past were greatest; to-day that teaching is changed. It is Deucalion they hold up as model. Mothers name their sons Deucalion as the best birth-gift they can give. Your name is a household word. Indeed there is only one name that approaches it in familiarity.”

“You trouble me,” I said, frowning. “I have tried to do duty for its own sake, not for the pattings of the vulgar. And if names must be on men’s mouths, let them be the Gods’.”

“The Gods?” Tatho said, and his shrug was half weary, half mocking. “They occupy us very little these latter years. With our modern science we have gone past the tether of the older Gods, and no new one has appeared. No, my Lord Deucalion—if it were merely Gods you competed with, your name would be a thousand times the better known.”

“Of human names,” I said, “the Empress’ should come first, now the old King is dead.”

“She would have it so,” Tatho replied—and something in his tone showed there was more behind the words than politeness could carry.

I drew him to one of the marble seats and leaned toward him, familiarly, as we had leaned together over a hundred hard cases in our early postings, when a hay-byre’s ownership might take as much law and sweat as the fate of a rebellious town does now.

“I speak,” I said, “not to the Viceroy of Yucatan, but to my old friend Tatho—of the Priests’ Clan as I am—who worked beside me in hamlets and villages, in lesser towns and greater, as we learned war and the tedious art of ruling men. I speak in your private abode that was mine two hours ago. I would have an answer with the plainness we used then.”

He sighed, almost whimsically. “I almost forget how to speak plain words. We have grown so polished that bald truth would be hissed as indelicate. But for those early years’ sake, I will try. Tell me, old friend—what is it?”

“What of this new Empress?” I asked. “Tell me of the changes. What has Phorenice done to make the throne unstable?”

His frown deepened. “If I did not know you to be honest as our Lord the Sun, your questions would carry mischief. Phorenice has a short way with those who discuss her policies for any purpose but praise.”

“You can leave me ignorant,” I said, letting chill touch my voice.

He saw it at once. “You force me back to my old self,” he said with a half smile. “Though it is hard to cast off caution learned in twenty years. Still—whatever has happened to the rest of us, you at least have not changed. And I am ready to trust you with my life if you ask it. Indeed, you do ask me that when you bid me speak all I know of Phorenice.”

I nodded. The old confidence returned like a familiar cadence.

“The Gods will it that I return to Atlantis,” I said, “and what follows the Gods alone know. But it would serve me to land with some knowledge of Phorenice. At present I am as ignorant of her as some savage from Europe or mid-Africa.”

“What would you have me tell?”

“Tell all. I know only this—that a woman reigns where ancient law said a man should rule; that she is not of the Priests’ Clan from which rulers must be drawn; and that, from what you hint, she has made the throne totter. In the old King’s day it stood firm as the everlasting hills.”

“History has moved with pace since then,” said Tatho, “and Phorenice has spurred it. You know her origin?”

“I know only what I have said.”

“She was a swineherd’s daughter from the mountains,” he said. “This is never whispered now. She has declared herself a daughter of the Gods with miraculous birth and upbringing. She has decreed it sacrilege to question it, and has ordered burnt all those who seem to recollect her earthly origin. The fable passes current for truth. You see what faith I put in you, Deucalion, by telling you this.”

“There has always been trust between us.”

“I know. Yet suspicion becomes a habit, and habits cling. Listen further. Zaemon—you remember—was governor of the swineherd’s province. Zaemon’s wife saw the girl and took her away to adopt and bring up as her own. It is said the swineherd and his woman objected. Perhaps they did. In any case I know they died. Phorenice was taught arts and graces, and raised as a daughter of the Priests’ Clan.”

“But adopted only,” I protested.

“The omission of the word ‘adopted’ was her will early,” Tatho said dryly, “and she learned early to have her will made fact. Before she was fifteen she ruled not only the women of the household, but Zaemon also—and the province beyond Zaemon.”

“Zaemon was learned and devout,” I said. “A searcher into higher mysteries. But as ruler he was always flabby.”

“I do not deny opportunities served her,” said Tatho, “but genius served her too. For a woman to raise herself at all from what she was is remarkable. Not one in a thousand, placed as she was, would have become more than some sturdy countryman’s wife, with no one caring of pedigree. But Phorenice—she took it into her head to train like a man-at-arms, to practice with the utensils of war; and then, before any quite knows how, a rebellion broke out, and there she was—a slip of a girl—leading Zaemon’s troops.”

“Zaemon in the field was a derision.”

“Hear me. She put down the rebellion masterfully, and gave the conquered their choice between sword and service. They fell into her ranks at once and were faithful from that moment. There is a marvellous fascination about the woman.”

“Her historian seems to feel it,” I said, letting the barb show.

“Of course I have,” he replied without shame. “Every one who sees her comes under her spell. Frankly, I am in love with her too, and look upon my coming here as detestable exile. All near her—high and low—love her, even though they know it may be her whim to send them to execution the next minute.”

I suppose contempt showed in my face.

“You despise our weakness?” he said. “You were always strong, Deucalion.”

“At least,” I said, “you see me unmarried. I have found no time to palter with women’s fripperies.”

“Ah,” he laughed softly, “but these colonists here are crude. Wait till you see the ladies of the court, my ascetic.”

“It occurs to me,” I said, “that I lived in Atlantis before I came out here, and saw as much of court life as most men. Yet then also I felt no inducement to marry.”

“Atlantis has changed so you would hardly know her,” Tatho said. “A new era has come over everything—especially the other sex. I remember the women of the old King’s time—how monstrous uncomely, how they knew nothing of walking or carrying themselves, how barbaric their notion of dress. I dare swear your ladies here in Yucatan are not so provincial now as ours were then. But you should see them today at home. They are delicious. And above all in charm is the Empress. Oh, Deucalion—you shall see Phorenice in her beauty and magnificence soon, and you will go down on your knees and repent.”

“I may see,” I said, “and, because you say so, I may alter. The Gods make all things possible. But for the present I remain celibate and not wishful otherwise. Continue your history.”

“It is a long story of success,” he said. “She deposed Zaemon from his government in name as well as in fact. The news spread, and the Priests’ Clan rose in wrath. Two neighboring governors were ordered to join forces, take her, and bring her for execution. Poor men—they tried. They attacked her surely enough, but in battle she laughed at them. She killed both and made slaughter among their troops. To those who remained alive and became her prisoners she made her usual offer—the sword or service. Naturally they chose quickly. To common people one ruler is much like another. So her army was reinforced again.

“Three times bodies of soldiery were sent against her, and three times she was victorious. The last was a final effort. Before, it had been the custom to despise this adventuress. But then the priests began to realize their peril—to see the throne itself in danger—and to know that if she were to be crushed they must put forth their utmost. Every man who could carry arms was pressed. Every known art of war was ordered into employment. It was the largest, best-equipped army Atlantis had then ever raised, and the Priests’ Clan put in supreme command their general—Tatho.”

“You!” I cried.

“Even myself,” he said. “And mark you, I fought my utmost. I was not her creature then. When I set out—because they wished to spur me—the High Council pointed out my prospects. The King we had known so long was ailing and old, wrapped in mysteries till earthly matters nauseated him. At any time he might decide to die. The Priests’ Clan chooses the new ruler, but it notes popular sentiment; and a general who came home victorious from a great campaign, freeing a harassed people from constant war, would be the idol of the moment. These things were told me solemnly in full council.”

“They promised you the throne?”

“Even that. So I went with a high stake. Phorenice I had never seen, and I swore to take her alive and give her to be sport for my soldiery. I had great confidence in my strategy then. But the old Gods I trusted remained old and taught me nothing new. I drilled my army by the forms you and I learned together; I armed them with the choicest weapons we knew—sling and mace, bow and spear, axe and knife, sword and throwing fire. I covered their bodies with metal plates. Even their bellies I cared for, driving droves of cattle behind the fighting troops.

“But when the encounter came, they might have been men of straw for all the harm they did. Out of her own brain Phorenice had made fire-tubes that cast a dart which would kill beyond two bowshots. The way she handled her troops dazzled me. They threatened one flank, harried the other. It was not war as we had been used to. It was a newer and deadlier game. I watched my splendid army eaten away as waves eat a sandhill. Never once did I get the chance to force close action. These tactics—born of her invention—were beyond my art. We were eight to her one, and our close-packed numbers only made us easier slaughter. Panic came, and those who could fled. I had no wish to go back and earn the axe that waits for the unsuccessful general. I tried to die where I stood. But death would not come. It was a fine melee, Deucalion, that last one.”

“And so she took you?”

“I stood with three others, back to back, with a ring of dead around us and a ring of the enemy hemming us in. We taunted them to come on. But in hand-to-hand they had learned we could hold our own, and they called for fire-tubes to strike us down from a distance. Then up came Phorenice.

“‘What is this to-do?’ she says.

“‘We seek to kill Lord Tatho, who led against you,’ say they.

“‘So that is Tatho?’ says she. ‘A fine figure of a man, and a pretty fighter after the old manner. Doubtless he is one who would acquire the newer method. See now, Tatho—my custom is to offer those I vanquish either the sword (which, believe me, was never nearer your neck than now) or service under my banner. Will you choose?’

“‘Woman,’ I said, ‘fairest I have ever seen, finest general the world has ever borne—you tempt me sorely. But there is tradition in our Clan that we should be true to the salt we eat. I am the King’s man still, and I can take no service from you.’

“‘The King is dead,’ says she. ‘A runner has just brought the tidings, meaning them for your hands. And I am the Empress.’

“‘Who made you Empress?’ I asked.

“‘The same most capable hand that has given me this battle,’ says she. ‘It can be a kind hand also, as you may learn if you choose. With the King dead, Tatho is masterless. Is Tatho in want of a mistress?’

“‘Such a mistress as you,’ I said, ‘yes.’

“And from that moment, Deucalion, I have been her slave. You may frown. You may rise and walk away. But keep back your harshest judgment till after you have seen Phorenice yourself—in the warm and lovely flesh. Then your own senses will plead for me, and win back, perhaps, your old esteem.”

Tatho’s words were no draught of poppy to lull a man into sleep. They lay on my tongue like smoke from green wood, stinging and bitter, and all that night I turned them over as one turns a hard seed between the teeth, wondering why it will not break.

For twenty years I had wrapped myself in the governance of Yucatan as in a plain cloak against weather—work, justice, harvests counted, granaries watched, roads cut through forest—so completely that I had let the winds from Atlantis blow past unheard. Yet even in that long labor there had been signs: the mariner folk who crossed the seas spoke with bridled mouths, and only such news as the Empress willed was allowed to seep beyond her borders, like broth strained thin so none can taste what meat went into it. I had been too busy to pry at a veil set there on purpose; and I have always held it foul practice to put men to torment because they have been ordered into silence. But the iron discipline of our Priestly Clan knows little of private scruple.

As is customary, my office was taken from me without warning, as a trencher is whisked from before a guest when the feast is declared ended. From that instant all papers and authority belonged to my successor. By courtesy I might remain as a guest in the pyramid that had been mine, to see another sunrise; but I was plainly enjoined to quit the territory at the topmost of my speed and hasten to report in Atlantis.

Tatho—whatever else he had become—was eager to further my interests as far as his hands could reach. Before dawn he was again beside me, already dressed, already alert, his mind clinking like a purse of coin.

“I put all my resources at your disposal,” he said.

I had little enough to ask. “A ship to take me home,” I told him, “and I shall be your debtor.”

The request surprised him. “That you may certainly have if you wish it. But my ships are foul with the long passage and need a careen. If you take them, you will make a slow voyage to Atlantis. Why do you not take your own navy? The ships are in harbour now—I saw them there when we came in. Brave ships they are too.”

“But not mine,” I said. “That navy belongs to Yucatan.”

“Well, Deucalion,” he returned, “you are Yucatan—or rather, you were yesterday, and have been these twenty years.”

I saw his meaning, and it soured in me. I answered stiffly that the ships were owned by private merchants or belonged to the State, and I could not claim so much as a ten-slave galley.

Tatho shrugged. “I suppose you know your own policies best. Yet to me it seems risky for a man who has attained a position like yours and mine not to have provided himself with a stout navy of his own. One never knows when a recall may be sent; and through lack of precautions a life’s earnings may be lost in a dozen hours.”

“I have no fear for mine,” I said.

“Of course not, because you know me to be your friend. But had another man been appointed to this vice-royalty, you might have been sadly shorn. It is not many fellows who can resist a snug hoard ready and waiting in the very coffers they have come to line.”

“My Lord Tatho,” I said, “it is clear to me that you and I have grown to be of different tastes. All the hoard I have made for myself in this colony few men would covet. I have the poor clothes you see me in this moment, and a box of drugs such as I have found useful to the stomach. I possess also three slaves—two scribes, and the third a sturdy savage from Europe—who cooks my victual and fills for me the bath. For my maintenance during my years of service here, I have bled the State of a soldier’s ration and nothing beyond; and if in my name any man has mulcted a creature in Yucatan of so much as an ounce of bronze, I request you as a last service to have that man hanged for me as a liar and a thief.”

He looked at me as though I were a curious dish set down before him—plain, perhaps, but prepared with an unfamiliar spice. “I do not know whether I admire you most or pity. I do not know whether to be astonished or to despise. We had heard of your uprightness over yonder in Atlantis, of your sternness and your justice, but I swear by the old Gods no soul guessed you carried your fancy so far as this. Why, man—money is power. With money and the resources money can buy, nothing could stop a fellow like you; whilst without it you may be tripped up and trodden down irrevocably at the first puny reverse.”

“The Gods will choose my fate.”

“Possibly. But for mine, I prefer to nourish it myself.” His voice warmed as he spoke, as though he savoured the fatness of his own intention. “I tell you with frankness I have not come here to follow the pattern you have made for a vice-royalty. I shall govern Yucatan wisely and well to the best of my ability; but I shall govern it also for the good of Tatho, the viceroy. I have brought with me my navy of eight ships and a personal bodyguard. There is my wife also, and her women and her slaves. All these must be provided for. And why indeed should it be otherwise? If a people is to be governed, it should be their privilege to pay handsomely for their prince.”

“We shall not agree,” I told him. “You have the power now, and can employ it as you choose. If I thought it would be of any use, I should like to supplicate you most humbly to deal with lenience when you come to tax these people who are under you. They have grown very dear to me.”

“I have disgusted you with me, and I am grieved for it. But even to retain your good opinion, Deucalion—which I value more than that of any man living—I cannot do here as you have done. It would be impossible, even if I wished it. You must not judge all other men by your own strong standard: a Tatho is by no means a colossus like a Deucalion. And besides, I have a wife and children, and they must be provided for, even if I neglect myself.”

“Ah,” I said, “there it does seem that I possess the advantage. I have no wife, to clog me.”

He caught at the word as though it were a sweetmeat. “It seems to me you have nothing that makes life worth living. You have neither wife, children, riches, cooks, retinue, dresses, nor anything else in proportion to your station. You will pardon my saying it, old comrade, but you are plaguey ignorant about some matters. For example—you do not know how to dine.”

At that, the whole man of him became chef and critic, and the memory of sea fare rose in his speech like rancid oil. “During every day of a very weary voyage, I promised myself, sitting before meagre sea victual, that presently the abstinence would be repaid by Deucalion’s welcoming feast. Oh, I tell you that feast was one of the vividest things that ever came before my eyes. And then when we get to the actuality—what was it? Why, a country farmer every day sits down to more delicate fare. You told me how it was prepared. Well, your savage from Europe may be lusty, and perchance faithful, but he is a devil-possessed cook. Gods! I have lived better on a campaign.

“I know this is a colony here, without home refinements; but if in the days to come the deer of the forest, the fish of the stream, and the other resources of the place are not put to better use than heretofore, I shall see it my duty as ruler to fry some of the kitchen staff alive in grease so as to encourage better cookery. Gods! Deucalion, have you forgotten what it is to have a palate? And have you no esteem for your own dignity? Man, look at your clothes. You are garbed like a herdsman, and you have not a gaud or a jewel to brighten you.”

I could not help, even in my chill, the fleeting thought that a man may rule whole provinces with a mind trained to mysteries, and yet be judged, finally, by the scent that rises from his own pot. The domestic is the oldest temple; the tongue keeps its own gods.

“I eat,” I said, “when my hunger bids me, and I carry this one robe upon my person till it is worn out and needs replacement. The grossness of excessive banqueting and the effeminacy of many clothes are attainments that never met my fancy. But I think we have talked here over long, and there seems little chance of our finding agreement. You have changed, Tatho, with the years, and perhaps I have changed also. These alterations creep imperceptibly into one’s being as time advances. Let us part now, and forgetting these present differences, remember only our friendship of twenty years agone. That for me, at any rate, has always had a pleasant savour when called up into the memory.”

He bowed his head. “So be it,” he said. “And I would still charge myself upon your bounty for that ship. Dawn cannot be far off now, and it is not decent that the man who has ruled here so long should walk in daylight through the streets on the morning after his dismissal.”

“So be it,” I said.

“You shall have my poor navy,” he answered. “I could have wished you had asked me something greater.”

“Not the navy, Tatho. One small ship. Believe me, more is wasted.”

“Now there,” he said, and for an instant the old comrade vanished behind the new viceroy, “I shall act the tyrant. I am viceroy here now, and will have my way in this. You may go naked of all possessions—that I cannot help. But depart for Atlantis unattended, that you shall not.”

And so, since the choice was set beyond me, it was in the Bear—Tatho’s own private ship—with all the rest of his navy sailing in escort, that I did finally make my transit.

Yet we did not start at once. The vessels lay moored against the stone quays of the inner harbour, gutted of their stores, crews dulled and hollow-eyed after the long passage, and it would have been suicide to drive them straight back into the sea. So the courtesies were fulfilled by the craft whereon I abode hauling out into the entrance basin and anchoring there in the swells of the fairway; and forthwith she and her consorts took in wood and water, cured meat and fish ashore, and refitted in all needful ways with all attainable speed.

For myself there came, for the first time in twenty busy years, a breathing space from work. I had no further connection with the country of my labours; officially, indeed, I had left it already. Into the ship’s working it was contrary to rule that I should make inspection or show interest, since all sea matters were the exclusive property of the Mariners’ Guild, secured to them by royal patent and most jealously guarded. So there remained to me hours to gaze upon the quays, the harbours, the palaces, and the pyramids of that splendid city I had watched grow stone by stone from foundations I could still see in my mind like the first kneading of dough. Or I would roam my eye over the pastures and grain lands beyond the walls, and look longingly at the dense forests behind, from which field by field we had so tediously ripped our territory.

Would Tatho continue the work so healthily begun? I trusted so, even in spite of his selfish words; for men are seldom one thing only, and even the greedy must eat, and so must keep the fields from failing. At all hours—during the radiance of our Lord the Sun or under the stars of night—I was free to pursue that study of the higher mysteries on which we of the Priests’ Clan are trained to set our minds, without aid of book or instrument, image or temple. The harbour, too, had its own music: the slap of water against stone, the rasp of rope through a sailor’s hand, the hollow thunk of casks rolled, and over all the constant, living hiss of wind. I found myself listening as I had not listened in years, and thinking how much of a man’s well-being is governed by what his ear is forced to endure. Here, at least, there was no ceaseless metallic din, no grinding engine-roar to drown thought into submission—only the honest labour-sounds of wood and water, and the human voice still able to carry meaning.

The refitting of the navy was gone about with speed. Never, it is said, had ships been reprovisioned and caulked and remanned so swiftly for an over-ocean voyage. Indeed, barely over a month from the day they brought up in the harbour, they put out beyond the walls and began their eastward passage over the hills and dale of the ocean.

Rowing-slaves from Europe for this long sea passage are not taken now, owing to the difficulty of provisioning them, for modern humanity forbids the practice of letting them eat one another according to the home custom of their continent. Sails alone are but an indifferent stand-by. But modern science has shown how to extract force from the Sun when He is free from cloud, and this—kept secret by mariners—draws sea-water at the forepart of the vessel and ejects it with such force at the stern that she is appreciably driven forward even with the wind adverse.

In another matter also has navigation vastly improved. It is not necessary now, as formerly, to trust wholly to a starry night (when beyond sight of land) to find direction. A little image has been made and is stood balanced in the forepart of every vessel, with an arm outstretched, pointing constantly toward where the Southern Cross lies in the Heavens. So, by setting an angle, can a just course be correctly steered. Other instruments have they also for finding a true position on the ocean wastes, for the newer mariner, when he is at sea, puts little trust in the Gods and confides mightily in his own thews and wits.

Still, it is amusing to see these tarry fellows, even in this modern day, take their last farewell of the harbour town. The ship is stowed, and all ready for sea, and they wash and put on all their bravery of attire. Ashore they go, faces long with piety, and seek some obscure temple whose God has little flavour with shore folk; and there they make sacrifice with clamour and lavish outlay. Finally there follows a feast in honour of the God, and they arrive back on board and put to sea for the most part drunken, heavy and evil-humoured with gluttony and their other excesses.

The smell of their going lingered on the water: sour beer-breath, fat smoke, and the rank sweetness of spilled offerings. It put me in mind, not without contempt, how art and worship alike are so easily made into show—how quickly a living rite is turned into a purchased spectacle, loud enough to satisfy the ear, empty enough to leave the heart unfed.

The voyage itself was very different from my previous sea-going. There was no creeping timorously along in touch with coasts. We stood straight across the open gulf in the direction of home, came up with the band of the Carib Islands, and worked confidently through them as though they had been signposts to mark a sea highway; and stopped only twice to replenish with wood, water, and fruit. These commodities the savages brought us freely, so great was their subjection; and in neither place did we have even the semblance of a fight. It was a great certificate of the growing power of Atlantis and her finest over-sea colony.

Then boldly on we went across the vast ocean beyond, with never a sacrifice to implore the Gods that They should help our direction. One might feel censure toward these rugged mariners for impiety; but one could not help admiration for their lusty skill and confidence. The dangers of the desolate sea are dealt out as the Gods will, and man can only take them as they come.

Storms we encountered, and the mariners fought them with stubborn endurance; twice a blazing stone from Heaven hissed into the sea beside us, though without injuring any of our ships; and, as was unavoidable, the great beasts of the sea hunted us with their accustomed savagery.

But only once did we suffer material loss from these last, and that was when three of the greater sea lizards attacked the Bear, the ship whereon I travelled, at one and the same time.

The hour of their onset was during the blazing midday heat, with the Sun at the full of His power; our machines were drinking His strength greedily, and the vessel drove forward faster than a man on dry land could walk. Yet for all that thrust of stolen sunlight, she might as well have been standing still when the beasts sighted her.

There were three of them. We saw them come up over the curve of the horizon, beating the sea into foam with their flappers and waving their great necks like masts as they swam. Our navy was spread out in a long line of ships, and in olden days each beast would have chosen a separate prey and gone for it. But like men, these beasts have learned the necessities of warfare: they hunt in pack now, and do not separate their forces. It was plain they were making for our ship.

Tob, the captain, would have had me go into the after-castle and be secure from marauding. He was responsible to Lord Tatho, he said, for my safe conduct; it was certain the beasts would contrive to seize some of the ship’s company before they were satiated; and if the hap came to Lord Deucalion, then he—Tob—would have to give himself voluntarily to the beasts to escape a very painful death at Tatho’s hands later on.

However, my mind was set. A man can never have too much experience in fighting enemies, whether human or bestial, and the attack of these creatures was new to me, and I was fain to learn its method. So I gave the captain a letter to Tatho saying how the matter lay (for which the rude fellow seemed little enough grateful), and stayed in my chair under the awning.

The beasts surged up with champing jaws, and all the shipmen stood armed on their defence. They came alongside—two females (the smaller) on one flank, the giant male by himself on the other. Their great heads swooped about as high as the yards that held the sails, and the reek from them gave one physical sickness: a hot stench like rotten fish left in sun, mixed with a sour, swampy breath that seemed to coat the back of the throat. It was as though the sea itself had opened a pantry of corruption and flung it in our faces.

The shipmen faced the monsters with sturdy courage. Arrows were useless against those smooth, bull-like hides. Even throwing fire could not so much as singe them. Nothing but twenty axe blows delivered together on an attacking head could beat it back, and even these succeeded only through sheer weight of metal and did not make so much as the scratch of a wound.

During all time beasts have disputed with man the mastery of the earth, and it is only in Atlantis and Egypt and Yucatan that man has dared to hold his own, and fight them with a mind made strong by many previous victories. In Europe and mid-Africa the greater beasts hold full dominion, and man admits his puny number and force, and lives in earth crannies and the higher tree-tops as a confessed fugitive. Upon the great oceans the beasts are lords, unchecked.

Still, out upon that desolate sea, though the giant lizards were new to me, it was a pleasure to pit knowledge of war against brute strength and courage. Ever since the first men did their business upon great waters, they have fought these beasts with desperation. Hiding coward-like in a hold is useless; for if the enemy cannot find men above decks to glut them, they will break a ship with their paddles, and so all will be slain. It is recognised, therefore, that the fight must go forward as desperately as may be, and can only end when the beasts have got their prey and have gone away satisfied.

It was in a one-sided conflict after this fashion, then, that I found myself, and felt the joy once more to have my thews in action. But after my axe had got in a dozen lusty blows—which, for all the harm they did, might have been delivered against a city wall, or indeed against the Ark of the Mysteries itself—I sought about till I found a lance, and with that made very different play.

The decks were slippery with the horrid slime of them. The crew surged in battling; and moreover, by reason of Tob’s threats, they constantly offered themselves as a rampart before me. But I gave a few shrewd progues with the lance to show I did not choose my will to be overridden, and presently I was given room to manoeuvre.

The eyes of these lizards are small and set deep in bony sockets, but I judged them vulnerable. It was upon the eyes that I made my attack.

Deliberately I placed myself in the sight of one of the beasts and offered my body to its onset. The challenge was accepted. It swooped like a dropping stone, and I swerved and drove the lance into its oozy eye. I thanked the Gods then that I had been trained with the lance till certain aim was instinct. The blade went true and stuck there; the shaft broke in my hand.

The beast drew off blinded and bellowing, beating the sea into a cataract of foam. I saw it bend its great long neck and rub its head—spear still fixed—against its back, thereby enduring new agonies without dislodging the weapon. Then, finding it of no avail, it set off for the place from which it came with extraordinary quickness, and rapidly grew smaller against the horizon.

The male and the other female had also left us, but not in similar plight. Tob, seeing my resolve to take hazards, deliberately thrust a shipman into the jaws of each of the others so they might be sated and gone.

It was clear he dreaded for his own skin if I came by harm. And I thought, with a warming heart, of the threats Tatho must have used in his kind anxiety for my safety. It is pleasant when one’s old friends do not omit to pay these little attentions.

Land came to us first as a change in the wind’s breath—no longer only the hard salt and the rank bilge-steam of long voyaging, but a sweetness like crushed grass underfoot and the faint pepper of sun-warmed leaves. It was not the sight of Atlantis’ coast that saved men so much as that smell, and the way it reached into the hollows of their bodies and reminded them, stubbornly, of gardens and cooking-fires and water drawn cold from a well.

We had made landfall with Tob’s new instruments—clever little eyes of metal and glass that pretended to know the sea’s mind—but even so there remained ten days’ more of dogging our own wake, hunting the arm of water that led inland to the great city itself. In those ten days the deck became a sickroom and a graveyard by turns. Men who had endured storm and battle fell to scurvy’s slow theft: gums that bled at the mere thought of hard tack, joints that loosened like rotten lashings, skin that took bruises as easily as flour takes water. The sea fed us on salt and necessity, and it showed in us. Yet at the first true scent of earth, even the worst of them lifted their heads like hounds catching a remembered trail. They moved again—not well, but with a spark. They could coil a rope, haul a sheet, and, when the moment came, hold a weapon.

For my own part, since the day I had been stripped of rule in Yucatan, I had kept a quiet question behind my ribs about what waited for me in Atlantis. Tatho had assured me; others had sworn; still, the doubt lived there, a small hard seed. But I had let it lie. The Gods governed the harvest. If Phorenice meant to take my head the instant my foot touched her quay, it would be because my Lord the Sun, who rode above her, allowed it. So I had made my mind a clean table and busied myself with higher mysteries, letting the future arrive in its own time.

It arrived two days short of the capital.

Two vast headlands—black shoulders of rock thrusting out to narrow the sea’s mouth—marked the entrance to the inland waters. Between them, where the current thickened and the sound of the ocean changed pitch, we met our welcome.

A navy waited in a sheltered bay as though it had been planted there like a trap. Scouts stood on the high ground; their cries carried down on the wind, sharp as gull-screams. When our five remaining ships rounded the headland, the strangers were already standing out to bar the passage.

Five ships were all we had now. The others had been swallowed by storm or lagged behind with crews too dead to pull a rope. Our hulls wore the voyage: storm-torn rigging, weather-gnawed planks, bottoms shagged with trailing weed. Opposite us came three ships and three many-oared galleys, clean as if they had only just left a dockyard—black and bright and hungry-looking, like fresh-cured iron.

We showed our colours without shame—the signs of Tatho and Deucalion, plain for any eye. The other navy came on bare-masted, no banner, no antient, as though their very purpose made them blush. That, in itself, was a language: not the speech of the mouth, but the speech of power hiding its face.

At sea, law thins out like smoke. Men who would share a pot ashore will cut one another’s throats out of earshot of the beach, especially when one ship smells of homecoming and whatever profit was gathered abroad. Tob, who did everything by system even when the world burned, set our vessel—the Bear—into fighting trim. The other captains drew in close and formed on us, compact as a clenched fist. None of them wished to taste slavery now, with the land’s breath so near.

The Sun shone hard and brilliant, driving the machines as though He himself leaned into the work. The two navies ran toward one another, the galleys holding back to keep their line. Then, at a bare hundred ship-lengths, the enemy halted. One galley slipped ahead and raised green branches from her mast: a parley.

It was an odd courtesy, but we were sea-battered and not eager to waste blood for pride. Hoarse cries passed; our ships slowed. Tob had no greenery to hand—only a withered stick, dry as old bone—but he hoisted it as our sign that we would talk and respect an envoy.

The galley came on, swung, and backed until her stern rasped along our shield rail. A man clambered up and dropped onto our deck with the careless ease of one who had been eating land-food and sleeping on steady earth. He was young, well-made, dressed with a dandy’s attention to himself. His skin had that warm, fed look—oil and sun and good broth in the blood. He let his eyes travel over our torn rigging and our sick men, and his mouth bent in a sneer that meant to be a smile.

Then he saw Tob and nodded as if meeting an acquaintance in a wine-house.

“Old pot-mate,” he said, “your woman waits for you by the quay-side in Atlantis yonder, with four youngsters at her heels. I saw her not half a month ago.”

“You didn’t come out here to tell me home news,” Tob answered. His voice was calm, but it had iron behind it. “I’ve drunk enough pots with you, Dason, to know your pleasantries.”

“I wanted you to remember your home is still there. Wife and children ready to welcome you.”

“I am not a man that ever forgets it,” Tob said, grim as a stone. “Because I’ve got them always at the back of my mind, I’ve sailed this ship over the top of more than one pirate, when, if I’d been single, I might have been content to take the hap of slavery.”

Dason’s gaze flicked across the Bear’s scarred deck, the men with swollen gums and bandaged limbs, the crude repairs where planks had been stitched back to life.

“Oh, I know you’re desperate enough,” he said. “And I’ll confess if it comes to blows, we’re like to lose a few men before we get you and your cripples here, and your crazy ships comfortably sunk. Still—our navy has its orders. We’ve come to see if you’ll act sensible and give us what we want. Then you may go on to your home with a skin unslit and dry.”

“You have come to the wrong bird for plucking,” Tob said, and gave a heavy laugh that was half bark, half thunder. “We took no treasure in Yucatan. We stayed long enough to cure our sea-victual and fill with food and water, and no longer. We sail back as we sailed out—barren ships. You won’t believe me. I wouldn’t believe you if our places were changed. Search the holds if you like. You’ll find nothing but a few poor sailormen half in pieces with scurvy. No, you can steal nothing here but blows, Dason—and we’ll give you those with little asking.”

“I am glad to hear you value your cargo so slenderly,” Dason said. “For it is the cargo I must take back with me on the galley, if you mean to earn safe conduct.”

Tob’s brows knitted. “Speak plainer. I’m a common sailor. I don’t understand fancy talk.”

“It is clear,” Dason said, “that you’ve been set to bring Deucalion back as a prop for Phorenice. We find Phorenice hard enough to fight without further reinforcements. So we want Deucalion in our own custody, to deal with as we choose.”

“And if I deny you this piece of my freight?”

Dason looked around again at our splintered wood and weed-fouled hulls, at the four ships close in on us like battered brothers. “Why then, Tob, we’ll send you all to the fishes in very short time. Instead of Deucalion standing before the Gods alone, he’ll go down with a fine ragged company limping at his heels.”

“I doubt it,” Tob said. “But we shall see. As for giving you my Lord Deucalion—that’s out of the question. First, if I go to Atlantis without him, Tatho will come back one day, and in his hands I’ll die by the slowest of slow inches. Second, I’ve seen Deucalion kill a great sea-lizard, and he showed himself such a proper man that day I wouldn’t give him up against his will, even to Tatho. Third—and this is the simplest—you owe me for your share in our last wine-bout ashore, and I’ll see you with the nether Gods before I give you aught till you’ve settled that score.”

Dason’s smile sharpened. “Well, Tob, I hope you drown easy. As for that wife of yours, I’ve always had a fancy for her myself. I’ll know how to find a use for the woman.”

The air on our deck changed. Even the sick men seemed to hear the words as something physical—like a knife slid between ribs. Tob’s face went red beneath the salt crust.

“I’ll draw your neck for that, you son of a European,” he roared. “And if you do not clear off this deck I’ll draw it here. Go, you father of monkey children! Get away and let me fight you fairly, or by my honour I’ll stamp the inwards out of you and make your silly crew wear them as necklaces.”

Dason went, his boots clacking on wood as he scrambled back to his galley.

Tob turned at once, and the Bear’s machines were set going. Orders flew from his mouth like sparks from iron, and the crews—weak, bleeding, shaking—answered as though a drum beat in their bones. The Sun held steady and bright; the vessels gathered speed with astonishing readiness, as if the light itself had become a wind behind us.

Our five ships converged on one of the enemy’s three. She was not ready for such a swift, compact rush. Instead of meeting us bows on, she flurried and tried to turn away to find room. That decision killed her. All five of us struck together on her broadside, our underwater beaks tearing her planking. We backed clear as she went down, quick as a dropped stone, and the sea closed over her with barely time for screams.

But the advantage lasted no longer than a breath.

The three nimble galleys formed line. Their boatswains’ whips cracked—sharp, ugly sounds, the kind that poison a man’s sleep long after. Slaves bent to the oars, backs rising and falling in a dull rhythm, and the galleys leapt forward with a speed that mocked our storm-worn hulls. One of our ships was gored and sunk; men spilled into the water and were killed there without hope of rescue.

Then the fight locked tight.

Ships and galleys jammed together and ground on the swells as if they were living beasts, snarling and biting. Arrows hissed. Axes chopped. Swords thrust and hacked. The throwing fire flew—a sickly miracle, burning wood and flesh with the same greedy flame. Overhead, great gluttonous birds circled, crying, their wings beating the air into a ceaseless, nerve-chewing roar. Beneath, man-eating fish swarmed up, the water’s surface boiling where bodies fell, gnawing and quarrelling.

Even the sound had a weight to it: whip-cracks, iron on bone, men’s breath in panic and rage, the groan of timbers, the wet slap of waves against hulls. It was not the clean music of wind and sea that calms the mind; it was a harsh, industrial din of cruelty—like a city’s market when the merchants have forgotten what their words mean and only the coin speaks. In that uproar, mercy had no tongue.

Yet the shipmen did not quail. They were sea-bred; Death was an old shipmate. They were fellows who would brawl to the finish in taverns ashore for the sheer lust of a fray, and now the passion burned in them like fire-stone from Heaven in a dry forest. Some took death-wounds laughing, as if laughter could spit in Death’s eye.

On our side the cry was “Tob!” It was strange to hear an obscure captain’s name carry such force, but it did. It gathered men the way a familiar song gathers a scattered family back into one room. The enemy had a dozen cries, and their voices tangled. As their commanders were killed one by one and only Dason remained, their shout narrowed at last into “Dason!” hurled back at our “Tob!”

I had heard “Deucalion” shouted as a battlecry for half a life-span. I had seen lusty encounters. Still, this sea-fight surprised even me with its savage fierceness—perhaps because the element itself was unstable, because each step was taken on a swaying plank that might tilt you into teeth and water, because the air was thick with smoke that stung the eyes and made every breath taste of burnt pitch.

And in every way the fight converged upon the Bear.

It was on us the enemy spent their hottest spite. It was to us that the crews of Tatho’s navy rallied as their own vessels burned or sank or were taken. One by one, all the ships of either side were destroyed or lay with their people killed, until at last only Dason’s galley and the Bear remained.

For the moment, we were being mastered. A score of men remained to us out of all those who had sailed from Yucatan. The enemy had boarded and turned our decks into the field. But they had been too liberal with throwing fire. In the midst of our rage we became aware—by the smoke that thickened, by the sudden heat underfoot, by the way the air turned acrid—that the Bear herself was past salvation.

Tob saw it and laughed as if the fire were a joke told by an old friend.

“They may stay here and fry if they choose,” he shouted, “but for ourselves the galley is good enough now. Keep a guard on Deucalion, and come with me, shipmates!”

“Tob!” our fellows roared back in a kind of fierce joy. And I, though it was a change for me not to be the name men shouted, could not hold my voice. I sent out “Tob!” too. It was a luxury, in its way, to fight behind him—uncouth, loud, but clear as a knife in purpose.

There was no stopping the rush, though progress came slow through bodies and steel. Tob went first with his bloody axe, cutting a road. We followed like furies, the lust of battle up to the chin. Smoke and flame spat at our heels as we forced our way across the embrace of hulls.

Ten of us won to the galley.

We turned at once and stabbed madly at those who tried to follow. We hacked through the grapples that held ship to ship. The swells shoved the Bear away, and she drifted, burning, a stubborn old beast dying on its feet. The slaves chained to the oar-benches watched with dull eyes. Their interest was in neither side. They were used to whip-cracks and shouted orders as a way of weather. Only when a stray missile found flesh did they flinch, and even then it was the small flinch of a man who has long since learned that the world’s noise will not consult his pain.

A handful of the enemy’s fighting men scrambled onto the galley after us, preferring any fate to roasting on the Bear. They had to be dealt with quickly. Three, still hot with mischief, were killed wastefully, simply to clear space. Five threw their weapons into the sea; Tob had them chained to oar looms in place of slaves who were dead. And then there remained only Dason.

The fight had gone out of him. He had cast his arms away and stood sullen, ready.

Tob went up to him, face exulting, as if he had been given a fine cut of meat and meant to decide the cooking.

“Ho, pot-mate Dason,” he cried, “you made a lot of talk an hour ago about that woman of mine with her brats on the quay-side in Atlantis yonder. Now I’ll give you a pleasant choice. Either I take you along home and tell her what you said—before what’s left of the ship’s company, which is for the most part dead now, poor souls—and I’ll leave her to perform on your carcase as she sees fit by way of payment. Or I deal with you here myself.”

Dason swallowed, and for the first time his fine-fed confidence looked like a garment too big for him. Then he did a thing I did not expect. He knelt and offered his neck.

“I thank you for the chance,” he said.

Tob’s axe fell clean. The head was set on the galley’s beak—an advertisement, as Tob said, and a warning in the only language sea-raiders respect. The body was thrown overboard. One of the great hovering birds swooped down, took it as though it were a fish, and carried it off toward a nest in the crags.

And so, at last, we had a moment to eat.

The galley carried stores meant for men who had not been rotting on salt for weeks: fruit that still held sunshine in its flesh, fresh meats that smelled of blood and smoke rather than brine, water that did not taste of barrel-staves. Even the sound around us altered as the oar-slaves drove the vessel onward toward the capital—the steady, wet beat of blades, the creak of thongs, the hoarse breathing of men forced into rhythm. It was a hard sound, an ugly one, but after battle’s chaos it became almost a kind of order.

There was a wine-skin in the after-castle. I filled a horn and poured some out at Tob’s feet in salutation, the wine dark as old stories.

“My man,” I said, “you have shown me a fight.”

“Thanks,” he said, wiping smoke and blood from his brow with the back of his hand. “And I know you’re a judge. ’Twas pretty whilst it lasted. Seeing my lads were, for the most, scurvy-rotten, I’ll say they fought with credit. I have lost my Lord Tatho’s navy, but I think Phorenice will see me righted there. If those against her took such trouble to kill my Lord Deucalion before he could come to her aid, I can fancy she will not be niggard in her joy when I put Deucalion safe—if somewhat dented and blood-bespattered—on the quay.”

“The Gods know,” I said. It is not my custom to discuss policy with inferiors, even when battle has loosened manners, as it had then. “The Gods will decide what is best for you, Tob, even as they have decided it is best that I should go on to Atlantis.”

Tob held a horn, filled it, and I thought for a moment he meant to pour at my feet as I had at his. But the thirst of fighting was on him. He tipped it back and emptied it down his throat.

“It is thirsty work, this fighting,” he said. “And that drink comes very useful.”

I set my hand on his blood-smeared arm. “Tob,” I said, “whether I step into power again, or whether I go to the block to-morrow, is another matter the Gods alone know. But hear me tell you now: if a chance is given me of showing gratitude, I shall not forget the way you have served me in this voyage, and the way you have fought this day.”

Tob filled another brimming horn and splashed it at my feet, wine spattering the deck like a sealing of bargain.

“That’s good enough surety for me,” he said, “that my woman and brats never want from this day onward. The Lord Deucalion for the block, indeed!”

Until the rival ships lunged at us in the mouth of the gulf, I had worn my own importance like a loose cloak—useful for warmth, not for show. A man trained to the priesthood learns early to keep his stomach quiet and his mind quieter: the Gods turn the wheel; we are only spokes. But those enemy galleys did not fight like men guarding a frontier. They fought like men hunting a single quarry, snapping oars, burning pitch, dying gladly, if only I might be taken living. That kind of appetite in an enemy is the surest seasoning a fate can have. Even the dullest palate reads it.

Phorenice, it seemed, had scouts as keen as her enemies were hungry. The arm of sea that leads to Atlantis is a twisting thing, narrow as a river where old fire-mountains have spilled themselves into stone, broad as a lake where the shores blur into heat-haze.
