A galley must follow every bend, and every bend costs time; but a runner with strong lungs can cut across flats and reeking swamps, pick his way between smoking hills, and—if he does not get taken by a fire-stream, a sudden flood, or some wandering beast—reach the capital far ahead of any ship.

Phorenice was no miser with messengers. They said later she flung twenty couriers from the headland like seed on the wind, each with his own route and his own prayers. Seven came in clean and whole, and three more were known to have survived. At the time we knew none of it. We drove on thinking ourselves unheralded.

The slaves on the row-banks were, for the most part, European savages: thick-bodied, raw-skinned men with old fear baked into them. The stink of them hung under the deck-planks and seeped up through every seam—sour sweat, rancid fat, the sweetish taint of unwashed sores. Add to it the wind that carried fine grit from an erupting mountain—ash like flour but sharp as ground glass—and what could have been a clean, bright voyage became a choking, gritty mouthful you could not spit out. We were eager to be done with it.

And if I am to confess a weakness without dishonour, I had something else burning in me besides impatience: curiosity, vast and very human, about how Atlantis would take me. A formal prisoner? A quiet nobody, to be summoned later? Some ceremonial welcome I could rehearse in my head, like a litany? I guessed at many shapes for my reception, but the true one never came to me.

At daybreak one morning, the sacred mountain showed its peak, a permanent bruise of fire on the horizon behind the city. The boatswains’ whips cracked harder, and the men on the benches—those offensive, panting bodies—gave us a last, ragged spurt. The wind was dead against us; no sail could be trusted. But the oars bit, and with each throb the city lifted itself higher from the water: first the pyramid-tips like stone teeth, then the taller towers, then the long white line of the harbour walls.

Twenty years is enough for a man to forget the exact feel of his childhood street under bare feet, yet the sight of Atlantis brought it all back as if the stones had remembered me. I picked out the temple where I had first been admitted to manhood, the pyramid where I had been given the lesser mysteries; and then, when lesser things sharpened into their proper outlines, I saw the house where my father and mother had reared me. My eyes blurred, and for a moment the city swam like a reflection.

We lay to, as law demands, outside the harbour’s white walls. The row-slaves sagged over their oar-looms and sobbed softly, their breath making small wet noises in the heat. Most captains must wait here while the port-captain measures his dignity by another man’s impatience. But this time there was no waiting at all.

A signal rang from the two entrance castles. The chain that hung between them dropped with a metallic slither, and a ten-oared boat shot out as though it had been held in a clenched fist and released. It came alongside so fast the wake slapped our hull.

“That should be Dason’s galley?” the port-captain called.

“It was,” Tob answered. He had the helmsman’s stance—feet planted, shoulders easy, eyes awake as a hunting dog’s.

“Oh, I saw Dason’s head on your beak,” said the port-captain. “You were Tatho’s captain?”

“And am still. Tatho’s fleet was sent by Dason and his friends to the sea-floor. We took this stinking galley to finish the voyage in, seeing it was the only craft left afloat.”

The port-captain’s eyes travelled over those of us on the after-deck. His gaze had the quick, nervous darting of a man who lives too close to punishment.

“I fear, captain, you’ll have but a dangerous reception. I do not see my Lord Deucalion. Or does he come with some other navy? Gods, captain—if you have let him get killed whilst under your charge, the Empress will have the skin torn slowly off you living.”

“What with Phorenice and Tatho both so curious for his welfare,” Tob said dryly, “my Lord Deucalion seems but a dangerous passenger. But I shall save my hide this voyage.”

He jerked his thumb at me. “He’s there to put in a word for me himself.”

The port-captain stared as if he had been told that a hearth-stone spoke. Then his face rearranged itself into ceremony. He bowed until his spine looked in danger.

“I trust my lord, in his infinite strength, will pardon my sin in not knowing him by his nobleness before. But truth to tell, I had looked to see my lord more suitably apparelled.”

“Pish,” I said. “If I choose to dress simply, I cannot object to being mistaken for a simple man. It is not my pleasure to advertise my quality by gauds. If you think amends are due, I pray of your charity that this inquisition may end.”

He became a machine of bows. “I am the humblest of my lord’s servants. It will be my exceeding honour to pilot my lord’s galley into the berth appointed.”

His boat leapt ahead; our slaves heaved the oars back into stroke. Tob stood by the helm with a dry smile as the harbour mouth widened before us.

“Well,” I said, indulging his mood, “what is it?”

“I’m thinking,” Tob said, “that my Lord Deucalion will remember me only as a very rude fellow when he steps ashore amongst all this fine gentility.”

“You don’t think anything of the kind.”

“Then I must prove my refinement,” he said, “and not contradict.”

He took my hand in his huge, hard fist—callused like old leather—and pressed it once, a sailor’s pledge, plain as salt. “By the Gods, Deucalion, you may be a great prince, but I’ve only known you as a man. You’re the finest fighter of beasts and men that walks this world to-day, and I love you for it. That spear-stroke of yours on the lizard is a thing the singers in the taverns shall make chaunts about.”

We passed between the entrance castles. Soldiers on the parapets blew trumpets in welcome, and the sound struck the stone and came back doubled, a bright brassy ringing that made the air feel crowded. The port-captain had run up my banner to his masthead, as though it had been waiting folded for this very hour, and across the wide basin of the harbour I heard musicians answering—pipes and drums—while welcoming fires sent up pale smoke that smelled of resin and sweet oil. Even in sunlight the flame-glow made a trembling veil above the square.

We were taken—no choice given—straight to the royal quay and made fast to posts and rings of gold, sacred to the reigning house. The rope in my hands felt suddenly like a poor thing: hemp against sanctity.

“If Dason could only have foreseen this honour,” Tob muttered with his grisly humour, “I’m sure he’d have laid in a silken warp to make fast on the bollards instead of mere plebeian hemp. I’m sure there’d be a frown on Dason’s head this minute, if the sun hadn’t scorched it stiff. My Lord Deucalion, will you pick your way with niceness over this common ship and tread on the genteel carpet they’ve spread for you yonder?”

The port-captain heard him and went white with horror, as if Tob’s words were a knife drawn in a temple. I felt, with a small inward sigh, how quickly colonial freedom curdles into offence in the air of a court. Atlantis had always loved ceremony, but this Atlantis wore it like armour.

A pavilion of crimson cloth had been erected to shelter me until the Empress should arrive. I stepped into it because refusing would have been a kind of childishness. But my complaisance ended there.

Three chamberlains, dazzling with goldwork and stiff with practice, presented changes of raiment—robes heavy with embroidery, sashes studded, ornaments that clinked and flashed. They would have had me dressed like a sacred bird for a festival.

I refused curtly. When one of them stammered that it was the Empress’s taste, I asked him plainly if he had any definite command from her mightiness on this paltry matter.

He had none.

“Then hear me,” I said. “Phorenice commanded Deucalion, the man, to attend before her, and sent no word concerning his outer casing. This dress suits my temper. It keeps the heat and wind from my skin, and—more than can be said for many things in this city, I suspect—it is clean. It seems to me, sirs, that your interfering savours somewhat of impertinence.”

With one accord they drew their swords and pushed the hilts toward me, faces set in a fearful sincerity.

“It would be a favour,” said their spokesman, “if the great Lord Deucalion would take his vengeance now, instead of delivering us to the tormentors hereafter.”

“Poof,” I said. “The matter is forgotten. You make too much of a little.”

But their gesture showed me a new texture to the air of Atlantis: fear. Not the fear of battle, which is honest and smells of sweat and iron, but the fear of instruments and rooms where screams are counted like coin. A city where men offer their throats over the cut of a coat is a city changed.

There was a bath in the pavilion. I took it gladly, though someone had laced the water with scent—some syrupy flower-sweetness that clung to the nostrils and made the clean water feel half-spoiled, as if a cook had scattered perfume over good broth. Still, it washed away salt and ash-grit. I sat afterward and waited, outwardly composed.

The chamberlains stood like statues. I offered no small talk; they did not break my calm. Outside, the music blared—pipes shrilling, drums thudding—while the welcoming fires sent their odours in waves: resin, oil, something like burned cedar. Over all, the sun hammered on the scarlet roof so that the cloth glowed hot, and the air inside tasted of warmed dye.

Some say the great should gather dignity by making lesser folk wait. I have always thought that trick paltry. Phorenice seemed to share my distaste. As she later told me, the moment Tob’s galley was reported moored to the royal quay, she left the palace. The procession was already marshalled—bedecked, aligned, rehearsed—waiting only for its chief ornament. When she mounted, the trumpets ordered the advance.

From the pavilion doorway I watched the head of the concourse emerge from the broad street. First came soldiery in ordered lines, crossing the square to salute, then ranging to the far side to make space. Then the Mariners’ Guild—men who smelled of tar and rope even at a distance—then more soldiers. After them the merchants, the tanners, the spear-makers, and all the acknowledged Guilds, wearing deliberate finery as though each trade had dressed itself for its own legend. Some walked; some rode beasts trained to carry them with dull patience.

And then the spectacle split open and showed its two wonders.

From the shadow between the houses swung a beast so vast it made the square seem suddenly smaller: a bull mammoth, huge even among its kind. I had hunted mammoths when they raided villages and trampled cornlands—had seen them shaggy and monstrous in wild ground, hotter in rage than cave-tiger or cave-bear, the most dangerous of beasts that dispute dominion with man save only a few greater lizards. I had killed them, yes, but always at risk and always with men around me.

This one walked like a disciplined slave. On its back sat a half-castle of gold stamped with the outstretched hand and decked with silver snakes. Its tusks were gilded; flowers were tangled into its hairy neck; and it moved in procession as if pageantry were its natural pasture. Savage power held in a leash—there was no clearer emblem of a ruler’s will.

And at the same moment, as if the mammoth were merely her platform, came the greater wonder: Phorenice.

At first the beast held my eye by sheer uncouth hugeness. But the woman within the golden half-castle drew my gaze away and kept it. When the crowd shouted at her approach, I rose and waited at the pavilion’s porch until the mammoth halted in the centre of the square. Then I walked out across the bare pavement toward her.

“On your knees, my lord,” whispered one chamberlain behind me, terror in his breath.

“At least with bent head,” urged another.

I had my own measure for what a man owes to his own self-respect. I went with head erect, giving her gaze for gaze.

She was summing me. I was doing the same. Those few seconds showed me a kind of woman I had not imagined living. I had always said women had no influence over me; that is true in the crude way men mean it. But I had watched their power over others as carefully as I watched a river’s current, for it shapes policy all the same. Still, this woman baffled me. I could read not one syllable of her thoughts.

Her body was slight, supple, beautifully made; her figure rather small. Her face was a perfect book of cleverness—and yet she was fair beyond belief. Her hair was a lovely ruddiness, cut short in the new fashion and bunching on her shoulders. And her eyes—no tint can hold them. They were like deep water that reflects sky one moment and shows black depth the next.

She nodded slightly as I drew near. When I did the obeisance permitted to my rank, she spoke in a voice clear enough that all near could drink the words.

“Never put forehead on the ground again on my behalf so long as I rule in Atlantis. For others it is fitting—once, twice, or several times, according to rank and station—for I am Empress and they are beneath me. But you are Deucalion, my lord. Till to-day I knew you only from pictures drawn with tongues. I have seen you now and judged for myself. And so I decree: Deucalion is above all other men in Atlantis. If there is one who does not render him obedience, that man is enemy also of Phorenice and shall feel her anger.”

A sign from her, and a stair was brought. She called to me. I mounted and sat beside her in the golden half-castle under the canopy of royal snakes. Behind us a girl attendant fanned with perfumed feathers. The scent was heavy—sweet, almost cloying—like honey left too long in the sun. At Phorenice’s word the mammoth turned, bearing us back toward the royal pyramid by the street we had come. Soldiers and guilds fell into order before and behind. I noted, with a soldier’s eye, heavily armed troops marching close on either flank of the mammoth, a living wall.

Phorenice turned to me with a smile that was sharp at the edges.

“You piqued me at first,” she said.

“Your Majesty overwhelms me with so much notice.”

“You looked at my steed before you looked at me. A woman finds it hard to forgive a slight like that.”

“I envied you the greatest of your conquests,” I said, “and do still. I have fought mammoths and killed at times, but I never dared even to think of taking one alive and bringing it into tameness.”

“You speak boldly,” she said, still smiling, “and yet you can turn a pretty compliment. Faugh, Deucalion—the way these people fawn on me gives me a nausea. I am not of the same clay as they are, I know; but because I am the daughter of Gods they must feed me on the pap of insincerity.”

So Tatho had been right. The swineherd fiction held; it had been swallowed whole and digested into doctrine. If she wished to live inside the story she had made, it was not my place to tear the cloth. Rightly or wrongly, I was her servant.

“I have been pining long enough for stronger meat than they can give,” she went on. “At last I have sent for you. I have been at some pains to procure my tongue-pictures of you, Deucalion, and though you do not know me yet, I may say I knew you with thoroughness even before we met. I can admire a man with a mind great enough to forego silly gauds of clothes, or excesses of feasts, or the pamperings of women.”

She glanced down at her own silks and glittering jewels, as if amused by her own contradiction.

“We women like to carry colours upon our persons, but that is a different matter. And so I sent for you here to be my minister and bear with me the burden of ruling.”

“There should be better men in broad Atlantis.”

“There are not,” she said, and the certainty in her tone made it sound like an inventory already taken. “I know them all by heart. They are enamoured of my poor person; they weary me with empty phrases and importunities. Though they cry service, their own advancement and their own treasuries come first. So I have sent for you, Deucalion—the one strong man in all the world. You at least will not sigh to be my lover?”

She watched me from the corner of her eyes, measuring, weighing.

“The Empress is my mistress,” I said. “I will be an honest minister to her. With Phorenice the woman, it is likely I shall have little enough to do. Besides, I am not the sort that sports with this toy they call love.”

“And yet you are a personable man enough,” she said thoughtfully. “That still further proves your strength, Deucalion. You at least will not lose your head through weak infatuation for my poor looks and graces.”

She turned her head slightly. “Ylga, fan not so violently.”

Our talk broke then, and I let my eyes roam as we moved through the chief street of the fairest city in the world. I had been gone a score of years and expected to see change as one expects a grown child’s face to have lengthened. In public buildings, Atlantis had indeed grown: new temples, new pyramids, new palaces, statues everywhere, stone and bronze and gold catching sun so that the street seemed sprinkled with hard light.

Yet the sharper shock was not the splendour but what crouched beside it.

The throngs lining the street showed gaunt bodies and hungry faces. Here and there stood men and women naked as European savages, but without even the savage’s fierce pride—only a dulled shame, or no shame at all, as if hunger had eaten that too. Even the traders, wearing trumpery gauds to ape the fashion of display, had a hunted look: eyes too quick, mouths tight, as though the word “safety” had slipped out of their language and left no synonym behind. The city’s music rolled over them—pipes and drums and trumpets—but beneath it I caught other sounds: a thin coughing, the moan of a child, the restless scuff of too many bare feet on hot stone. A city’s health lives in its noises as much as in its walls; this one had a fever-hum under the ceremonial clangour.

Phorenice followed the direction of my gaze.

“The season has been unhealthy of recent months,” she said. “These lower people will not build fine houses to adorn my city, and because they choose to live on in squalid, unsightly kennels, there have been calentures and other sicknesses amongst them, which make them disinclined for work. And then, too, for the moment, earning is not easy. Trade is nearly stopped this last half-year, since the rebels have been hammering so lustily at my city gates.”

I was startled clean out of decorum.

“Rebels!” I cried. “Who are hammering at the gates of Atlantis? Is the city in a state of siege?”

“Of their condescension,” Phorenice said lightly, “they are giving us holiday to-day, and so my welcome to you comes undisturbed. If they were fighting, your ears would have told you. They are noisy enough in all their efforts.”

As she said it, the street’s festival-sounds struck me newly: trumpets, drums, cheering—so much chosen noise laid over the quieter, truer sounds of want. It was like putting a bright sauce over meat that had begun to turn.

“My spies say they are making ready new engines for use against the walls,” she went on, “which you may sally out to-morrow and break if it gives you amusement. But for to-day, Deucalion, I have you, and you have me, and there is peace round us, and some prettiness of display. If you ask for more I will give it you.”

“I did not know of this rebellion,” I said, forcing my voice back into its proper harness. “But as Your Majesty has made me your minister, it is well I should know at once its scope. This is a matter we should be serious upon.”

“And do you think I cannot take it seriously also?” she snapped, and for the first time a crack showed in the smooth glaze of her amusement.

“Ylga,” she said to the girl behind us, “set loose my dress at the shoulder.”

The attendant’s hands went to the jewelled clasp. She obeyed, but with ill grace that showed in the stiffness of her fingers, in the way she did not quite hide her reluctance. Phorenice stripped down the fabric herself, baring the pure skin beneath and showing, just below the curve of the left breast, a bandage of bloodstained linen.

“There is a guarantee of my seriousness yesterday, at any rate,” she said, looking at me sidelong. “The arrow struck on a rib and that saved me. If it had struck between, Deucalion would have been standing beside my funeral pyre to-day instead of riding on this pretty steed of mine which he admires so much.”

Her voice took on mockery again, but the blood on the linen was not mockery, and the smell of it—faint iron under perfume—cut through the sweet oils like truth.

“Your eye seems to feast itself most on the mammoth, Deucalion. Ah, poor me. I am not one of your shaggy creatures, and so it seems I shall never be able to catch your regard.”

She turned her head back. “Ylga, you may link my dress up again with its clasp. My Lord Deucalion has seen wounds before, and there is nothing else here to interest him.”

For the present, it seemed, my lodging was to be within the royal pyramid itself.

The glittering procession drew up in the paved square before that enormous pile of stone. Groups broke and re-formed like schools of bright fish; gold flashed; the skin of the mammoth steamed under its harness; the air rang with trumpets. A stair was brought; the canopy of snakes above our golden half-castle shivered in the light; and we three who had ridden beneath it descended into the heat of the open day.

I was passing from sky into stone, from sun into the cool entrails of the palace. Without thinking—because habit and reverence had been kneaded into me since childhood like salt into dough—I turned toward the Sacred Mountain, where the towering rocks frowned above the city, and I made the customary obeisance. I touched my forehead to the ground, and in silence I offered the prescribed prayer of thanks for the good air our Lord the Sun permitted us to breathe before we entered a dwelling.

When I rose, I could have sworn I heard a titter—thin, tinkling laughter somewhere among the fanciful crowd.

I glared toward the scoffers, ready to demand their punishment. Then I turned to Phorenice, expecting to see her rising too from the same reverence.

She stood upright. She had never bent.

She was watching me with a look I could not name—amusement, perhaps; perhaps something sharper under it, as when a cook tastes a sauce and decides whether to sweeten or scorch.

Whatever she thought, she had no mind to shout it in the square. “Come,” she said. She faced the doorway and gave the secret word appointed for the day.

The ponderous stone blocks that barred the porch swung back on their hinges. Phorenice passed from the glare into the pyramid’s cool gloom, her fan-girl following decorously. With a heaviness beginning at my heart like over-fermented bread, I followed. The stone doors shut behind us with a sullen thud, and the outdoor sound—hoof, trumpet, chatter—was clipped off as cleanly as a thread.

We did not go far. Phorenice halted in the hall of waiting.

I knew the place well: red walls painted with kings; the bronze jet in the center that breathed burning earth-breath and gave light. Twenty years earlier the old King—now gone—had brought me thus far when he bade me farewell for my vice-royalty in Yucatan. Then the air had been pure, almost sweet, as though the stone itself had been washed and aired in sun. Now it lay heavy with perfume, thick and languid, an oppressive sweetness that made my lungs work as if I were breathing through cloth steeped in syrup.

“My minister,” said the Empress, “I acquit you of intentional insult; but I think the colonial air has made you very simple. Such an obeisance as you made to that mountain has not been made since I was sent to reign over this kingdom.”

“Your Majesty,” I said, “I am of the Priests’ Clan. I was reared in their tenets. Before entering a house I have been taught to thank the Gods—and more especially our Lord the Sun—for the good air He and They provide. More than once I have been chased by streams of fire and stinking vapours among the mountains when they boil suddenly, and so I can say the prescribed prayer straight from my heart.”

“Circumstances have changed since you left Atlantis,” Phorenice said, and her voice carried easily in that high chamber. “When thanks are given now, they are not thrown at those old Gods.”

I understood. The meaning struck like sourness in milk: unmistakable, immediate.

If this was now the rule, I would have no hand in it. I could serve a reigning monarch faithfully. But to make sacrilege of my faith, to accept a swineherd’s daughter as a God to receive prayers and obeisance, revolted every part of me that had ever knelt in discipline before the Sun.

So I chose the crisis.

“Phorenice,” I said, “I have been a priest from childhood, revering the Gods and growing intimate with their mysteries. Until I find for myself those old things are false, I must stand by that allegiance. If there is a cost for faithfulness, I must pay it.”

She looked at me with a slow smile. “You are a strong man, Deucalion.”

I bowed.

“I have heard others as stubborn,” she said. “They were converted.” She shook out the ruddy bunches of her hair and shifted so the glow of the earth-breath struck full upon her face and form. In that light she was almost unearthly—beauty sharpened into a weapon. “I have found it as easy to convert the stubborn as to burn them. Indeed, there has been little talk of burning. They have all rushed to conversion, whether I would or no. But it seems my poor looks and tongue are wanting in charm to-day.”

“Phorenice is Empress,” I said stolidly, “and I am her servant. To-morrow, if she gives me leave, I will clear away this rabble clamouring outside the walls. I must begin to prove my uses.”

“I am told you are a pretty fighter,” she said. “Well, I hold some small skill in arms myself, and have a conceit that I am something of a judge. To-morrow we will take a taste of battle together. But to-day I must carry through the honourable reception I have planned for you. The feast will be set soon, and you will wish to make ready for it. Chambers have been chosen for your use, and stored with what is needful. Ylga will show you.”

We waited until Phorenice had passed out of the circle of the light-jet and vanished through a doorway in the far shadow. Then the girl took up a lamp and led me into the narrow mazes of the pyramid.

Everywhere perfume lay in the air—oily sweetness, resin, crushed blossoms, something musky beneath as if the stone sweated it. The passages turned and twisted and doubled in cunning fashion through the solid mass, as though the builders had planned not merely to shelter kings but to confuse murder. A stranger could have wandered hours—days—before finding the chamber he desired. There was craft in those forgotten hands. And men say the king of that time, to make doubly sure, killed all who had built it, or had even learned the lay of its inner stones.

But the fan-girl went as one bred to it. She doubled here, turned there, stopped at a blank wall and pressed a stone that swung inward. Once she set her foot to the corner of a flag, and the slab lifted, showing a stair steep and narrow. We descended, crossed the foot of an inclined way that led upward again, and by degrees reached the chamber assigned to me.

“There is raiment in those chests,” she said, “and jewels and gauds in that bronze coffer. They are Phorenice’s first presents—she bade me say—and only a small earnest of what is to come. My Lord Deucalion can drop his simplicity now and fig himself in finery to suit the fashion.”

“Girl,” I said sharply, “be more decorous, and spare me such advice.”

“If my lord calls it rudeness, he can give a word to Phorenice and I shall be whipped,” she retorted, eyes sullen under black brows. “If he asks it, I can be stripped and scourged before him. The Empress will do much for Deucalion just now.”

“You are nearer to that whipping than you think.”

“I have a name,” she snapped. “They call me Ylga. You might have heard it as we rode on the mammoth if you had not been so wrapped up in Phorenice.”

I studied her. “You have never seen me before,” I said, “yet you speak as though you meant to provoke trouble. There is some object in this.”

Instead of answering at once, she pushed the massive stone of the doorway shut. Then she took my garment with her jewelled fingers and drew me away from the airshaft into the farther corner, as though even the moving air might carry words to some hidden ear.

“I am the daughter of Zaemon,” she said, “whom you knew.”

Zaemon. The name fell into me with the weight of an old loaf set on a table—solid, remembered, homely in a way this perfumed stone was not. A man of the Mountain. A friend from before my exile into the colonies.

“You bring me a message from him?”

“How could I? He lives in the priests’ dwellings on the Mountain you obeised. I have not put eyes on him these two years.” Her voice tightened. “But when I saw you step out from that red pavilion they pitched by the harbour, I—I felt pity for you, Deucalion. I remembered you were my father’s friend, and I knew what Phorenice had in store. She has been plotting it these two months.”

“I cannot hear words against the Empress.”

“And yet—”

“What?”

She stamped her sandal on the stone as though to beat sense into it. “You must be blind, Deucalion—or daring. I shall not interfere further; not now. Still, I shall watch. If ever you seem to want a friend, I will try and serve you.”

“I thank you for your friendship.”

“You take it lightly.” A flush rose under her skin. “Even now you do not know my power, any more than you guess my motive. You may be first man in this kingdom, but I rank as second lady. And women stand high in Atlantis now. My friendship is a commodity sought with frequence and industry.”

“And I am grateful,” I said. “You think little of my gratitude, Ylga; but I have never bestowed it on a woman before. Treasure it for its rarity.”

She looked as though she did not know whether to laugh or strike. “My lord, there is an education before you.”

She showed me how to call slaves when I wished for their help and left me.

For a full minute I stood in that chamber, hearing my own words as though another had spoken them. Who was the daughter of Zaemon that she should draw from me a thing I had withheld as carefully as a priest guards a sacred name?

At my bidding the slaves came. They were eager to deck me in a thousand foolishnesses—robes, belts, chains, trinkets—and, in what seemed to be the modern fashion of their class, to press perfumes and unguents upon me. They spoke of scents as though they were sauces: this one “sharp,” that one “round,” this one “warming,” that one “cool,” each to be layered upon the skin in measured touches. But their pressing attentions irritated me. I was already clean and shaved; my hair was trimmed; my robe unsoiled. Their insistence felt less service than impertinence. I set them to beat one another as punishment, promising that if they did not do it thoroughly I would send them to the brander for stripes that would endure.

It is strange, but a common menial can often ruffle one more deeply than a rebellious general.

I had seen many strange sights that day and borne many new sensations, but nothing surprised me so much as the manner by which Phorenice summoned her guests to feast.

I sat waiting in my chamber when, growing out of nothing, a sound touched my ear—first a faint tremor, then increasing by infinitely small steps until it became a clanging din that hurt like a hammer on the bone. Then the whole massive fabric of the pyramid trembled and groaned and shook, as though it were a child’s wooden toy brushed by a strong man’s sandal.

The earthquake-cry.

It was the portent served out yearly by the chiefs of the Priests’ Clan on the Sacred Mountain when they bade all the world take count of sin: the sacred reminder that from roaring fire and monstrous tremors man had been born, and by those same agencies he would be swallowed up—he and the sins within him.

Here the Empress was prostituting that solemnity into a call to gluttony, a signal for ribald laughter and sensuous display.

Yet how had she authority to do it? Who was Phorenice that she should tamper with forces that dwell within the liquid heart of our mother earth? Had there been treachery—some priest who forgot his vow and babbled holy mystery into her ear? Or had she, with her agile brain, discovered a key herself?

If she had found it herself, I could serve her with silent conscience. She might not be of my making, but she was Empress, and obedience was duty. But if she had suborned a weaker member of the Clan, the matter was different. Our constitution demanded that secrets and mysteries be kept inviolate, and that we pursue with undying hatred both the betrayer and the receiver of his confidence.

With these undecided feelings working in me, I obeyed the summons. Slaves led me through the windings to the great banqueting-hall.

The scene was dazzling.

The vast chamber with its marvellous carvings blazed under jets of earth-breath bright as midday. The company filled it—gauds and colours beyond counting, like a living tapestry. They had been shaken by the sacred quake-call, and yet no chastening fell upon them. They recked nothing of death and misery stalking through the city wards without, of rebels beyond the walls, of neglected Gods and their priests on the Mountain. They were gluttonous for the passions of the moment; it was their fashion to look at nothing beyond.

When I stepped onto the pavement, trumpets blared to proclaim my arrival. There was no roar of welcome; only mincing voices and ridiculous bows, ornaments jangling and silks swishing to show themselves. When Phorenice appeared, cries rose and lawful obeisance was made—but even that seemed staged, polished, airless. Heartiness, once a virtue, had come to be counted barbarism.

Two chamberlains—posturing, smirking—took me in charge and ushered me with flimsy golden wands to the dais. I was to sit on Phorenice’s divan and eat from her dish.

“There is no stint to the honour the Empress puts upon me,” I said, kneeling and taking my seat.

She gave me that queer sidelong look. “Deucalion may have more beside, if he asks prettily. He may have what all other men have sighed for, and what none will ever get. But I have given enough of my own accord; he must ask warmly for further favours.”

“I ask first,” I said, “that I may sweep the boundaries clear of the rabble clamouring against the city walls.”

“Pah.” She frowned, as though I had spoken of stale bread at a dessert table. “Have you appetite only for sterner pleasures? They must have been rustic folk in your colony. You shall give me news now of the toothsomeness of this feast.”

Dishes and goblets were set before us. I ate, though I had little appetite for victual so broken and so highly spiced—meat minced fine and drowned in sauces that stung the tongue; sweets heavy with syrups; wines too lush, as though they had been cooked down to a perfume. The smell of it all rose and mingled with the hall’s incense until my senses were cloyed, as if I had been made to dine in a perfumer’s shop.

But if the food did not please me, it pleased them. Men and women alike lingered over each titillation of the palate as though it were a caress from the Gods. They moaned and sighed and praised the cookery with extravagance, turning eating into display. They did not take sustenance as one takes strength for labour; they took it as one takes a drug.

Phorenice watched with quick bright eyes, throwing a word here and there to the groups while she spoke with me. At times she called some favoured creature up to receive a scrap from the royal dish. The honoured one ate it with exaggerated gesture—or, twice, tucked it away into the folds of his clothes as though human lips were too common to profane it.

To me, the flattery was gross. Phorenice accepted it as due. There had to be a weakness in her somewhere, though none showed plainly. Her face was strong and subtle and wondrous comely; the Gods had favoured her in body beyond mortal fairness. The courtiers raved about her, and even I, no lover of such matters, could not deny the groundwork for their admiration.

Yet lovely as she was, my eyes strayed more willingly to Ylga, who by rank sat behind us on the divan as immediate attendant. There was an honesty in her face which Phorenice’s lacked.

The feast rolled on in new absurdities. Some manners touched closely upon disrespect. At the halfway, a gorgeous popinjay—a governor of an out-province driven into the capital by rebellion—lurched forward with flushed face and unsteady gait. He made obeisance before the divan.

“Most astounding Empress,” he cried, “fairest among Goddesses, Queen regnant of my adoring heart, hail!”

Phorenice smiled and stretched him her cup. I expected respectful libation poured out. He drank it all, draining it to the final drop.

“May all your troubles,” he cried, “pass from you as easily, and leave as pleasant a flavour.”

Phorenice turned to me. “You do not like this new habit?”

I said bluntly that pouring liquor at a person’s feet had through custom become respect, but drinking it was mere self-indulgence which could be practised anywhere.

“You still keep to old austere teachings,” she said. “Our newer code bids us enjoy life first, and order other things so as not to meddle with immediate pleasure.”

So the guests pursued gluttony and display beneath the roaring light-jets, while guards stood round the walls motionless and stern as the stone kings carved outside. The contrast—orgy within, discipline without—made the hall feel like a drum: taut hide held by rigid pegs, beaten at the center for noise.

But suddenly the noise changed.

There was a stir at the far doorway; a clash as two guards crossed spears. The man they tried to stop—perhaps to pin—passed unharmed. He walked across the pavement between the lights and the groups of feasters.

All turned to look. A few flung ribald words. None stepped to bar him. Mostly women shuddered as he passed, as though a breath of mountain cold had entered and laid a chill along their skin.

He halted before Phorenice’s divan and stared at her without obeisance.

He was a frail old man. White hair tumbled to his shoulders; a ragged beard hung like torn wool. Mud clotted his feet and legs as though he had come far through rough country and would not pause even to wash. His wizened body was bare save for a single cloth wound about shoulders and loins. In his hand he carried a wand, and at its tip the symbol of our Lord the Sun glowed.

That wand declared his caste. Yet in no other way could I recognize him.

I took him for one of those ascetics of the Priests’ Clan who forswear the nurtured life of the Sacred Mountain and dwell among the burning hills, where the ground splits, rocks fall, fire streams run, evil vapours rise, and monstrous beasts prowl. Such sojourners keep the might of the Holy Gods ever before the mind, and through that constant peril become chastened—and sometimes gain power over mysteries even the learned cannot reach.

Silence filled the hall when he stopped. Phorenice broke it first, her voice clear as a blade.

“Those two guards who held the door are not equal to their work. I cannot have imperfect servants; remove them.”

The soldiers next in rank lifted spears and drove them home. The two men who had admitted the old ascetic fell. One shrieked once; the other made no sound. Both thrusts were clean.

The old man’s voice rose thin and high, broken as wind through dry reeds. “Another crime added to your tally, Phorenice. Not half your army could have hindered my entrance had I wished to come. I am here to bring you your last warning. The Gods have shown you much favour; they gave you merit by which you rose above your fellows, till at last only the throne stood above you. It was seen good by those on the Sacred Mountain to let you have this last ambition and sit upon the throne long honourably filled by the ancient kings of Atlantis.”

Phorenice leaned back on her cushions, smiling as though at an amusing singer. “I seemed to get these things as I chose, and in spite of your friends’ teeth. I may owe you, old man, a small parcel of thanks—which I offered to repay. But as for my lords the priests, their permission was small value when it came. Remember I was firm upon the throne as this pyramid on its base when your worn-out priests crawled up with their tottering benediction.”

He waved aside her interruption. “Hear me out. I am here with no trivial message. There is nothing paltry in the threat I bring. With your fire-tubes, your handling of troops, your other fiendish clevernesses, you may not be easy to overthrow by mere human means—though these poor rebels who yap at your walls have held their ground long enough. It may be you are becoming enervated. It may be you are too wrapped up in feasting, dressing, pomps, and debaucheries to find leisure for war. It may the man’s spirit has gone out from your arm and brain and you are a woman once more—weak and pleasure-loving. I do not know.

“But this must happen: you must undo the evil you have done. You must give bread to the starving people, even if you take it from these gluttons in this hall. You must restore Atlantis to the state in which it was entrusted to you—or you must be removed. It cannot be permitted that the country sink back into lawlessness and barbarism from which its ancient kings have dug it. You hear, Phorenice. Now give me true answer.”

From behind us, Ylga’s voice came in a hurried whisper, quick as a knife drawn in panic. “Speak him fair. Oh—for the sake of your fortune, speak him fair.”

Phorenice did not so much as turn her head. She leaned forward, brow knit, eyes bright with anger and interest.

“Do you dare threaten me, old man,” she said, “knowing what I am?”

So this, then, was my welcome back in Atlantis’s capital—and my first taste of the new order that had risen while I was away.

I will not pretend to courage I did not feel. My dullness that day was chosen, not forced; a stiffness put on like a cloak. In the elder time, duty had been simple: to serve the king was to serve the Clan of the Priests, for the king had been their chosen vessel, and their head. But Phorenice had forged herself into power as a smith forges a blade—by heat, hammer, and her own will. If Zaemon spoke truth, he was the Priests’ mouth, and the Priests stood at open defiance of her. And where, in that split, was an honest man to plant his foot without slipping into treason against someone?

Cold reason, at least, offered one narrow plank: a government exists for the country’s good. I told myself I would choose by that measure. I would learn which hand—Phorenice’s, Zaemon’s, or any other—promised Atlantis the sounder harvest, the steadier hearth, the quieter nights. And at once a third thread showed itself in the tangle: talk of rebels, of another faction strong enough to ring the capital with iron and hunger. Before I bound myself to any banner, I would weigh theirs also.

So on that very night—the same day my foot had touched the old shore again—I went out to gather my own tidings.

Within the royal pyramid no one forbade me. The banquet had ended like a feast struck by lightning. With Tarca twisting on the floor and showing those frightful leprous scars as if they were proof of prophecy burned into flesh, even the most ravenous court-bellies had found their appetites fled. The smell of roasted meats and warmed wine had turned sour in the nostrils, as though the very spices had taken offense. Phorenice sat a while longer, glowering over the tables in silence, her anger thick enough to taste. At last her eyes came to me, and something in them softened, as a fire softens when a hand closes a damper.

“You may be an honest man, Deucalion,” she said at length, “but you are a monstrous cold one. I wonder when you will thaw?”

She smiled with it—an expression that promised either spring or a trap.

“I think it will be soon,” she added. “But for now I bid you farewell. In the morning we will take this country by the shoulders, and see it in some new order.”

Then she went, Ylga following. Because my rank gave precedence, I left next while the others stood and made their salutes. Yet I stopped beside Tarca first and laid my hand on his unclean flesh. The touch was like laying fingers on damp stone—alive, but wrong with suffering.

“You are an unfortunate man,” I said, “but I can admire a brave soldier. If relief can be gained for your plague, I will use interest to procure it for you.”

His thanks came mangled and low from his ruined mouth. Some of those nearest shuddered theatrically, as if the gesture of pity might soil them. I turned on them.

“Your charity, my lords, seems of as small account as your courage. You made a fine show of disbelieving Zaemon and sneering at his priesthood. But when it comes to laying a hand on him, you discover a discretion which, in the old days, we should have named with an ugly word. I had rather be Tarca, with all his uncleanness, than any of you as you stand.”

They gave me my due salutation at last, stiff and offended, and I walked from the hall without another glance.

At the grand gate of the pyramid I called for the officer of the guard and demanded exit. He bowed to the ground readily enough, but began with a careful reluctance.

“My lord’s attendants have not yet come up?”

“I have none.”

“My lord knows the state of the streets?”

“I did twenty years back. I shall be able to pick my way.”

“My lord must remember the city is beleaguered,” he persisted. “The people are hungry. They prowl in bands after nightfall, and—I make no question my lord would conquer in a fight against whatever odds, but—”

“Quite right,” I cut him off. “I covet no street scuffle to-night. Lend me, I pray you, a sufficiency of men. You will know best what are needed. For me, I am accustomed to a city with quiet streets.”

So they detailed a score of sturdy fellows for my escort. In double file they took station on either hand, and we marched out from the close perfumed air of the pyramid—heavy with incense, warmed wine, and the greasy sweetness of feast-smoke—into the cool moonlight of the city. My intent was to tour the walls and read, by sight and sound, the posture of these rebels beyond.

But the gods chose to teach me something else first.

The city I walked through that night was not the old capital I had known—the patient accretion of ages, where comfort and splendour had grown together like two vines twining the same trellis. Splendour remained, and was vastly increased. Whole wards had been torn away to make space for new palaces, new pyramids of the wealthy. Under the moon they stood like cliffs of dressed stone, their faces clean and proud, their angles sharp as a chisel’s first cut. I could not help admiring the brain and hand that made such works possible; indeed, seeing them silvered by the night, I almost understood why foreign barbarians worship great buildings as gods, believing no mere human could set them up without some hidden giant’s aid.

Yet even in that beauty the cost was advertised, as plainly as smoke tells of fire. From each plot where one of these stately piles now earned moonlight, a hundred families had been driven out to harbour as they might in open air. Every niche held sleepers. Every shadow held watchers—lean, fierce, half-made beasts of men, ready to pour out and rob or kill any wayfarer weaker than themselves. The city had its own night-language now: the hiss of whispered bargains, the pad of bare feet, the distant argument of hunger. Even our escort’s sandals sounded too loud, too rich.

I am no pamperer of the common people. I have always held that if a man is left to hunger and shiver, he will work to win bread and cloth; and if he will not, then let him die, and the State is rid of worthless flesh. But what I saw went past the stern justice of necessity into blind oppression. Starved bodies lay in gutters, bones standing out through skin as if the flesh had been scraped away. Here and there the wind carried the sour odor of sickness and unwashed humanity, and the sharper stench of things dead too long. It was plain that, save for the favored few, the people of this great capital lived under a weight that crushed thought flat as grain under a millstone.

And yet, though I abominated it, I could not at once call it treason. By the ancient law of the land, all the people, great and small, were the servants of the king to be used without question for his purposes. Phorenice stood in the king’s place. So I forced my eyes upward—above misery and squalor—to the stars hung like cold lamps in the purple night, and to the High Gods said to dwell among them. I asked for guidance, if any guidance could pass through the thick noise of a city that no longer slept.

In time the street-windings brought us to the walls. We coursed beside them, answering the sentries’ drumbeats as we passed—short, hard signals that chopped the quiet into pieces. At last we came to the great gate under the charge of the captain of the garrison.

Here there was commotion. Laughter rose into the still air—laughter with teeth in it—and now and again the snarl and roar of a great beast, and now and again the shriek of a hurt man. The sounds were thrown back by stone, multiplied in the hollow spaces of the gateworks, until the whole place seemed like a throat clearing itself of something foul.

This was no scene to be stumbled upon by chance. The entrance gates of our capital had been built in the old strength: four pairs of valves, each a monstrous block of stone two man-heights square and a man-height thick, with the wall doubled to receive them. Between the inner and outer walls lay an open circus, a killing-yard by design—an empty space intended to swallow an enemy. One set of valves opened only when the other was shut, the hidden machinery so linked that a careless moment could not betray the city. To force them without engines would be like pushing down the royal pyramid with bare hands.

My escort sounded the horn that hung from the wall. A warder came to an arrow-slit, and began the ancient formalities—long and tedious even in calm times. To-night the noise from within drowned speech again and again, so that each question had to be repeated, each answer shouted.

At last the rites were satisfied. The warder drew back stone bars and bolts and set ajar one tremendous valve. I and my men crowded into the passage within—the chamber formed by the thickness of the wall between the two doors. Then he pulled the opened valve to behind us and came bowing through the press.

“I have no vail to give you,” I said. “Get on with your duty. Open me the other door.”

“With respect, my lord, it would be better I should first announce my lord’s presence. There is a baiting going forward in the circus, and the tigers are as yet mere savages, and no respecters of persons.”

“The what?”

“The tigers, if my lord will permit them the name. They are baiting a batch of prisoners with two great beasts which the Empress—whose name be adored—has sent to aid us keep the gate. But if my lord will, there are ward rooms leading off this passage, and galleries which run out commanding the circus. From there my lord can see the sport undisturbed.”

The mere lust for killing excites only disgust in me. But I suspected Phorenice’s purpose in this—and I wanted to see her scheme with my own eyes. So I stepped into the warder’s lodge and on into the galleries that commanded the circus through narrow arrow-slits.

The old builders had meant these slits for slaughter: if the outer doors were forced, an enemy could be shot down in the circus without striking back—a death-trap that fed itself. As a place to watch, they served too well.

The circus below lay bright with moonlight, and the air that rose from it was acrid with blood, as if iron had been boiled and spilled. There was no sport in what was being done. It was mere killing, and it turned my stomach. I am no prude. Give a prisoner weapons, set him in a pit with beasts of reasonable strength, and let him fight for his last breath—there is something in that which a brave man may watch without shame. A war prisoner has earned death by the natural law, and often prefers to strike one last honest blow rather than be taken by the headsman’s axe. But this was not battle. This was insult.

To begin with, the beasts were no fair match for single men. Twenty armed might have fled from them. When the warder said tigers, I had supposed the great cats of the woods. But these were cave-tigers—huge striped monsters of such ponderous strength that, in hunger, they will drag down a mammoth if they find it away from its herd.

How they had been taken alive I could not guess. Hunter of beasts though I had been all my days, I approached the slaying of a cave-tiger with stratagem and infinite caution. To trap one living and bring it to a city on a chain lay beyond my boldest plans. Yet there they were, and in them I saw another proof of Phorenice’s genius: her mind did not stop at the customary tools.

Their purpose was plain. While those two cave-tigers ranged loose in the circus, no living thing could cross from one gate to the other. They were a living barrier, a new defense that needed no sleep and feared no arrow. Bronze collars ringed their throats, each collar fastened to a massive chain that ran to the wall, to be paid out or hauled in by windlass in hidden galleries. At ordinary times, they could be tethered one near each end, leaving a passage clear between the doors. The litter of bones and other filth showed how often that arrangement had been used.

But now the chains were slackened—though men stood by each windlass—and the two great brutes prowled, the links clanking and chinking behind them like iron laughter. Eight bodies lay stark on the pavement, dead and uneaten. The tigers nuzzled them, pawed them as one might paw at a toy, but made no beginning to feed. It was killing they wanted, not meat; or they had been taught to wait until the entertainment ended.

Presently a valve of the farther gate swung back an arm’s length, and a prisoner, resisting, was thrust out. He fell on his face. After one look about him, he lay still, eyes to the stone, as if by refusing to move he could refuse the game. The gate clapped shut. The tigers turned their heads. Behind the arrow-slits, men muttered wagers, their voices buzzing with anticipation.

There were niceties of cruelty even in this. The windlasses clanked; the chains were drawn in by a score of links. One tiger crouched, lashed its tail, and launched itself in a thunderous spring. The chain snapped taut; the links sang with strain; the beast’s roar shook the walls. It missed the prone man by a hand’s breadth. The watchers shrieked with delight. The other sprang and missed too, and again the hidden men cheered as if they had witnessed a clever trick at a festival.

The prisoner did not stir. One more cowardly, and he would have run; one more brave, and he might have faced death upright. This man chose the middle course: he let death come, and had doggedness enough to wait for it without flinching.

The tigers, used to the sport, ceased their wild springs. They lay down and began to purr, rolling on their sides, rubbing themselves luxuriously, like house-cats made monstrous. The man lay as he had fallen.

Slowly each brute drew to the end of its chain and began to reach toward the prisoner with an outstretched forepaw. The male could not touch him. The female could just reach with the tip of a claw. Each light stroke raised a red scratch on the man’s bare side. Still he would not move. I thought the keepers must slack out more links or else let the vile play sink into tedium.

But there was worse learning in store.

The male tiger rose and turned, backing against the chain. A yell of applause from behind the slits told that the watchers knew what came next. Stretched to the utmost of its length, the beast kicked sharply with one hind paw.

I heard the crunch of ribs as the pads struck. The prisoner’s body was spurned away as one tosses fruit. It did not fly far. The she-tiger knew the maneuver; she caught him as he bounded, nuzzled him a moment, then tossed him high and sprang after him, leaping to her full splendid height.

The onlookers found it magnificent. Their voices made a hideous music, ringing off the stone like a drumbeat at a slaughter. My gorge rose. The man had been killed without lingering once the tigers reached him—one light blow from such paws would slay the strongest alive—but to see the body toyed with was an insult to our race’s pride.

Yet I had not come to preach. I had come to learn how Atlantis now balanced itself, and what weight Phorenice had set on the scales. So I stood with a still face behind the arrow-slit and watched.

The cave-tigers tired of their play. One and then the other rose and prowled again over the littered pavement, chains scraping and chinking. They did not eat. Killing had swollen in them; they waited for more victims, as if they knew their keepers’ habits better than their own hunger.

Again the windlasses clanked, drawing the beasts away from the doorway. Again a valve of the farther door swung ajar, and another prisoner was thrust out—struggling.

Sickness seized me at once. It was a woman.

It was not that I had never seen women die. A general who has done his fighting has killed women as well as men, and seen women earn their death-blow with fierce battling. But this—this helpless sacrifice, stripped for a crowd’s amusement—was wanton cruelty, and it took all my restraint not to interfere. Still, policy has always demanded that the individual be sacrificed for the greater need. And it was becoming clearer with every breath of that blood-tainted air that Atlantis wanted a minister as much as it wanted mercy. So I watched on, outwardly cold.

But inwardly my heart quickened. Here was no sullen victim. Whether from fear or courage, it was plain she meant to fight, to hurt, to carve something back from fate before she was taken. The watchers sensed it too; their wagers rose into a hum of appreciation.

The prisoners were stripped before being thrust out—cloth has value, even when life is treated as if it has none. So the woman stood bare-limbed in the moonlight, skin pale against the dark stone.

She set her back to the gate she had come from and faced the circus with a glowing eye. Gods—there were times in my younger years when I would have leaped down sword in hand and fought beside her for the sheer delight of such a battle. But now I held myself, because the needs of a kingdom are heavier than the needs of one brave creature. And it should be noted: if I did not jump, no other man there that night had manhood enough to try.

She bent, chose a bone from the litter, and beat off its head against the wall. Then she put the broken end to her teeth and worked it to a sharper point. I saw her teeth gleam in the moonrays as she bit—white, resolute, like a tool used without pity.

The cave-tigers prowled nearer, high as her head as they walked, and yet they seemed to neglect her. That was part of their torment: the quiet padding, the ceaseless circling, the sense of enormous life choosing its moment. I could see her breast heave with the effort of not breaking. Many a victim would have been sick and helpless under such noiseless pressure.

But she was bolder than I had thought. She did not wait for the spring. She attacked first.

As the she-tiger strolled toward her and began to turn, the woman flung herself forward in a sudden leap and struck viciously at the beast’s eye with her sharpened bone. The onlookers roared their approval. The tiger’s eye was not darkened, but the weapon drew blood, a smart flesh wound, and the beast bellowed in surprise and pain, scampering away to make space for a rush.

The woman did not await it.

With a shrill scream she ran at full speed straight across the moonlight toward the shadowed stretch of wall beneath my gallery—toward the very thickness where the arrow-slits lay. Then, throwing every tendon into the spring, she made the most prodigious leap I believe any human ever made, driven by terror and desperate intelligence. In a later day I measured it; and though her run gave her force enough that her feet clung a moment to the roughness of the wall, it remains certain that in that splendid bound her feet must have dangled a man-height and a half above the pavement.

The end of the leap left her with five fingers hooked on the sill of the arrow-slit from which I watched. The sill sloped and the stone was smooth; alone, she would have slid back. I shot out my hand and gripped her wrist. Instantly she hauled herself up until both knees rested on the sill, and her fingers twined around to seize my wrist in return—like a drowning swimmer fastening on a rescuer.

Now you will suppose she poured out thanks and prayers, thinking only of safety. There was nothing of that in her.

Panting savagely, wordless at first, she tightened her grip on the sharpened bone with her free hand and lunged through the arrow-slit. With the hand that held my wrist she dragged me closer, to give her blow more certainty. I was so unprepared, and her fury was so fierce and sudden, that the first thrust near gave me my quietus. I grappled with her as gently as might be, the thickness of the wall between us, and stripped her of the weapon. Then I held her captive, firm but not brutal, until the wildness in her might lessen.

“That was an ungrateful blow,” I said. “But for my hand you’d have slipped and been the sport of a tiger’s paw this minute.”

“Oh, I must kill some one,” she panted, “before I am killed myself.”

“There will be time enough to think upon that some other day,” I answered. “For now you are far enough off meeting further harm.”

“You are lying to me. You will throw me to the beasts as soon as I loose my grip. I know your kind: you will not be robbed of your sport.”

“I will go so far as to prove myself to you,” I said.

I called for the warder. “Bid those tigers be tethered on a shorter chain,” I ordered, “and then go yourself outside into the circus and help this lady delicately to the ground.”

The word was passed. The windlasses answered with their clanking iron song. The great brutes were hauled nearer their posts until they could not reach the center. The warder went out, cautious as a man walking near a fire, and the woman was helped down. I came out into the circus myself and joined her under the moonlight.

But the men who had been watching were not suited by this change. Their laughter died into angry cries. From the farther gate a stone valve opened in haste, and a man strode out, armed and flushed with drink and cruelty.

“By all the Gods!” he shouted. “Who comes between me and my pastime?”

Sleep took me the way hunger takes a man who has walked too long without bread—suddenly, without bargaining. I had not known, until my eyelids dropped, how my body ached for the simple mercy of stillness. The temple floor was cold stone, slick with the night’s damp that crept in through cracks and narrow wall-slits; yet I lay there as if on piled wool, and went down into darkness at once.

When I woke, it was night already. Nais sat near me in the dim, watchful as a cat in a granary—silent, alert, listening to the world beyond the walls. The camp outside steamed and hissed from the day’s wetness; through the slits we could see fires guttering, their light smeared by drizzle. Men and women in skins and rags crouched close to the flames, turning their hands as if they were turning meat on a spit, trying to coax life back into numb fingers. The rain struck the coals with sharp little clicks—sound after sound, a whole harsh music of discomfort: the spit of water, the rasp of damp cloth, the cough of smoke caught in throats. It was not a quiet night. It was the kind of night that makes folk mean, the kind that steals sleep and leaves only watchfulness and a short temper behind.

“A fine night,” Nais said at last, voice low and dry. “If the air were kinder, I’d have had you away to the sea already and none the wiser. But in this cold they sleep sourly. We must wait until the hour drugs them deeper.”

So we waited, two figures in a temple that had forgotten incense and hymn. I tasted the stale air—stone-dust, old ash, damp moss—like the memory of a kitchen long shut up, where the hearth has gone cold and the pots hang untouched. We spoke little. There are silences that are not emptiness, only shared listening.

Then the night’s sound changed.

From far off came a horn-note—harsh as a cracked shell blown by an impatient hand. It did not call once and stop like a sensible signal; it worried the air, again and again, a raucous thread drawn across the camp’s nerves. The note came nearer. Voices rose in anger near the fires, shouted curses flung at the invisible blower. And then the sound steadied, no longer approaching: it had found its stage.

Through the slits we saw people stirring, climbing to their feet as though pulled up by that brutal tone. They gathered in a wedge of bodies around a figure raised above them on the pedestal of a war engine. He spoke, and though his words were blurred by distance and rain, the cadence of them came through: the rhythmic push of a man used to driving others’ minds where he wished.

Nais leaned toward me. “If all who won’t sleep go to feed their eyes on this windbag, you can slip out the other way.”

But chance, which loves a snare, had other arrangements. A dozen feet splashed up to the temple—men and women together—and voices called for Nais by name, loud and urgent. They wanted her at the gathering. She had a tongue, they said, that could cut through argument like a good knife through sinew. They feared this stranger-preacher would split their ranks unless someone stood up against him.

For a breath I thought: so here is how the gods amuse themselves—by dragging a man toward the very blaze he meant to avoid.

Nais’s face tightened. “Come out with me,” she whispered. “Keep away from the firelight.”

“And my being here?”

“There’s nothing to explain,” she said with a bitterness that tasted like scorched grain. “They’ll take you for my lover. It’s the fashion here. But—ah—why did the gods not give you a beard to curl like other men? Then you could have been gone and safe hours ago.”

“A smooth chin pleases me better.”

“So it does me,” she murmured, so softly it was almost swallowed by the rain, as she leaned her shoulder into the door-stone and pushed it ajar. “Your chin.”

We stepped out.

The air hit like a wet cloth slapped across the face—cold, mineral, smelling of smoke and soaked skins. The ragged ones waiting there did not study me long; their eyes were greedy for the gathering, not for details. They threw a jest or two, coarse as uncooked roots. I had the strength in me to pay such talk with steel, but steel was not the tool for this moment. They turned and sloshed into the dark, glancing back to be sure we followed.

All around, the camp moved as one creature. Shadows converged. Rain pattered on hides. Fires hissed and flared, and their smoke carried the scent of half-burnt fat, damp wood, and the sour breath of unwashed bodies. The soundscape was a bruise: muttering, splashing, horn-blast, the restless clank of weapons. There were no sleepers now; even those who had tried to burrow into warmth were dragged up by the promise of words. If we had swerved, the movement of the crowd would have betrayed us at once.

So we went straight on, carried like leaves in a stream, until we came close enough to hear.

“Who are your chiefs?” the preacher demanded at the end of some fierce speech.

The answer exploded back at him—an uproar, a thousand mouths finding unity in refusal. “We have no chiefs! We are done with chiefs! We are all equal here! Take away your silly magic. You may kill us with magic if you choose, but rule us you shall not. Nor the other priests. Nor Phorenice. Nor anybody. We are done with rulers!”

We were pressed nearer and nearer until the man above them resolved in firelight and rain.

He was old, naked but for a wisp of rag at his loins, his skin shining with wet as if newly oiled. White hair spilled to his shoulders; his beard was long, untrimmed, uncurled—more like a wild goat’s than a court priest’s. But in his hand he held what made all the difference: a wand whose tip burned with fire that did not consume. It glowed, twinkled, flared—an obedient little star, bright enough that raindrops, passing through its light, flashed like thrown salt. He pointed with it; he circled it; at times he swept it around himself until his wet, bare body seemed wreathed in a halo.

The Symbol of our Lord the Sun. No forgery could mimic that living light. No civilized man would doubt it.

The rebels did not doubt his caste either. Even while they screamed defiance, they admitted his power. “You may kill us with your magic,” they cried, and yet their stubbornness held: “but rule us you shall not. We have suffered too much. We are done with rulers for always.”

But my own blood went cold in a way the rain could not manage.

That old man was Zaemon—father to Nais. Zaemon who had seen me yesterday beside Phorenice, who could name me now if he chose. Four days earlier these same rebels had spent a navy trying to destroy me. If the name Deucalion was loosed among them while I stood packed in this crush, Nais’s cleverness would buy me nothing. The gods keep their tethers hidden; but I felt mine drawn thin.

Nais felt it too. Her fingers twined into mine—tight, urgent. A farewell pressed into skin. I pressed back, because even in the midst of danger I was sorry—sorrier than was useful—that I might not see her again.

And yet, beneath sorrow, another heat stirred: the old soldier’s lust for the final fight. I could almost taste it, iron and smoke on the tongue, like the scent of a knife just drawn from a sheath. If I was to die, I wanted to die in space enough to swing a blade and build a mound of the unskilled before they dragged me down.

For the moment, Zaemon did not look my way. He was wrapped in his own thunder. I listened, and measured the weight of his words in the crowd’s response.

He preached no novelty. He spoke of how, since history’s beginnings, the gods had set apart one Clan to rule and to serve as priests; and until Phorenice, he said, they had been just and exact. They had fought invaders and beasts; they had studied the earth’s tremors and learned to foretell earthquakes and eruptions; they had warned the people so they might flee. They had not ruled for personal gain but for Atlantis, honoring the kingdom the gods favored. Under their guidance Atlantis had risen to a height of prosperity and ease.

“But,” Zaemon cried, and the Symbol’s light washed his wet skin as if he stood under a falling furnace, “the people grew fat with comfort. They began to believe their fortune sprang from their own puny strength and wit, not from the gods’ gift. They neglected worship. Temples stood bare. And punishment came.

“In Their inscrutable way,” he went on, voice quavering but sharp, “the gods decreed that a wife of one of the Priests should find a woman child by the wayside and take it as her own. That child the gods fashioned into a scourge for Atlantis. You who have felt Phorenice’s hand know how perfectly the High Gods craft Their instruments.

“Yet as They set up, so can They throw down. Those appointed to debase Phorenice are even now chosen. The old rule shall return—but not until you, who have sinned, are chastened enough to cry out for it.”

He thrust the glowing wand toward them. “See! You know the unspeakable Power for which I am mouthpiece. Decide now. Are the gods to throw down this woman who scorned Them and trod you cruelly? Or must you be further purged before deliverance?”

He broke off, beard sinking against his chest. Immediately a young man, skin-clad, weapon in hand, forced his way up and sprang onto the engine beside him.

“Hear me, brethren!” he bellowed. “We are done with tyrants. Death may come—none here fear it. But rulers again we will not have. My lord,” he said, turning bravely to Zaemon, “I know you can kill me by magic. I have spoken. I can pay the price.”

“I can kill you, but I will not,” said Zaemon. “You have said your silliness. Now go down.”

“We have free speech here. I will not go till I choose.”

“Aye, but you will,” Zaemon said.

He did not strike. He did not even raise the Symbol. He only narrowed his brows, and the young man’s defiance broke as if a bone snapped without a hand touching it. The man tried to retort—and found his tongue dead in his mouth. His face twisted with sudden fear. He leaped down into the press like a man fleeing a flame.

The crowd trembled with expectation, and when no further marvel came, they began to shout for a miracle—something to feed their eyes, something to barter their rebellion for.

Zaemon stooped and glowered at them, fury making his old face seem carved from wet stone. “You dogs,” he cried. “Empty-witted dogs! Do you ask me to degrade the Higher Mysteries, to dance them before you like a mummer’s trick? Do you think the gods are to be tempted by your conditions? It is for you to woo Them back with humility. Come so, and I declare you will have pardon and relief. Remain stubborn, and Phorenice will scourge you into annihilation before she herself is made to answer. Choose.”

Voices surged. Weapons lifted, menacing as thorns. It was plain the party for “independence” held the greater weight—by numbers and by brute appetite for violence. Those who might have been weary enough to surrender kept silent, terror-muzzled by the loud ones. So much for the “free speech” they boasted of.

I leaned toward Nais and whispered the thought with a sour edge. But she clutched my hand hard.

“Be silent,” she begged. “They will tear you in pieces. They’re on fire for mischief.”

“Yet a few hours ago you meant to kill me yourself,” I could not help saying.

She flashed on me, hot-eyed. “A woman can change her mind. It becomes you little to remind her of it.”

A man beside me twisted around, peering at my face through shadow and rain. “Oh,” he said, voice thick with suspicion. “A shaved chin. Who are you, friend, that you cut a beard instead of curling it? I see no wound that forced you.”

I answered civilly enough that, since “freedom” was their watchword, my chin was private business. He pushed for more—one of those sour, quarrelsome men who rot any gathering. There was no room here for a brawl, no space to draw steel. So I used the older, quieter method.

I took him suddenly by throat and shoulder and turned his neck with a swift, practiced wrench. I heard the crack. I released him before any neighbor understood what had happened. The crowd’s press held his body upright; his head nodded forward as if he had fallen asleep from weariness. In a crush like this, a man may die and remain standing.

Zaemon’s voice still cut through the night. He began to chant the ancient ritual, setting forth the cult in the prescribed form. And the people—astonishing in their own way—answered in the old responses, at the old places, as habit awakened like embers stirred back to heat.

It was strange beyond telling: this long-neglected service, dragged out here in mud and rain among wild folk who had boasted of having thrown it away. Yet as they spoke the words, their voices took on a different tone—not only anger now, but something like remembered shelter. The gods’ old phrases fit their mouths again, as if they were tasting a forgotten bread.

Zaemon cried:

“...WHO STILLED THE BURNING OF THE MOUNTAINS, AND MADE COOL PLACES ON THE EARTH FOR US TO LIVE!—PRAISE TO THE MOST HIGH GODS.”

And the crowd answered, the sound swelling and rolling like surf:

“PRAISE TO THE MOST HIGH GODS.”

“WHO GAVE US MASTERY OVER THE LESSER BEASTS AND SKILL OF TEN TIMES TO PREVAIL!—PRAISE TO THE MOST HIGH GODS....”

“PRAISE TO THE MOST HIGH GODS....”

The earnestness of it could move a man’s bones. And yet it saddened too, because I knew how easily such fervor drains away from common people, leaving them empty and crueler for the emptiness.

When the ritual ended, Zaemon did not demand their loyalty again. He stepped down from the war engine, the Symbol thrust before him like a blade of pure daylight. The crowd had been packed to its limit, but the light drove them back. They recoiled as if the wand were a stream of lava. A lane opened.

And then the turning point came.

At first I thought—as perhaps all did—that the old man was making his way back to wherever he had come from. But his path held steady, and it aimed toward me.

The thought came clean and cold: having spoken to the many, he would now settle with the one. He had seen me yesterday as Phorenice’s minister. He could denounce me as Deucalion. If he chose, he could end me here without granting me even the last battle I craved.

I felt the ache of that loss sharply, like the pang of a man promised a feast and then offered only thin broth. Yet I had been trained to wear obedience as a second skin. If the council on the Sacred Mountain had decided Atlantis would prosper more with Deucalion sent to the gods, then I would bow.

So when Zaemon came close, I did not flinch. I stood my ground and gave him the salutation prescribed between our ranks. He returned it with full correctness.

The Symbol’s glow widened around us. The crowd was driven back into a ring, a sea of ravenous faces shining with wet and firelight. Even Nais, priest’s daughter though she was, could not stand against the ineffable force that poured from that sign; she was pressed away with the rest.

So Zaemon and I stood alone in the center, bathed in the unconsuming light, while the night hissed with rain.

The people drew their own conclusion at once. “A spy!” they roared. “A spy! Zaemon salutes him as a Priest!”

Zaemon turned to them, and something queer crossed his grim face. “Aye,” he said. “This is a Priest. And if I give you his name, you may find greater interest. This is the Lord Deucalion.”

My name went through them like sparks through dry grass—hatred, triumph, fear, hunger. They had already chosen me as enemy; they had already flung a navy at my destruction. Now, with me ringed in by their masses, they wanted to pull me to pieces with their fingers.

But the Symbol’s glare held them back. They raged and shuddered and edged away as though the light burned their skin. And Zaemon, emissary of the Priests’ Clan, would not hand me over until he had spoken what he had come to speak.

He faced me and spoke in the sacred tongue—the hidden language the common people do not know, a speech old as the first temples, heavy with meanings that do not translate cleanly into any market talk.

“My brother,” he said, “which have you come to serve—Deucalion or Atlantis?”

“Words are a poor thing to answer that,” I replied in the same tongue. “You know my record. By the Law, every ship from Yucatan will have carried its sworn report to the council. And before I went there, what I did was written plain on Atlantis’ face.”

“We know,” he said. “You governed well. You lived austerely. You set up Atlantis for mistress and served her. But you had no Phorenice to tempt you into change.”
