“You can send me where I shall see her no more, if you think me frail.”

“Yes,” he said, “and lose your usefulness. No, brother. You are the last hope this land has. All other human means against Phorenice have failed. You have returned from overseas for the final duel. You are the strongest we have. If you fail, only the terrible Powers locked within the Ark of the Mysteries remain. It is not lawful to speak of their scope—but you know their potency.”

I shrugged. “Then throw me to these wolves and be done. Let them end me now.”

Zaemon’s brows tightened. “I bid you do your duty. Why wish to evade it?”

“I remember your words in the pyramid,” I said. “‘Phorenice, whilst you are yet Empress, you shall see this royal pyramid—polluted with your debaucheries—torn tier from tier, stone from stone, scattered as feathers before a wind.’ You spoke as if you foresaw defeat.”

A shudder went through him. “I cannot tell what she may force us to do. I spoke what was revealed must happen. Perhaps, when matters have reached that pass, she will repent. But before we loose the more desperate weapons of the gods, it is fitting we expend what human strength remains. You must go, brother, and play your part to the utmost.”

“It is an order,” I said. “So I obey.”

“You shall be at Phorenice’s side again by the next dawn,” Zaemon continued. “She sent for you from Yucatan as a husband—thinking you have the arm to prolong her tyranny. You are a Priest, and a man of convincing tongue. You must make her see the invincible power that can be loosed against her. Make her understand the hopelessness of resisting.”

“If it is ordered, I will,” I said. “But the chance is small. I have seen Phorenice. Her will is a locked door. Others have tried. You have tried. All have failed.”

“Words wasted on a maiden may go home to a wife,” Zaemon said. “You have been brought here to be her husband. Take your place.”

The command struck me with a pain I had not expected. Women had been little to me through a life of campaigns and rule; and when I landed, the thought of taking Phorenice for policy’s sake had not revolted me. But the last two days had rearranged the world. I had seen two women who had drawn me strangely, and one of them had stirred a turmoil in me I had never felt amid my careful economies.

To marry Phorenice meant severance from that other woman forever. The thought tasted like smoke in the mouth, bitter and choking.

Yet I did not set my private hunger above the council’s will. I bowed to Zaemon and touched his hand to my forehead. “It is an order,” I said. “If our Lord the Sun grants me life, I will obey.”

“Then let us begone,” said Zaemon.

He took my arm and swept the Symbol before us. No further word passed between me and Nais. I would not risk tangling her in the rebels’ fury. But as we moved away, I caught one last hot glance from her eyes—like a brand held up in rain—and that had to serve as farewell.

The crowd opened before us, driven back by that starry light. They cried for my blood, their voices thick with hate; yet no one dared rush the lane. Not a hand lifted to strike. When we neared the outskirts and the ranks thinned, some surged as if to follow—then Zaemon turned and whirled the Symbol back into their faces. The blaze flared lurid. Men who had shouted bravely fell to their knees, grovelling like animals before a sudden fire. They did not press us further.

The rain still fell. As we passed the camp-fires, the old man’s wasted body gleamed with wet, the light turning each raindrop on his skin into a brief spark. Ahead, through the darkness, loomed the Sacred Mountain—vast, inevitable—its crest encircled by the ring of eternal fires.

I breathed out, and the sound was lost beneath the hiss of rain and the far horn’s fading echo. In my mind rose the memory of older days on that mountain—groves quiet enough to hear one’s own heartbeat, temples where the only sound was the measured chant and the soft crackle of incense on hot stone.

Peace, remembered, has its own sharpness. It cut me as we walked.

The tunnel had been masked with all the cunning a mason’s pride could muster, but the rock itself betrayed what it had once been: a vein where Earth’s hot blood used to run. The old lava had long since cooled, yet the passage still breathed warmth and brimstone; it laid a film on the tongue like overcooked egg, and it stung the back of my throat until every breath wanted to be a cough. I found the lamp set for us in its niche, struck it, and went on briskly, cursing under my breath the little refinements of medicine I no longer possessed. Once, in Yucatan, I had kept a packet of dried herbs—mullein and sweet resin, the sort of simples that, steeped in hot water, soften catarrh and lay a balm on a raw windpipe. That packet had gone to the bottom in the sea-fight with Dason’s navy, and since I set foot in Atlantis there had been no leisure for such domestic mercies.

The ways branched and rebranched like the roots beneath an old orchard tree. A man who came down here unprepared would wander until his lamp died and then fall, perhaps, into one of those pits that drop to the boil below. But I carried the chart of the true course not on parchment but in memory, fixed there from an initiation twenty years gone, when as an appointed viceroy I had been raised to the highest degree but one of our Clan and shown its implements and its secrets. The floor rose and broke in cruel ridges; the air grew fouler in pockets; the sulphur sat on the palate and soured even the thought of food. I knew the day would be far advanced before the signs would tell me I had passed under the walls and come within the city’s precincts.

And here the vow of the Seven hampered me. Under no stress, it is ordained, may egress be made from these passages before mortal eye. One branch after another I tried, hearing above, through stone, the muffled living of the city. Always there were loiterers near the outlets. I had hoped to rise by the path that comes out inside the royal pyramid; but that place was humming like a hive—too many feet, too many voices, too much light and movement to slip unseen. So with five more. The city was agog with some excitement that ran like yeast through dough.

At last I came beneath a temple of one of the lesser Gods. I waited behind the image long enough to make sure my luck was not a trap: the place was empty. Dust lay thick on floor and benches, a grey robe over everything, as though worship had been a language no one spoke here anymore. I moved the appointed stone, stepped out, closed the entry, and found a broom left on a pew like a forgotten tool after harvest. With it I scuffed and blurred my own footmarks until the dust looked as undisturbed as stale flour in a bin, then went to the temple door.

It was shut; priest though I was, its opening was beyond my knowledge. The doorway was filled with a massive stone that sat in its groove like a millstone. A pretty pass indeed. Only the attendant priests of this temple knew the mechanism. If they had all gone to gape at the spectacle that had set the city buzzing, I would be no nearer liberty than when I was choking in the veins beneath the earth.

No sound of life came from within the precincts—only the dry whisper of my lamp and the faint, sour breath of disuse. Yet by ancient law there must be always at least one on watch in the priests’ dwellings. So I made my way along the passage to the roof where those dwellings lay, and gave the call.

After a time the door opened upon darkness and a dull face peering out. The man who stood there had remained, at least, constant to his vows, and I made him the salutation with respect.

“Ro,” I said, for I remembered him.

We had passed through the sacred college together. Always he had been known as the dullard: slow to learn the cult, slower to master the arts of ruling, and hopeless with arms. Yet here he was, second priest and one of two custodians, raised not by merit but by the desertion of all others. It sat ill with me that a fool should remain true where cleverer men had abandoned the old beliefs.

He did the greater obeisance. His beard was curled in the prevailing fashion but clumsily; his robe hung ill and carried the smell of old sweat and stale oil. Ro had never been cleanly.

“The temple door is shut,” he said, “and I only have the secret of its opening. My lord comes here, therefore, by the secret way, and as one of the Seven. I am my lord’s servant.”

“Then serve me with words,” I said. “What stirs the city?”

His mouth tightened, and for once his dull eyes sharpened into spite. “That impious Phorenice has declared herself Goddess. She says she will light the sacrifice with her own divine fire—she will do it too. She does everything. But I wish the flames may burn her when she calls them down. This new Empress is the bane of our Clan, Deucalion, these latter days. The people neglect us; they bring no offerings. And now, since these rebels have been hammering at the walls, I might have gone hungry if I had not some small store of my own. I tell you, the cult of the true Gods is well-nigh oozed out of the land.”

“It comes to my mind,” I said, “that the priests of our Clan have been limp in their service to let these things come to pass.”

He shrugged in a way that made his robe gape at the neck. “We did as we were taught. But if the people will not come to hear your exhortations and neglect to adore the God, what hold have you? The High Gods try our own faith hard. Come in. Look there on my bed.”

On the pallet lay the shape of a man untidily swathed in reddened bandages. The room stank of old blood, damp cloth, and the sour taint of poultices left too long. Even in such a place there should have been some cleansing incense, some boiled herb-water to keep rot away; but Ro was Ro, and the old discipline was dying here.

“This,” he said, “is all that is left of the poor priest who was my immediate superior. It was his turn yesterday to celebrate the weekly sacrifice to our Lord the Sun with the circle of His great stones. Faugh! You should have seen how he was mangled when they brought him back.”

“Did the people rise on him?” I asked. “Has it come to that?”

“The people stayed passive,” Ro said bitterly. “What few cared enough to attend. Our Lord the Sun saw fit to try His minister harshly. The wood was laid, the sacrifice disposed according to the rites; the procession formed round the altar; and the drums and the trumpets were speaking forth so all men might know that the smoke of their prayer would presently be wafted up. But then, above the noise, there came the rushing sound of wings. From out of the sky flew one of those great featherless man-eating birds—bigger than is commonly seen.”

“An arrow in the eye,” I said, “or a long-shafted spear receives them best.”

“All men know what they were taught as children,” he returned, “but the priests were unarmed, as the rubric ordains. They must intrust themselves wholly to the guardianship of the High Gods during the hours of sacrifice. The bird swooped down, settled on the wood pyre, and attacked the offering with beak and talon. My poor superior, still strong in his faith, called loudly on our Lord the Sun to lend power to his arm and sprang up with naught but his teeth and bare arms for weapons. Perhaps he expected a miracle—he has not spoke since, poor soul—but all he met were blows from leathery wings and talons that went near to disembowelling him. The bird brushed him away as we would sweep a fly aside. There he lay bleeding on the pavement whilst the sacrifice was torn and eaten in the presence of all. When the bird was glutted, it flew away to the mountains.”

“And the people gave no help?”

“They cried it was a portent,” said Ro. “They said our Lord the Sun was a God no longer if He would not guard His own sacrifice. Some cried there was no God remaining; others that a new God had come to weigh on the country and had taken the form of a common man-eating bird. But a few began to shout that Phorenice stood for all the Gods now in Atlantis. That cry was taken up till the stones of the great circle rang with it. Some may have believed it; many liked the newness of it; and not a few joined because it is dangerous to leave such an outburst unwelcomed. The Empress can be hard on those who neglect to adore her.”

“The Empress is Empress,” I said formally, though the words tasted like ash. “Her name carries respect. It is not for us to question her doings.”

“I am a priest,” Ro said, “and I speak as I was taught. Whether there is a Faith any longer, I begin to doubt. Anyway, it yields a poor enough livelihood nowadays. There have been no offerings at this temple five months past. If I had not a few jars of corn put by, I might have starved for anything the pious of this city cared.”

Corn. Even in his whining, the word made my mind picture what he meant: clay jars sealed with pitch, the dry kernels whispering when poured, the faint smell of sun-baked grain. There was comfort and continuity in such stores, the old domestic prudence that survives even when temples go cold. Yet Ro spoke of it as a private hoard, not as a sacred trust. The old order was not only attacked; it was being forgotten in the very habits of those who claimed to keep it.

“When did it happen?” I asked.

“Twenty hours ago. To-day Phorenice conducts the sacrifice herself. That is the stir. The city is in the throes of getting ready one of her pageants.”

“Then open the temple doors,” I said. “I must go and see this thing for myself.”

“It is not for me to offer advice to one of the Seven,” he began, and then his voice sank into a mumble. “But they say the Empress is not overpleased at your absence. I should not like harm to come in your way, Deucalion.”

“The future is in the hands of the most High Gods,” I told him, “and I at least believe They will deal out our fates as They in Their infinite wisdom see best—though you seem to have mislaid your faith. Now I must be your debtor for passage. And, plagues, man, it is no use holding out your hand. I do not own a coin in all the world.”

He muttered something about force of habit as he led me down. I spoke tartly of the unpleasantness of his begging customs.

“If it were not for your sort and your customs,” I said, “the Priests’ Clan would not be facing this crisis to-day.”

“One must live,” he grumbled, pressing the hidden levers. Stone groaned on stone; the great door swung ajar, and a slice of daylight cut into the dim.

“If you had been a more capable man,” I said, “I might have seen the necessity.” I passed out and left him.

I could never bring myself to like Ro.

A motley crowd filled the street before the obscure temple, all hurrying one way. They flowed like a stream that has found a new channel, and I let it take me. With what Ro had said, it needed little art to guess their mark: the great stone circle of our Lord the Sun. It grieved me to think how many venerable centuries that fane had stood, braving weather and earth tremors, without witnessing such profanation as it would see to-day.

From one quarter of the walls the din of attack carried over the roofs: the dull thud of rams, the rattle and shriek of darts, the deep, grinding complaint of stone struck by heavier engines. But no one heeded it. They had grown callous to bombardment. Their nerves, their hunger, their desperation were strung so tight that little short of an actual storm would lift their attention from this new excitement.

All were weaponed. The naked carried arms in hopes of finding someone to overcome and rob; those who still had a possession walked ready to fight for it. There was no security, no trust. The lesson of civilisation had dropped away as mud washes from feet in rain, and in habit and thought these townsfolk had slid back toward a savagery—raw need, loud impulse, and the unspoken rule that the strongest mouth eats first.

The streets we wound through had once been clean and trim; now they were foul and mud-strewn, with stale refuse rotting in corners and the sharp, ammoniac stink of neglect. Men and women had died of hunger here in these later years and rotted where they fell. We trod bones underfoot without pause. Yet above this misery rose pyramids and palaces, splendid beyond telling, their polished faces catching the sun as though they belonged to another city entirely. That jarring mixture—glitter over carrion—was Phorenice’s Atlantis in one view.

At last we came to the open space at the city’s center, ground so ancient and so secular that even Phorenice had not dared to encroach upon it with her ambitious schemes. There stood the most grand and the eldest of our temples: the circle of stones raised to our Lord the Sun.

Since the beginning of time—since man first rose from among beasts—our Lord the Sun has been his chiefest God. Legend says He raised the circle Himself so votaries might offer worship. Modern fashion scoffs at such tales; but for myself, this one satisfies me. By our wits we can lift blocks weighing six hundred men and set them as capstones on pyramids. Yet to uprear those stones—undressed, rugged, enormous—would be beyond our art; and more impossible still to haul them from their distant quarries over the mountains.

There were nine-and-forty stones, set in a perfect circle with spaces between, and across their tops lay other stones just as huge, bridging them like the laid beams of a roof. In the center stood the altar stone, greater still, carved with the rude chiseling of the ancients: the snake and the outstretched hand. The carving had the blunt honesty of a language that does not bother to flatter—marks hammered into rock by men who named the world with a few strong syllables and were understood. Even now, that old speech seemed to hum under the newer tongues that chattered around it, as if the place remembered words the people had forgotten.

The crowd that bore me on stopped at the circle’s edge. For common folk to trespass within is death. I had the right of entrance, but I stayed where I was, merged among the mob, unnoticed, waiting. The sun burned high in a clear blue sky; heat pressed down until sweat tasted of salt and metal. The siege noise still came from the walls, but even that was swallowed by anticipation.

We waited long. I learned later that the rebels’ attack had been so pressing, and their new engines so destructive, that Phorenice had gone first to the walls to lend her brilliant skill for repulse and put heart into the defenders. The day scorched toward its middle before drums and horns began to speak from afar that the pageant had formed. Many in the crowd fainted; not a few died where they stood. No one heeded the fallen. In Atlantis now, life was cheap as stale husks.

Nearer and nearer came the percussion—drums that struck like fists on hollow wood, and brazen horns that brayed and tore at the ear. There is an ecology to sound as there is to water and air; a city’s spirit is shaped by what it hears. Once, the music of Atlantis had been measured—chants that rested the mind, flutes that braided with birdsong, silence kept in reverence around old stones. Now the noise was made to overwhelm: to drive thought out as smoke is driven from a room. Even the siege had its own brutal rhythm, but this was different—a commercial din of worship turned spectacle, sound sold as power.

The head of the procession arrived and disposed itself in the appointed space. Lords and courtiers glittered—jewels flashing, fine cloth swaying, gold and dyed feathers paraded in wanton splendour. Many a starving wretch sighed at the sight. These new nobles of Atlantis cared for their bellies and their backs, and for one other appetite: their simpering adulation of Phorenice. Their loyalty was real enough, for everything they had came from her lavish hands.

And then she appeared.

She rode in the golden castle—where I also had ridden—set upon the back of her monstrous shaggy mammoth. At once the crowd’s starved, sullen faces brightened as though a meal had been laid before them. Without command, without compulsion, they burst into shouts. The sound hit like a wave, a massed roar that made the stones seem to tremble.

She acknowledged them with a smile of thanks. Her cheeks were a little flushed, her movements quick, her manner high-strung—as well it might be, with the sacrilege she meant to commit. Yet she was undeniably lovely, more adorably beautiful than ever with that thrill of excitement running through her. When the stair was brought and she stepped down from the mammoth’s back, those nearest fell to their knees and gave her worship out of sheer fascination.

Only Ylga, the fan-girl, kept her formal paces and duties without surrender. Even from where I stood I could see she looked pained and troubled, as though jewels lay on her shoulders like weights. It was fitting. Though long fostered by the Empress, Ylga was Zaemon’s daughter, and he was chiefest of our Lord the Sun’s ministers on earth. She could not wholly forget her upbringing at this supreme moment when the highest of the old Gods was to be openly defied. Perhaps, too, because she cared for Phorenice, she dreaded what such defiance might call down.

But Phorenice had no eye for a single sad face among that sea of glowing ones. Boldly, proudly, she strode into the circle as though duly appointed priest. After her came a knot of men dressed as priests, bearing the victim. Some were her creatures—ignorant laymen, dazzled by her. But some, to their shame, were men born in the Priests’ Clan, raised in the groves and colleges of the Sacred Mountain. For their apostasy there could be no palliation.

The wood had already been stacked on the altar in the form the ancient symbolism requires—pieces laid with care as a cook lays kindling for an even flame: thicker logs as the base, smaller above so the fire may take and travel. In a proper sacrifice, the smell of the chosen wood matters; resinous branches give a sweet lift to smoke, and the old priests knew which trees pleased which Gods. Here, I could see, the pile had been made by rule but without reverence, as if copying a recipe in a dead language.

Phorenice stood aside while they did what was needful. As they opened out, I saw the victim: one of the small cloven-hoofed horses that roam the plains, an acceptable offering. They bound its feet with metal gyves and set it upon the pyre. For a while it lay there, flanks heaving, eyes rolling white, giving little neighs that were thin with terror. Then they stepped back and left it living.

That was an innovation, and a cruel one. The true rite does not linger over suffering for spectacle.

The false priests went to the farther side of the circle. Phorenice stood alone before the altar. She lifted her voice—sweet, tuneful, carrying. Though the siege din still came from the walls, no ear there lost a word. She raised her glance, and all eyes followed.

The heaven had been clear as deep sea, a gorgeous blue. But as her words came, a small mist was born overhead, wheeling and circling like a ball though the day was windless. It grew rapidly darker and more compact until it threw shadow on part of the sacred circle and soothed it into twilight, though outside, where the people stood, the day remained garish and hard. Within that ball of mist, little quick stabs of noiseless flame winked and vanished like sparks under a pot lid.

She did not speak in the priests’ sacred tongue—though such was her wicked cleverness she may have learned it—but in the common speech of the people, so that all might understand. She told of her wondrous birth, as she chose to name it, and of direct aid from the most High Gods that had enabled her to work marvels. In the telling she used words that glittered and slid, bending old meanings to new ends. Even the very name of “God” sounded altered on her lips, as though she had taken a word meant for the heavens and pressed it into service for herself. I felt, with sudden sharpness, how language dies: not always by silence, but by being made to mean what it never meant before, until those who inherit it can no longer taste its original flavour.

At last she lifted both her fair white arms toward the blackness above. Her face set with strain; her limbs shook.

“O my high Father, the Sun,” she cried, “acknowledge me as Your very daughter. Give this people a sign that I am indeed a child of the Gods and no frail mortal. Here is sacrifice unlit, where mortal priests with their puny fires had weekly, since the foundation of this land, sent savoury smoke toward the sky. Send down the heavenly fire to burn this beast here offered, in token that though You still rule on high, You have given me Atlantis to be my kingdom, and the people of the Earth to be my worshippers.”

She broke off and strained as though dragging the sky down by will alone. Her beauty distorted; the lovely mouth tightened; the tendons stood in her throat.

“O mighty Father,” she cried, “who hast made me a God and an equal, hear me! Hear me!”

Out of the black cloud came a blinding flash that spat down onto the altar. The cloven-hoofed horse gave one shrill neigh, one convulsion, and fell back dead. Flames crackled out through the wood pile. At once the air grew thick with the smell of burning flesh—fat turning and dripping into fire, hair singing, the sick-sweet reek that clings to the nostrils and settles in the throat. Smoke rose, first heavy and oily, then thinning as the blaze steadied.

And lo: in another moment the cloud melted into nothingness. The flames burned pale; the smoke went up in a thin blue spiral toward the deeper blue of the sky, as neat and composed as though it had always belonged there.

Phorenice stood before the great stone and before the snake and the outstretched hand carved upon it, flushed and exultant, radiantly lovely once more. The knot of priests within the circle, and the great mob without, fell to the ground adoring.

“Phorenice, Goddess!” they cried. “Phorenice, Goddess of all Atlantis!”

But I did not kneel. I would have no part in this apostasy. I stood where I was, unbowed among the bowed, and waited for fate.

A whisper ran through the ring of kneeling bodies like wind through dry reeds, and in a breath it thickened into a growl of voices.

“Kneel.”

“Kneel, beardless one—kneel, or you will be marked.”

Another, nearer, spat the words as if they were bone splinters. “Kneel and do obeisance to the only Goddess, or by the old Gods I’ll make myself her priest and butcher you!”

The air itself seemed to bruise with that roar—until a name began to skip from mouth to mouth, softening the fury as water softens meal into dough.

“Deucalion.”

“Deucalion—”

It was not Deucalion the absent viceroy of Yucatan they feared; that was a story grown thin with distance. It was Deucalion they remembered at Phorenice’s side beneath the writhing canopy of golden snakes, the one who might still have teeth enough to bite back. The shouting sagged, grudgingly, into silence.

When the hush had settled and Phorenice turned, seeing me upright among prostrate worshippers, I stepped from the press. Between two of the great stones I passed, over the trampled ground, and across the circle toward the altar where she stood. I did not kneel. At the proper distance I gave the salutation she herself had required when she made me chief minister, and hailed her with the measured decorum of court.

“Empress.”

Her smile came sideways, like a knife drawn half an inch. “Deucalion, man of ice.”

“I still adhere to the old Gods.”

“I was not referring to that,” she said, and let the words linger as if they were perfume she expected me to breathe.

Then Ylga hurried up, pale as scalded milk, her hand fluttering like a bird’s wing against her breast. She fell into supplication for my life, voice breaking in little jerks.

“If he will not leave the old Gods yet, surely you will pardon him. He is strong—he does not become a convert easily. You may change him later. But think, Phorenice, he is Deucalion; and if you slay him here for this one thing, there is no other man within all the marches of Atlantis who would so worthily serve—”

Phorenice cut her off as one snips a thread. “You slut!” she cried. “I keep you near to appoint my wardrobe and carry my fan, and you dare put a meddling finger on my policies? Back outside the circle, or I’ll have you whipped. Ay—and I’ll do more. I’ll serve you as Zaemon served my captain Tarca. Shall I point a finger and smite that pretty skin with a sudden leprosy?”

The girl’s shoulders caved. She went away as if each step cost her. Phorenice watched her go, then turned her full attention to me.

“My lord,” she said, and there was an eager brightness in her, as though she had only just discovered she owned a body and a will, “I am like a young bird in a nest that has suddenly found its wings. Wings have so many uses that I am curious to try them all.”

“May each new flight be for the good of Atlantis.”

An eye-flash—impatient, amused. “Oh, I know what you have most at heart. We will go back to the pyramid and talk this out at more leisure. Now, my lord, conduct me to my riding beast.”

So the refusal to worship her—publicly, before all—was for the moment swallowed, and Ylga’s intercession counted not as loyalty but as insolence. I did not understand the exact measure of either, but I accepted what advanced the work Zaemon had laid on me.

Phorenice offered her fingers. Warm they were, fine-boned and delicate despite their talent for holding war. I took them gravely and led her out of the great circle she had befouled with her tricks. I had expected the Sun to strike at the profanation in the very act—to scorch, to split stone, to make an example. Nothing came. The Lord chooses His own hour, and His own instrument; my arm is too small to guard His honour.

We walked away and the smell of sacrifice followed us: a heavy, copper-sweet reek underlaid with smoke and hot fat that clung to the back of the throat. It should have been the clean, honest scent of a hearth—meat seared over flame, salt driven into fibre—but this was worship’s kitchen, where piety and cruelty share the same pot.

At the edge of the square the huge red mammoth waited, shaggy as a hill in winter. Its breath steamed out, thick with the animal musk of old hair and warm hide. On its back rose the golden castle with its writhing canopy of royal snakes, metal scales catching the sun like fish in a net.

Phorenice climbed the stair and settled herself on the cushions, then bade me mount beside her. The seat behind—where the fan-girl should have perched—was empty. Our words, as we set off through the streets, had no ears to sip them up and carry them away.

She pressed me at once for what had befallen after the attack on the gate. I told her, and gave weight to Nais’s worth—cleverness, thought, that rare thing which seasons a crowd as a pinch of bitter herb seasons a stew. I said also that with care the girl might be won back to allegiance. Of Zaemon’s commands—spoken between him and me in the sacred tongue—I said nothing. Those matters are not for repetition save before the Ark of the Mysteries in the High Council itself.

“You seem to have an unusual kindliness for this rebel Nais,” Phorenice said.

“She showed herself more clever and thoughtful than the common herd.”

“Ay.” Her sigh sounded real enough, a sound like a harp string touched once and left to die. “An Empress loses much that meaner women get as common due.”

“In what particular?”

“She misses the honest wooing of her equals.”

“If you set up for a Goddess—” I began.

“Pah!” She snapped the word away. “I wish to be no Goddess to you, Deucalion. That was for the common people—power, and convenience, and a leash on their minds. All you Seven higher priests know that trick of calling down fire. It pleased me to filch it. Can you not be generous and admit a woman may be as clever in finding out natural laws as your musty elders?”

“Remains that you are Empress.”

“Nor Empress either. Think only that there is a woman seated beside you on this cushion. Look at her and say what words come first to your lips. Have done with ceremonies and statecraft. Do you wish to wait on till all your manhood withers? It is well not to hurry—yes, I grant that. Yet who but a fool watches fruit grow ripe, and then leaves it till it rots?”

The mammoth’s feet thudded a slow drum against the stone, a deep, living percussion that made the street’s smaller sounds—sandals, murmurs, the clink of weapons—seem like the hiss of frying oil around a great roast. Above it all drifted the city’s constant chorus: distant hammering in the smiths’ ward, donkey brays, the chatter of vendors selling salt fish and dried fruit, and under that, like a sour note that would not resolve, the siege’s far-off groan—creak of timber, twang of cord, the occasional thin whine of something thrown.

Phorenice’s beauty was a torch beside me. Yet, as I live, it left me cold. I remembered the command laid on me and forced a smile as one forces down food one cannot stomach.

“I may have been fastidious,” I said, “but I do not regret waiting this long.”

“Nor I. But I have played my life as a maid time enough. I am a woman—ripe, full-blooded—and the day has come when I should be more than what I have been.”

I let my hand close on hers. “Take me to husband then, and I will be a good man to you. But as I am bidden speak to Phorenice the woman now, and not to the Empress, I offer fair warning: I will be no puppet.”

She gave me that sidelong look again, measuring, tasting. “I have been master so long that I think it will come as enjoyment to be mastered sometimes. No, Deucalion—I promise you shall be no puppet. Indeed, it would take a lusty lung to do the piping if you were to dance against your will.”

“Then as man and wife we will live together in the royal pyramid. We will rule this country with all the wit the High Gods have bestowed. These miserable differences shall be swept aside. The rebels shall go back to their homes and hunt and fight beasts in the provinces. The Priests’ Clan shall be pacified. Phorenice—you and I will throw brain and soul into the government and make Atlantis rise again for peace and prosperity.”

Petulantly she drew her hand away as though I had smeared her with ash. “Oh, your conditions—and your Atlantis! You carry a crudeness in these colonial manners of yours, Deucalion, that palls after the first blunt flavour has worn away. Am I to do all the wooing? Is there no thrill of love under all your ice?”

“In truth, I do not know what love may be. I have had little enough speech with women all these busy years.”

“We were a pair then, when you landed,” she said, “though I have heard sighs and protestations from every bearded man in Atlantis. Some tickled my fancy for a day; none moved me deeper. No—I also have not learned what this love may be from my own feelings. But, sir, I think you will teach me soon, if you go on with your coldness.”

“From what I have seen,” I said, “love is for the poor, the weak, the flighty.”

Her laughter came sharp. “Then I would another woman were Empress, and I some ill-dressed gutter creature a strong man could pick up by force and carry home for sheer passion. Ah—how I could revel in it! How I could respond if he caught my whim!” She shook her curls. “But I should lead him a sad life of it if my liking were not as strong as his.”

“We are as we are made,” I said. “We cannot change the inwards that move us.”

Her glance turned sullen, heavy with threat like a storm cloud pressing low. “If I do not change yours, my Deucalion, there will be more trouble brewed for the Atlantis you set such store upon. There will be ill doings in this household of ours if my love grows for you and yours remains unborn.”

She would have had me fondle her there—on the mammoth’s back, in the golden castle—while the city stared and the street’s smells rose up: fish brine, crushed herbs, the sweet rot of fruit peelings, the smoky breath of cooking fires where poor folk boiled grains in thin broth. Privacy never tasted necessary to her. For the life of me I could not. The Gods know I was earnest about my task; They know also how it repelled me. I had put away personal liking to obey the Council. Had I known the craft of it—how to speak and touch as those jeweled triflers about the court do—I would have followed her mood. But I stuck, tongue and hand, as if the words themselves were stones in my mouth.

There was no help but to leave unsaid all except what I truly felt. Diplomacy I had learned; on most matters my speech could be smooth enough. But to palter with women was an art I had neglected, and if I tried to coin pretty phrases from clumsy inexperience, Phorenice would have weighed them and found them false at once. She had been fed for years on pap of protestations; she could taste the starch of a lie as a cook tastes flour.

And honest confession would not serve either. Plain truth would sour what must, for Zaemon’s bidding, be brought to seeming sweetness. So I held my tongue and submitted to be counted a clown.

“I had always heard,” she said, “that you colonists in Yucatan were far ahead of those in Egypt in arts and graces. But you, sir, do small credit to your vice-royalty. Why, I have had gentry from the Nile come here, and you might almost think they had never left their native shores.”

“They must have made great strides these last twenty years,” I said. “When last I was sent to Egypt to report, the blacks were masters of the land, and our people lived there on sufferance. Their pyramids were puny, their cities nothing more than forts.”

“Oh, they are mere exiles still,” she said, mockery like vinegar, “but they remember manners. My poor face seemed to please them—at least they went into raptures over it. And for ten pleasant words one cut off his own right hand. We made the bargain, my Egyptian gallant and I, and the hand lies dried on a shelf in my apartment to-day as a pleasant memento.”

Before I could answer, chance threw its own interruption between us.

The rebels outside the walls were attacking with vigour and some thought. More than once during our progress lighter missiles from their engines had sung overhead and split against a building, showering splinters that nicked those packed in the streets. It was the common music of a siege—no more to trained nerves than the crackle of a skillet to a cook. But presently the rebels changed their seasoning. They ceased to hurl stone and began to load their engines with wooden carcases lined with throwing fire.

Against stone, such things did little but scorch; yet when they fell among the rude booths and rush shelters of the poorer wards, they took hold at once. Dry reed and old wood drank flame like oil. There was no quenching it—no patient pouring of water, no smothering with earth. It was a fire that fed on itself, blue smoke first, then the pale tongue of flame showing under sun as if the day had bitten its own lip and bled.

Phorenice and I might still have passed it with small concern—trivial incidents of every siege—had the mammoth been properly schooled. But the huge beast knew fire in its bones, older than any collar or chain.

When the first blue whiff of smoke reached us down the street’s windings, the mammoth lifted its trunk and began to sway its head. When the drifts thickened and flame flickered, it stopped and trumpeted, the sound a brutal tearing that drowned human voices and turned the city’s chorus into a frightened muddle. The acoustic order of the street—vendor calls, children, the clean clack of hooves—was ripped apart by that one ancient scream. Even before the beast moved, the noise itself infected every mind around us with panic; men’s shoulders tightened, women clutched their own throats, as if sound were hands.

The guards tugged at the chains that hung from the jagged metal collar round its neck. The spikes bit deep, and blood welled—hot, dark, and sharp-smelling as iron left out in rain. But with each drop, its bought tameness seemed to ooze away. Fire was the mammoth’s oldest enemy. Men have always sheltered behind flame, and mammoths have always learned to dread it.

Phorenice’s brow darkened. She shook her red curls viciously. “Some one shall lose a head for this blundering. I ordered to have this beast trained to stand indifferent to drums, shouting, arrows, stones, and fire. The trainers assured me all was done, and brought examples.”

I slipped my girdle free. “Here—quick. Let me lower you to the ground.”

She turned with a gleam. “Are you afraid for my neck, then, Deucalion?”

“I have no mind to be bereaved before I have tasted my wedded life.”

“Pish! There is little enough danger. I will stay and ride it out. I am not one of your nervous women, sir. But go you, if you please.”

“There is little enough chance of that now.”

The mammoth’s fear mastered the pain of spikes and the tug of men. With wild squeals it turned and charged back down the way it had come. Spearmen were scattered like straw. A swath opened through the crowd as if a scythe had passed. Cries rose—thin, brief, swallowed by the beast’s own bellowing and the thunder of its feet. The golden castle lurched and swayed so that we had much ado not to be shot off like stones from a catapult.

I braced my feet and wrapped an arm around a pillar, then clapped my spare arm around Phorenice, offering my body as a buffer. She lay content enough, her face just beneath my chin, hair dampened with heat and fear-sweat, its scent faintly spicy—like crushed leaves warmed on a stone.

The mammoth ran on, insane with panic. We had outstripped the smoke—the cause—yet the fear remained as a taste remains after bitter herb has been swallowed. It held its trunk aloft and deafened us with screams. We left behind the glittering pageant of guards and courtiers and were carried helpless through ward after ward.

The brute was beyond control. Its pace made leaping suicide: even if we avoided shattered limbs, we would land at a frightful disadvantage, and the mammoth would scent us, turn, and trample us to pulp in moments. Up in the castle we were beyond its reach.

A thought came, cold and distinct: here might be the High Gods’ answer to sacrilege. The mammoth appointed as instrument, I appointed as witness. Yet no direct revelation had been given; I took no initiative. I clung, and held the Empress from bruises.

The streets wound and doubled, confusing the beast, and still it ran. By degrees we left the great palaces and pyramids behind and came among artisans—smiths and weavers gaping from doorways, their forges’ clang momentarily silenced by the passing thunder. Then the merchants’ quarter, where the air smelled of tar, rope, salt, and foreign spices kept in jars: peppery seeds, dried citrus peel, pungent resins. And then, down an open space, water glittered—harbour-water like a sheet of hammered bronze.

Now, I thought, the beast will stop and swerve and charge back. I tightened my grip. But it held straight on, screaming louder, and plunged into the harbour, sending spray up in bright sheets.

Its pace slackened at once. Here the great sewers—devised in old King’s time for the city’s health—vomited their drainings, and the harbour bed was layered with an impalpable sludge. Into that the mammoth sank deeper and deeper. When it finally stopped, bogged beyond hope, it struggled madly. Water and slime churned into a stinking compost—sour, rank, like grain left too long in a wet sack. The golden castle lurched so wildly that we were torn from it and shot out into the water.

There, of course, we were safe, and I was pleased enough to be rid of the bumping.

Phorenice laughed as she swam, the sound bright against the harbour’s foul breath. “You handle yourself like a sore man, Deucalion. I owe you something for lending me the cushion of your body. By my face, there’s more gallant about you at the test than one would guess to hear you talk. How did you like the ride, sir? I warrant it came as a new experience.”

“I’d liefer have walked.”

“Pish, man! You’ll never be a courtier. You should have sworn that with me in your arms you could have wished the bumping had gone on for ever. Ho, the boat there! Hold your arrows. Deucalion, hail me those fools. Tell them if they hurt so much as a hair of my mammoth I’ll kill them all by torture. He’ll exhaust himself directly, and when his flurry’s done we’ll leave him where he is to consider his evil ways for a day or so, then haul him out with windlasses and tame him afresh. Pho! I could not feel myself to be Phorenice if I had no fine red shaggy mammoth to take me out for my rides.”

A ten-slave galley churned toward us from the far side of the harbour, oars biting the water in time to the whips’ persuasion. Soldiers on the foredeck had begun to shoot, but at my shouts they stopped, and the steersman swerved to pick us up. We had been swimming at ease to avoid landing near the sewer outpour; now we stopped and trod water until respectful hands hauled us over the side.

The galley belonged to the captain of the port—a mincing mariner whose chief appetite was to lick the feet of the great. He prostrated himself at once, wishing his eyes had been blinded before seeing the Empress in such deadly peril.

“The peril may pass,” Phorenice said, shaking water from her sleeves. “It is nothing mortal that will ever kill me. But I have spoiled my pretty clothes, and shed a jewel or two, and that is annoying enough as you say, good man.”

The fool repeated his wish to be blinded.

She narrowed her eyes. “If you are tired of them, let me tell you you have gone the way to have them plucked out of their sockets. Kill my mammoth, would you, because he has shown himself a trifle frolicsome? You and your sort want education. Port-captains are easy to come by and small value when got. But my mammoth is mine—mine, do you understand?—property of Goddess Phorenice, and as such sacred.”

The port-captain abased himself lower. “I am an ignorant fellow,” he said, “and heaven was robbed of its brightest ornament when Phorenice came down to Atlantis. But if reparation is permitted, I have two prisoners in the cabin here who shall be sacrificed to the mammoth forthwith. Doubtless it would please him to make sport with them, and spill the last lees of his rage upon their bodies.”

“Prisoners you’ve got, have you?” Phorenice’s voice sharpened with interest. “How taken?”

“Under cover of last night they tried to pass between the two forts that guard the harbour mouth. Their boat fouled the chain, and by torchlight the sentries spied them. They were caught with ropes and put in a dungeon. There is an order not to abuse prisoners before judgment.”

“It was my order. Did these prisoners offer to buy their lives with news?”

“The man has not spoken. Indeed, I think he got his death-wound in being taken. The woman fought like a cat also, so they said in the fort, but she was caught without hurt. She says she has nothing of use to tell. She says she has tired of living like a savage outside the city, and moreover that inside there is a man for whose nearness she craves most mightily.”

“TUT!” Phorenice said, delighted. “Is this a romance we have swum to? You see what affectionate creatures we women are, Deucalion.”

By then the galley had been brought against the royal quay and made fast to its golden rings. I handed the Empress ashore; she turned back to face the boat, garments still dripping a thin thread of harbour-water.

“Produce your woman prisoner, master captain, and let us see whether she is a runaway wife or a lovesick girl mad after her sweetheart. Then I will deliver judgment, and as like as not surprise you all with my clemency. I am in a mood for tender romance to-day.”

The port-captain went into the little hutch of a cabin with a white face. Phorenice’s gaiety frightened him more than her rage. I saw him stoop, knife in hand, to cut a lashing.

“The man appears to be dead, Your Majesty,” he began. “I see that his wounds—”

“Bring out the woman, you fool. I asked for her. Keep your carrion where it is.”

He obeyed. And when he brought her out into the daylight, the harbour’s stink and the salt wind did not disguise her.

It was the girl I had saved from the cave-tigers in the circus—the one who afterward, in those hours of strange companionship, had drawn my thoughts as steadily as hunger draws a man toward bread. The sight of her struck like a remembered word in a dead dialect: not merely sound, but the whole taste of what it once meant.

Phorenice recognised her as well. She did not hide it. She spoke her name aloud and, with a cruel brightness, began to mock her—asking after the rebels’ ménage, and the success of the prisoner’s amours.

So the mightiest woman in Atlantis chose to be jealous of a captive.

The moment the words were out of her mouth my mind went soldier-quick, as it has been trained to do when the world tilts and you must catch your footing or be dashed under. I had come back to this doomed splendour to spend myself for Atlantis because the High Council commanded it; and if sacrifice were demanded, then—Gods forgive me—Nais might be counted among the offerings. Yet even as that bitter obedience hardened in me, another thought leapt beside it: a narrow path by which she might still be spared.

I bowed with the gravity Phorenice expected, and answered in the measured tones one uses to keep a blade from flashing too soon. “In this, and in all other things where a mere human hand is potent, I will carry out your wishes, Phorenice.”

Her fingers—cool, light, possessive—rested a moment on my arm, and her eyes, those deep wells that could look tender while plotting, brimmed with fresh feeling as if I had given her a gift. The Gods won her half her battles in the curve of her mouth and the music of her voice; it was a weapon as sharp as any sword.

Around the royal quay the neighbourhood had gathered: merchants, porters, waterside folk, all quick to offer a change of clothing, a doorway, a bath, a seat by a brazier. The basin into which the mammoth had flung us was foul enough—stale fish brine, bilge slop, rotting weed—so that even I, who can ignore wet wool when necessary, was glad to be rid of it. Phorenice, gracious as a queen at festival, accepted help as if it were tribute. She said little of Nais, but she did not forget her; before she allowed herself to be led away she gave the port-captain strict orders for guarding his prisoner, with the sweetness of a woman choosing a ribbon.

In the stone house they gave me a bath. The water steamed; it smelled faintly of crushed bay and ash-lye, and when the filth of the harbour finally slid off my skin it felt as if I had peeled away a whole layer of the city’s decay. I took the plainest garment my host owned—coarse-woven, undyed, honest cloth—and he was so relieved to be “robbed” cheaply that his thanks were almost fervent.

Afterward I spent an hour on the pavement outside, listening to the distant din of bombardment: a dull, recurrent thump like a giant pounding bread, the air shivering after each blow; somewhere nearer a tower bell rang in a nervous, scattered way, the sound swallowed and spat back by alleys. Even before I saw her again, I could hear Atlantis’s soundscape—its old choruses of oars, market cries, temple flutes—being drowned under war’s iron percussion.

When Phorenice came out, she was not merely dry and changed—she was adorned. Behind her shuffled the merchant, and it took no priestly insight to read ruin on his face. He had produced a store of jewels and gauds, much of it held in partnership, and Phorenice had taken what pleased her eye as simply as a child scoops honey.

“Are they not pretty?” she asked, turning her wrists so the sun struck sparks from bracelets and rings. “See how they flash. I am quite glad now, Deucalion, that the mammoth gave us that furious ride and that spill, since it has brought me such a bonny present. You may tell the fellow here that some day, when he has earned some more, I will come and be his guest again.”

Litters waited. She tilted her head toward them like a woman choosing which dish to taste first.

“Send one away,” she said, “and do you share mine with me, sir. We must play at being lovers to-day, even if love is a matter which will come to us both with more certainty to-morrow. No; do not order more bearers. My own slaves will carry us handily enough. I am glad you are not one of your gross overfed men, Deucalion. I am small and slim myself, and I do not want to be husbanded by a man who will overshadow me.”

“Back to the royal pyramid?” I asked.

“No—nor to the walls. I neither wish to fight nor to sit as Empress to-day. It is my whim to be Phorenice, the maiden, for a few hours; and if some one I wot of would woo me now as other maidens are wooed, I should esteem it a luxury. Bid the slaves carry us round the harbour’s rim, and give word to these starers that if they follow I will call down fire upon them as I did upon the sacrifice.”

I had already seen enough of the city’s unruliness to know that a harbour-side holiday was a risky whim, but she knew Atlantis’s pulse better than any man living and would not be warned. I dismissed the guard that had come up. I drove back the gaping knot of sightseers—those silly creatures who must stare at power as if it were a painted idol—and took my place beside her as she bade. The bearers dipped their heads to the pole, and we swung away.

We moved along the wide pavement between merchants’ houses and the dimpling water. The harbour smelled of tar and salt and old fish; once, in happier generations, those odours had meant wealth and movement and full bellies. Now they carried another note: emptiness. Too few ships lay on that splendid basin. Too few hands shouted over cargo. The quays looked like a feast-table after the guests have fled—plenty of stone and space, but no laughter, no clatter, no comforting rhythm of work.

In old days—when oar and sail did most of the world’s travelling and sun-drawn machines were only a clever aid—trade had roared. Ships had lain so close that many waited outside in the estuary, and memorials had been sent to the King to double the port. A voyage to Egypt and back took a year then, and of three ships that set out, one at least could be counted on to perish to deep water and the beasts that own it. Yet commerce had been alive, growing. Merchants had grown portly and pious; they had given generously to the Gods and to their neighbours’ tables.

Now all Atlantis seemed in arms, and chaffering was called vulgar. A merchant with substance forgot his ledgers, curled his beard after court fashion, and practiced the empty arts of banquet and fine sewing. Where his father had spent anxious days with cargo tally and ship-master, the son spent his nights among perfumed lamps, breathing spiced meats he did not earn and listening to musicians paid to drown the city’s hungry cough.

Those smaller merchants without savings were crushed by taxes and constant war-bickering. Markets vanished as districts rose and fell in rebellion. Nothing new was made that could be carried abroad: a weaver pressed into tending a fire-tube makes no cloth, and a farmer playing at revolt does not buy slaves to till his fields. They told me that not long before my return a cargo of slaves—fine bodies, doomed as always—had been brought into harbour; and there had been nothing for it but to set them ashore across the estuary and leave them free to starve or survive in the wild ground as they could. No man in Atlantis would take even one more slave, not as gift, not at any price. The very appetite for ownership had sickened.

All this grieved me, but remedy would be for afterward. It would only sour the hour to speak of it in the litter beside Phorenice. I knew her moods well enough to keep silence. And I think she, too, had learned to read my face; between her bursts of trifling she tried to show me wonders, as one offers a dish with a brighter sauce when one sees a guest has lost his taste.

We went out to where the westernmost harbour wall joined the land. The bearers, sweating, set us down. Phorenice led me toward a small stone house built alone on a promontory where tide ran constantly, whispering and sucking against the rock in a steady breath—so different from the harsh metallic uproar of the city’s engines and war-horns. Admission took much unbarring. Inside she showed me what she called her gold collectors.

In the dry learning of the Sacred Mountain, we had been taught that gold lies dissolved in sea-water. It was one of those facts filed away like a forgotten herb in a scholar’s cupboard: interesting, inert, seldom useful. Of any practical method to coax it into yellow hardness, none had ever been discussed. Yet here, this field-reared upstart Empress had found a trick as though a household recipe had been whispered to her over the kneading-trough.

She laughed, patting my arm while I stared. “I tell all others in Atlantis that only the Gods have this secret,” she said, “and that They gave it to me as one of themselves. But I am no Goddess to you, am I, Deucalion? And by my face, I have no other explanation of how this plan was invented. We’ll suppose I must have dreamed it.”

She pointed, and I leaned close. Sea-water sluiced in through a culvert and flowed over rough metal plates set in the floor, their surfaces ridged like a rasp. The stream ran out again on its natural course, never stopping, never tiring. In the grooves, caught like pollen in a comb, were yellow flecks—gold, stubbornly real. The air smelled of wet stone and salt; deeper in, a fire burned where her men melted the gathered grains into bars, and the smoke had that sharp, bitter tang of hot metal.

“You see it caught in the ridges?” she said. “That is gold. My fellows melt it with fire into bars and take it to my smith’s in the city. The tides vary constantly, as you priests know well, as the quiet moon draws them. It does not take much figuring to know how much sea passes through these culverts in a month, and how much gold to a grain should be caught in the plates. My fellows here at first thought to cheat me, but I towed two of them in the water once behind a galley till the cannibal fish ate them, and since then the others have given me credit for—for what do you think?”

“More divinity,” I said.

“I suppose it is that. But I am letting you see how it is done. Just have the head to work out a little sum, and see what an effect can be gained. You will be a God yet yourself, Deucalion, with these silly Atlanteans, if only you will use your wit and cleverness.”

Was she mocking me, or teaching me? With Phorenice it was hard to tell. One moment she set all down to calculation and brilliance; the next she offered some impossible tale and dared you to contradict her. She wrapped her power in myth as neatly as she wrapped herself in stolen jewels, and the crowd—hungry for wonders—chose to be dazzled.

The longer I stood beside her, the more she mystified me; and the more despairing I grew that Atlantis could ever be coaxed back to peace and the ancient Gods even after I had done what the Council commanded and taken this woman to wife. Sometimes one plan seemed brutally simple: curb her mischief by death and let the country recover in its own slow, natural way, like a field left fallow after a bitter crop. A dagger-stroke—done. Yet the idea sickened me. Each time that thought rose, I clung to the answer that if it were fitting, the High Gods in Their wisdom would have given me explicit command.

Still, such was the fascination of Phorenice that when we left her gold collectors and walked straight into peril where a withheld hand might have delivered her neatly to the nether Gods, I found myself fighting for her when she called—fighting as I have seldom fought. Some of that was the old lust of battle that wakes even in cold men when cries rise and steel begins to sing. But some of it—no use denying—was the sheer pleasure of protecting her, and the distaste of seeing her dragged down by rude, uncouth fishers, smelling of old nets and rancid oil, who would paw at her jewels as if they were fish-guts on a board.

The cause lay in the city’s unrest and street violence, and in the desperate poverty that drives people to risk anything for the price of a meal. We had mounted the litter again; the bearers swung us forward at their steady trot. Phorenice was talking, lightly as if discussing recipes for a feast, about her new supplies of gold. She had made fresh sumptuary laws.

“In the old days,” she said, “when yellow gold was tediously dredged grain by grain from river gravels in dangerous lands, a quill full would cost a rich man’s savings. So none but those whose high station fitted them to be so adorned could wear golden ornaments. But when the sea-water gave me gold here by the double handful a day, I found the price of those river hoards decreased, and one day—could you credit it?—a common fellow, one of my smiths, came to me wearing a collar of yellow gold on his own common neck. Well, I had that neck divided, as payment for his presumption. And as I promised to repeat the division promptly on all other offenders, that special species of forwardness seems checked for the time. There are many exasperations, Deucalion, in governing these common people.”

She would have gone on, but I saw two clumsy fisher boats paddling toward us over the ripples, hide-covered, low in the water; and at the same time, among the narrow lanes between the houses on the landward side, savage-faced men began to run after the litter in threatening clusters. Their feet slapped the dust; their voices rose in a ragged chorus, harsh as gulls fighting over offal.

“With permission,” I said, “I will step out and scatter this rabble.”

“Oh, the people always cluster round me,” she replied, amused. “Poor ugly souls—they take a strange delight in coming to stare at my pretty looks. But scatter them. I have said I did not wish to be followed. I am taking holiday now, Deucalion, am I not, whilst you learn to woo me?”

I stepped down. The fishers in the boats were already shouting to those darting among the huts to see we did not escape, and more bodies poured out every moment. We had passed beyond the merchants’ quarter and were level with the mud-and-grass huts the fishing folk deem sufficient shelter. Even in the old King’s peaceful days this part of the city was seldom without its weekly riot; now it was tinder-dry.

A fisherman’s life is hard beyond most. Wind and wave batter them; firestones from the sky can pelt them; great beasts in the sea hunt them with savage persistence. It is rare that some man of their guild does not fail to come home for tally. And their manner of fishing is itself a gamble.

Each man keeps a large sea-fowl taken as a nestling, trained to dive. A bronze ring is set round its neck to prevent it swallowing the catch; for each fish it brings to the boat, head and tail and inwards are given as reward, the ring removed while it eats. The birds are faithful once trained, seldom deserting their owners; yet their lives are as precarious as the men’s. Bigger fish and beasts take them as easily as they take the smaller prey.

Worse still: if a fisher’s bird is slain, and the second he has in training also dies, he is suddenly bereft of livelihood. The guild will not help. They say the sea Gods have judged him displeasing and robbed him of his birds; to aid him would be impious. So he may starve or rob as he chooses. That belief, hammered into them with hunger, makes them desperate men; they spend a month’s gains on one night’s debauch because tomorrow might be death. Their one point of honour is not to be curbed by ordinary fear of consequences. Their speech is harsh, and in it—half sea-slang, half old dialect—there are words for winds, currents, fish-fat, gutting, bait and brine that no priest in the upland groves can translate without losing their taste. It is a language born of salt and danger; and yet those same mouths were now filled with hunger’s simplest lexicon: give.

I went quickly toward the largest knot of them skulking behind the houses. The litter halted behind me.

“Disperse,” I ordered. “For employment, put your houses in order and clean the fish offal from the lanes between them. To-morrow I will come to inspect and put this quarter into better order. But for to-day the Empress—whose name be adored—wishes privacy. Cease your staring.”

“Then give us money,” piped a shrill voice from among the huts.

“I will send you a torch in an hour’s time,” I said grimly, “and rig you a gallows, if you give me more annoyance. To your kennels, you.”

They would have obeyed—authority still had a taste they remembered, bitter but familiar. There was a stir; those in sunlight slid instinctively into shadow; many dodged into houses and crouched in dark corners. But the men in the two boats called them back with boisterous cries, and the crowd’s courage thickened like bad stew when more fat is thrown in.

I signed to the litter-bearers to move on quickly. There was need for discipline, and I meant to deal it out myself. If Phorenice, glittering and jeweled, remained in sight, she was bait for robbery; if she were gone, I could cow the rest more cleanly.

But I had been presumptuous. One does not give orders about Phorenice’s person and expect obedience. She bade the bearers stand. She stepped out and drew weapons from beneath the cushions: a sword she strapped to her hip, and on her left forearm she set a well-dinted target of gold. She came toward me laughing.

“An unfair trick,” she cried. “If you keep a fight to yourself now, Deucalion, where will your greediness carry you when I am your shrinking, wistful little wife? Are these fools truly going to stand up against us?”

I had not coveted a fight, but it had chosen us. The robe and glittering gauds she had stripped from the merchant drew eyes like flies to sweet. The fishers paddled their boats into the surf and leaped out, leaving the frail basket-work hulls to be spewed up or smashed as chance ordered. From huts and filthy lanes poured more—women with the men—closing in until their bodies made a wall.

“Have a care,” shouted one on the outskirts. “She called down fire for the sacrifice once to-day, and she can burn up others here if she chooses.”

“So much the more for those that are left,” another jeered. “She cannot burn all.”

“Nay, I will not burn any,” Phorenice said, bright with malice, “but you shall look upon my sword-play till you are tired.”

I heard and understood her cruelty. I was one of the Seven; I knew how she had summoned the sky-fire to take that cloven-hoofed horse offered in sacrifice—and I knew, too, that she could summon no such fire here. She was feeding them myth as one feeds a starving crowd thin broth: it warms, it deceives, it keeps them obedient for a moment. Yet they gave us no time for argument. They feared an escort might be near; whatever they stole, they must steal quickly.

They surged in. The reek of them—sweat turned sour, fish slime, rancid oil, old smoke—made the throat catch. A grimy hand shot out to seize the jewels on Phorenice’s breast, and I lopped it off at the elbow. It fell at her feet with a wet sound, and the fight began.

“Your back to mine, comrade!” she cried, laughing, and drew and laid about her with fine dexterity.

It was slaughter, that first bout. Their greed jammed them too close; they had no room to draw back an elbow for proper thrust. We kept a circle by sheer whirling of steel. In such bouts you must work cleanly: a wounded man on the ground, half-seen, is as dangerous as a snake. As we circled I noted that all of Phorenice’s quarry lay still. By the Gods, she could play a sword. She touched life with every thrust.

The throne of Atlantis had not been won by face and tongue alone. Seeing her fight did not lessen her in my eyes as a fighter; it only made her more terrible. I caught glimpses of her out of the corner of my eye—small, quick, splendid in stolen gold—while I dealt with the press before me. The fishers had little knowledge of fence; their weapons were clumsy. But they had numbers and savage ferocity, and they believed they could pull us down by sheer hunger. More than once I felt bodies catch at my arms and shoulders like nets, and if I had not contrived to wrench a shield from a fool who came too proudly near, I cannot swear they would not have dragged me down.

Through the burly uproar—grunts, shrieks, the wet smack of blade into flesh, the scuffle of feet in filth—came very pleasantly, absurdly, Phorenice’s cry of “Deucalion!” which she chose as her battle shout. It rose above the din like a bell rung in a riot. I knew she was a past-mistress of compliment; I had heard my name roared over battle before. But here it was under peculiar circumstances, and for the life of me I could not help being tickled by the flattery.

Condemn my weakness if you will: I came very near then to liking the Empress in the way she wished. And as for the other woman who should have filled my mind, I confess the fury of the moment drove Nais and her strait clean beyond the marches of memory. Of such frail stuff are we made, even those of us who think ourselves strongest.

There is a temptation few born to the sword can resist: to give yourself to fight for fight’s own sake, to let the body answer the music of steel. Women, it seems, can be bitten by the same fierce infection. The attack slackened. We stood in the middle of a ring of twisted dead; the rest shrank back out of reach.

It was the moment for truce, the moment for a few strong words to send them cowering to their huts and give us passage. But Phorenice, drunk on her own skill, could not leave it there. She began to sing a hymn to our invulnerability, gloating over our feats; then she demanded payment for the litter-bearers they had killed; then she spoke, with bright relish, of the burnings and skinnings and sawings asunder with which this quarter would be treated soon, until they learned deportment and genteel manners.

“It makes your backs creep, does it?” she taunted. “I do not wonder. This severity must have its unpleasant side. But why do you not put it beyond my power to give the order? Either you must think yourselves Gods or me no Goddess, or you would not have gone on so far. Come now, you nasty-smelling people—follow out your theory. And if you make a good fight of it, I swear by my face I will be lenient with those who do not fall.”

Our Lord the Sun slid down toward the rim of His day, and the breath of a burning mountain unrolled before Him like a black banner. The air tasted of cinders and salt. Phorenice wrung her soaked tunic with quick, impatient hands; the water ran from the cloth in little streams, and she shivered as the evening cool crept up out of the lagoon.

“Work those paddles, Deucalion,” she said, her voice sharp as the snap of a knife on bone. “Take me in through the water-gate and let me be restored to comfort. That merchant would howl to see what his pretty garments have come to—and I howl too, being a woman and knowing he has no others I may seize in place of these.” She tossed back her short red hair, wet curls clinging to her brow. “What think you of my wisdom in coming where we came without escort?”

“The Empress can do no wrong,” I said, giving her the old answer with a smile.

“At least I have shown you I can fight.” Her eyes cut sidelong to me, bright as a coal in ash. “I caught you looking your approval once or twice. You were a difficult man to thaw, Deucalion, but you warm perceptibly the longer you are near me. La, sir, we shall be rustic sweethearts yet, if this goes on. I am glad I thought of the device of going near those smelly fishers.”

So the excursion, the lack of guards, the very choosing of that reeking strip of shore where fish-guts lay in windrows—this had been her bait, set not for rebels alone but for me: a fight to watch how I stood in it, and a chance to display her own skill as one sets a blade in lamplight to show its temper. If we must live together as husband and wife, it was well each should measure the other’s courage and steadiness.

Yet I could not keep my thoughts from straying to another woman, and to another choice made with a similar opening. Nais, with pity for squalid folk who did not even call her chieftain, had robbed me of struggle. Phorenice here had thrown away two score of hardy subjects for the mere pleasure of proving herself and pricking at my regard.

But I was bound by vow and council, and I forced my mind back to duty. As I rowed us over the heaving swells toward the forts which guarded the harbour mouth, I sent prayers up to the High Gods for dexterity of speech. They granted it. Words came smooth as oil, and Phorenice drank them with glowing eyes, as if my talk were some spiced broth warming her from the inside.

The fishers’ beach was safe from ships beyond, for a chain of jagged rocks ran from harbour side to the city wall like a saw laid flat beneath the waves. The fishers knew the passes; they slipped through, and if they misjudged, they leapt out and bore their light boats over the stones in their hands until the water took them again. But I had neither their knowledge nor their nimbleness. I felt the reef waiting—patient as a trap.

Now, I thought, the High Gods will show plainly whether They mean this woman—who has defied Them—to reign on; and whether They mean me to serve as her husband.

I held the prayer tight in my heart and waited for the omen.

It came without half-measure. A great wave rose behind us in the lagoon, not born of wind but of earth—a surge that could only have been sent by tremor. On its sleek back we were hurled forward, flung clean through the barrier where seaweed licked and combed the stones, and we passed without so much as scraping a plank.

I bowed my head and rowed on. Not yet, then, would vengeance fall.

The sentries in the two forts beat drums at each other in their accustomed calls, a steady pattern that stitched the evening together. In the thickening dusk they would have given little heed to a fishing-boat rattling at the great chain and shouting for passage. But two officers were taking the air atop the nearer fort, in a turret where stones were kept ready to drop on attackers, and they caught the tone of our voices. The drums were silenced; torches dipped down on cords until their smoke and resinous stink came to us with the glow, and faces were made certain. Then with a splash the chain dropped, and we slid through.

Inside lay a galley nosing the harbour wall. A rope ladder was thrown down from the fort roof, and men came clambering to take the oars. The officers sent gifts too—changes of raiment—plain, serviceable cloth that smelled of storage and old cedar rather than any palace perfume. In the cramped cabin we stripped off our sodden finery, the wet cloth sucking at skin like a leech. Fevers breed in damp after sunset; though I knew drugs that could ward them, I noted with grim amusement how the Empress—Goddess in proclamation—feared the small, mortal coughs and chills of common flesh.

The galley cut swiftly across the calm inner water and made fast to the gold rings on the royal quay. We waited for litters. While we waited, I watched a lantern glow in the guard-boat near Phorenice’s mammoth. The huge red beast stood shoulder-deep in the harbour, trunk curled upward, tame now, sending ripples out with each slow tremble. The light made its wet hide shine like fresh blood on stone.

I did not ask mercy for it. If the harbour mud swallowed it, I could have borne that loss with an easy mind.

To tell truth, the ride on its back had left me with a helplessness that scraped at pride. I have walked on my own feet across four continents; on my own feet I can guard my head and neck and set my weight where I choose. On the back of that detestable beast—pah!—I was as nervous as a child, perched high and powerless, tossed by each lurch like a sack of grain.

But there was no leisure for such private loathing. Phorenice, while we waited, turned on the port-captain—who came bustling up with officious salutations—and asked after Nais. He told her the girl had been clapped into a dungeon beneath the royal pyramid, and the officer of the guard had given bond for her safe-keeping.

“It is to be hoped he understands his work,” Phorenice said. “That pretty Nais knows the pyramid better than most. He may find himself sent to the tormentors for choosing a cell with a secret outlet. You would feel pleasure if the girl escaped, Deucalion?”

“Assuredly,” I said, for there was no profit in pretending. “I have no enmity against Nais.”

“But I have,” she snapped, and the small cruelty in her made itself plain, “and I am still minded to lock your faith to me by that wedding gift you know of.”

“The thing shall be done,” I said. “Before all, the Empress of Atlantis.”

“Poof! You are too stiff and formal.” She thrust her hand at me for the litter, and when I helped her in, she tugged me beside her. “Come in, keep me warm. Ho! you guards with the torches—keep farther back against the street walls. The perfume you burn stifles me.”

Again there was a feast in the royal banqueting-hall; again I sat beside Phorenice on the raised dais beneath the snake and the outstretched hand. This time what had been assumed before was proclaimed openly: Phorenice acknowledged me as her husband that was to be. The court—curled, jeweled, glittering like a heap of bright fish in a net—hailed me as greater than themselves by reason of her choosing.

Their homage was not the new, impertinent drinking of wine to my health. Rumour had reached them of my preference for older forms. So each lord and lady approached and, with decorum, spilled a goblet at my feet—wine dark as pomegranate syrup staining the stone. And when I called any up, man or woman, to receive tit-bits from my platter, they ate simply and thankfully instead of kissing the morsels or hiding them away as charms.

The flaring jets of earth-breath, hissing and glaring, showed me something else: a plainer habit of dress and a soberer mien among these thoughtless diners. No heavy clouds of scent fought the air tonight; the very hall sounded different without that languid courtly cooing that perfumes seem to invite. The scrape of sandals, the clink of metal, the low talk—each noise came clean, not muffled under sweet smoke.

Phorenice leaned close until the ruddy curls at her shoulder brushed my face. “Oh, sir,” she whispered, mock-pleading, “do not make your rule over us too ascetic. I have given no orders for this change, but tonight there are no perfumes in the air; the food is so plain I have half a mind to burn the cook; and as for the clothes and gauds of these diners—by my face!—they might have come straight from the old King’s reign before I stepped in here to show how tasteful could be colours on a robe, or how pretty the glint of a jewel. It’s done by no order of mine. They’ve swung round by sheer courtier instinct. Why, look at the beards of the men—there is not half the curl about them to-day that they showed yesterday. By my face! I believe they’d reap their chins as smooth as yours tomorrow if you go on setting fashions at this prodigious rate and I do not interfere.”

“Why hinder them if they feel more cleanly shaven?” I asked.

“No, sir. There shall be only one clean chin where a beard can grow in all Atlantis, and that shall be carried by the man who is husband to the Empress. Would you have no sumptuary laws?
