Would you have these folk here and the common people outside imitate us in every cut of hair and fold of garment we discover? If you and I chose to mark sovereignty only by superior strength of arm and wit, they would hate us for arrogance; but if we keep apart a few personal decorations, these become objects to admire and envy pleasantly.”

“You show me there is more in rule than meets the eye.”

“And yet they tell me—and show me—you have ruled with some success.”

“I employed the older method. It requires a Phorenice to invent these nicer flights.”

“Flatterer!” She smote my arm with the backs of her fingers. “You are becoming as great a courtier as any of them. You make me blush, Deucalion, and there is no fan-girl here tonight to cool my cheek. I must choose another. But it shall not be Ylga. Ylga seems to have more kindness for you than I like. If she is wise she will go live in her palace at the far side of the city and occupy herself with ordering slaves and making embroideries. I shall not be hard on Ylga unless she forces me, but I will have no woman in this kingdom treat you with undue civility.”

“And how am I to act,” I said, keeping to her mood, “when I see and hear all the men of Atlantis making their protestations before you? By your own confession they all love you as ardently as they loved you hopelessly.”

“You must not ask impossibilities. I have power if you will, but no force that governs hearts. Yet if you choose, make proclamation that I am given now body and inwards to you, and if they continue to offend your pride you may take culprits and give them to the tormentors. Indeed, Deucalion, I think it would be a pretty attention to me if you arranged such a ceremony. It seems to me,” she added with a frown, “that the jealousy is too much on one side.”

“You must not expect a man divorced from love for all a busy life to learn its niceties in an instant. I was proud of my progress. With any other schoolmistress than you, Phorenice, I should not be near so forward. Indeed—if one may judge by my past record—I should not have begun at all.”

“I suppose you think I should be satisfied? Well, I am not. I can be finely greedy over some matters.”

The banquet did not stretch to inordinate length. Phorenice had gone through much since last she slept; though she had declared herself Goddess, her body still dragged like any mortal’s. Black rings lay under her wondrous eyes. She sank back among the cushions, limbs slackened, the edge gone out of her bright cruelty for the moment. When dancers came and postured, she flung them a jewel and sent them away before they had given half their show. A poet—one of those swelling fellows who would have poured out an ocean of words—she would not hear at all.

“To-morrow,” she said. “But now grant me peace. My Lord Deucalion has given me much food for thought, and presently I go to my chamber to muse on the future policies of this State throughout the night. To-morrow come to me again, and if your poetry is good and short I will pay you surprisingly. But if there are superfluous words, I will pay you for those with the stick.”

She rose then. The hall murmured and bowed as one creature. I led her away from the banqueting-hall and down passages where secret doors lay cunningly masked in stone. She clung to my arm; once, while we waited for a great block to swing slowly aside, she drooped her head against my shoulder. Her breath came warm against my cheek. Her face, close at hand, was loveliness beyond the reach of speech.

I think she expected me to kiss her red lips held so near. Willing though I was to play the part appointed, I could not bring myself to it. When the stone at last moved, she drew away with a sigh, and we went on without further word.

“May the High Gods treat you tenderly,” I said when we reached her bed-chamber door.

“I am my own God,” she said, “in all things but one. By my face! you are a tardy wooer, Deucalion. Where go you now?”

“To my own chamber.”

“Oh, go then. Go.”

“Is there anything more I could do?”

“Nothing your wit or your will would prompt you to. Yes, you are finely decorous in your old-fashioned way, but you are a mighty poor wooer. Don’t you know a woman esteems some things more highly if they are taken from her by rude force?”

“It seems I know little enough about women.”

“You never said a truer word. Bah! And I believe your coldness brings you more benefit in a certain matter than any show of passion could earn. There—get you gone, if the atmosphere of a maiden’s bed-chamber hurts your rustic modesty, and your Gods keep you—if that’s the phrase, and if you think They can do it. Get you gone, man, and leave me solitary.”

I had taken the plan of the pyramid out of the archives before the banquet and learned it to the last turn and blind corner. I was wearied as a man is wearied who has been kept taut too long, but I dared not spend time on sleep. Nais must be sacrificed in part for Atlantis; yet a plan had come to me by which she need not be sacrificed wholly. To carry it through needed speed and exactness while night still held.

Help came from a quarter I did not expect.

As I threaded the angular maze between the ponderous stones toward the apartments Phorenice had assigned me, a woman glided from a side passage like smoke slipping from a crack. I lifted my hand-lamp. The light caught her face: Ylga.

She looked at me half-sullenly. “I have lost my place,” she said. “It seems I need never have spoken. She meant to have you all along, and no warning of mine could turn her. And you—you think me officious, if you have given me a thought at all since.”

“I never forget a kindness.”

“Oh, you will learn that trick soon enough. And you are going to marry her—you!” Bitterness pinched her words. “The city is ringing with it. I thought at least you were honest, but when a high place can be got by taking a woman with it, you are like the rest. I thought too you would be one of those men who distrust ruddy hair. And besides—she is little.”

“Ylga,” I said quietly, “you have taught me these walls are full of crannies and ears. I will listen to no word against Phorenice. But I would have further converse with you soon. If you still have a kindness for me, go to the chamber that is mine and wait there. I will join you shortly.”

Her eyes dropped. “What do you want of me, Deucalion?”

“I want to say something. You will learn who it concerns later.”

“But is it—is it fitting for a maiden to come to a man’s room at this hour?”

“I know little of your conventions in this new Atlantis. I am Deucalion, girl, and if you still have qualms remembering that, do not come.”

She looked up with a sneer. “I was foolish. My lord’s coldness has grown into a proverb, and I should have remembered it. Yes. I will come.”

“Go now, then,” I said.

I waited until her steps had faded and the passages held only the low, living sounds of stone—distant water seeping, a faint draught moaning through unseen joints. With Ylga’s help my tasks would be lightened, but first I must do the work that required no witness.

I made my way, with as little delay and show as possible, toward a certain sanctuary within the temple of our Lady the Moon. Here my knowledge as one of the Seven served me well.

All the temples of Atlantis lay in immediate and secret connection with the royal pyramid. The ways were little used; only the Seven knew them, and the Three above them—if, indeed, there lived at one time three men learned enough in the highest mysteries to stand in that sublime degree. Even then the secret alleys might be trodden only at moments of greatest stress, so that a full generation might pass without a human foot stirring the dust there.

With no little groping and experiment I found the hidden entrance; but once inside, memory guided me. Though I had never walked it, its windings had been taught at my initiation. I went quickly. The air in those deep cracks of stone held lurking evils—stale damp, a sourness like old breath trapped in cloth. And the hours were fleeing. Much must be done before our Lord the Sun rose again.

I came to the spy-place that commanded the temple. The holy space beyond lay empty, dust-covered, as if the very sounds of prayer had died out and left only silence to settle. A vast stone in the wall swung outward and gave me entrance. After the solemn prayer required before such deeds, I took the metal stair from where it is kept, set it firm, and climbed to the lap of the Goddess. Then, pulling the stair after me, I climbed again until my length lay against her calm, mysterious face.

A shiver seized me. Even a warrior hardened to ugly sights may quail when called to juggle with life and death, with years and history, holding the welfare of a country in one hand and the future of a woman who is as life to him in the other. But the hours flew. I laid hold of the jewel studded in the forehead of the image with one hand, and with the other thrust at a corner of the eyebrow.

With a faint creak the massive eyeball below—a stone I could barely have covered with my back—swung inward. I stepped off the stair, climbed into the gap, and found myself in the chamber hollowed from the head of the Goddess.

It was the first time my eyes had seen this most secret place. Yet its aspect was familiar from teaching, and I knew where to find what I needed. Even so, I could not help a moment’s wonder at the craft of its concealment.

High as I was in priestly learning, the structure of what I had come to fetch was hidden from me. I knew only power and effect. There, in the hidden niche, lay roughly rounded balls like nut-kernels, grass-green, hard as bees’ wax. Three of them. I took the one I needed and left the others concealed as I found them. Whether it was drug or something more, I did not know. But I was sure of what it would do, for that was set forth plainly in the teaching. I slipped it into a pouch of my garment, returned by the way I had come, and replaced all things in due order.

Before leaving the temple I looked once more at the image of the Goddess. The jet of earth-breath burned eternally from the central altar, hissing as it flared, lighting her from head to toe and throwing sparks from the great jewel in her forehead. Vast she was, calm beyond human imagining—a symbol of rest and quietness, that deep hush people hunger for and rarely find in this rude world, unless by piety they earn a place where our Lady the Moon and the High Ones reign in eternal majesty.

With tired, dragging limbs I made my way back to the royal pyramid and at last reached my own private chamber.

Ylga awaited me there, though at first I did not see her. The suspicions of these days had bitten deep; she crouched in hiding until she was sure it was I who entered, and that I came alone.

“Oh, frown at me if you choose,” she said sullenly, coming out at last. “I am past caring for your good opinion. I had heard so much of Deucalion, and thought I read honesty in you when first you came ashore. But now I know you are no better than the rest. Phorenice offers you a high place, and you marry her blithely to get it. And why should you not? People say she is pretty, and I know she can be warm. I have seen her warm and languishing to scores of men. She is clever too with her eyes, our great Empress. And as for you—it tickles you to be courted.”

“I think you are a very silly woman,” I said.

“If you flatter yourself it matters a rap to me whom you marry, you are letting conceit run away with you.”

“Listen,” I said. “I did not ask you here to make foolish speeches which are largely beyond my comprehension. I asked you to help me do a service to one of your own blood-kin.”

She stared, the sulk in her face shifting into wonder. “I do not understand.”

“It rests largely with you,” I said, “whether Nais dies to-morrow, or whether she is thrown into a sleep from which she may waken on some later and more happy day.”

Phorenice’s wishes did not linger in Atlantis like incense that might be allowed to fade. They struck, and the city moved.

Her edict went out for the burying alive of Nais, and though the words named me as builder of the throne of stone, everyone understood what that meant in practice: I would stand as patron and symbol, while a whole hive of hands did the lifting, chiselling, hauling, and dying.

Heralds cried the proclamation in every ward, their voices bouncing off pyramid-flanks and colonnades, their brass trumpets cutting through the morning’s softer sounds—the cluck of courtyard fowl, the splash of women rinsing grain, the low patient groan of donkeys under panniers. At once the guilds poured toward the square: masons with chalk-dusted beards, stonecutters with mallets slung like butchers’ cleavers, engineers smelling of hot metal and oil, architects with tablets and plumb-lines, sculptors with their fine blades wrapped in cloth. They came as if summoned to a feast—only this feast would be of granite and blood.

Any block they sealed for use was seized without coin or apology. The chosen stones were monstrous, and to bring them through the streets the guilds tore down whatever stood in the way—shops where spices had hung in fragrant braids, a bakery whose oven still breathed yesterday’s barley-sweet warmth, a row of poor dwellings where the cooking stones were scarcely cooled. The city’s domestic life—pots, hearths, pounding-stones, jars of preserved fruit—was shoved aside so the Empress’s stone could pass.

All this while the rebels’ engines still battered at the walls. Yet in Atlantis the mania for building did not pause for war. The guilds kept to their craft as if stone could drown out danger, as if architecture were a prayer louder than fear. They could not lift the impossible monoliths the early Gods had planted for the Sun’s sacred circle, but they had rams and cranes and wheeled trucks, and Phorenice’s new devices besides—metal that changed with heat and cold, machinery that made the city’s old acoustic ecology into something harsher. Where once you heard wind in palms and the measured chanting of temple choirs, now the square rang all night with hammer-blows, whip-cracks, the squeal of rollers, the groan of strained ropes, the sharp calls of foremen. The noise lay on the mind like grit, wearing it down.

The throne rose in the open square before the royal pyramid. Seven tiers formed its base, each knee-high, each front cut with three steps, like a ritual staircase for a god. At the topmost layer, the builders gouged a cavity—an awful pantry, a sealed larder—not for grain or cured meat, but for a living woman. Above it the huge seat-stone hung poised, held aloft by the heated rams that would, by slow contraction, settle it into place.

By torchlight, relay after relay worked through the night. Men sweated till their skins shone black in the flame-glow; some fainted and were dragged aside; some slipped and broke; some were crushed. Others stepped into their places at once. The whips did not cease. Phorenice had spoken; the city obeyed.

At last, as dawn cooled the stones and the square began to fill with people come to stare, the chippings were cleared away, the scaffolding pulled down, and along with it the bodies of half a score who had died from accident or exhaustion. The throne stood finished in its lower courses: carvings crisp as knife-work, the royal serpent coiling around the chair, its hooded head poised above like a shadow that would never lift. The upper block still hung on the rams, ready for the final act that everyone pretended was justice and everyone knew was spectacle.

In the cool of the next morning, the chamberlains summoned me from the royal pyramid. I stepped out into a world already arranged for pageantry. Each great man announced himself with banner-bearers and trumpet-calls. The middle classes glittered in their bright garments. Even the poor—dusty, ribbed with hunger—had wrapped scraps of colour around their heads, as if a strip of dyed cloth could turn dread into holiday.

My own trumpets sounded. The crowd shouted welcome. Chamberlains in gorgeous robes walked backward before me, and I crossed to the scarlet awning prepared in my honour and sat upon the cushions beneath it.

Then Phorenice came, fresh from sleep and made magnificent by it. They bore her in an open litter of gold and ivory, carried by those European savages she favoured—massive, scarred men whose brutishness threw her refinement into sharper brilliance. The crowd drew a single deep breath when they first saw her, the way a kitchen draws breath when a new spice is unstoppered. There was not a man in that throng who did not envy me, nor a soul who could imagine I was not drunk with gratitude at being chosen.

I had to summon all the iron I owned to keep my face still. This was ceremony, and in Atlantis ceremony mattered—older than any private grief. I meant Phorenice to see nothing to cavil at. My plan—my necessity—could not afford a trembling hand or a twitching cheek. When the time came, I told myself, I would place my love into that hollow as calmly as a priest lays a stranger into a tomb, and she would give no sign that betrayed what lay between us. With her, too, it was always, unbreakably, before all Atlantis.

If there was mercy anywhere in that morning, it was that no one treated the rite as a joke. The whole proceeding moved with a strict solemnity. Phorenice, for all her shifting moods, could be rigid in old ritual when it pleased her, and she had commanded pomp and precision in the ancient manner.

They carried her to the scarlet awning. I handed her down. She sat, beckoned me to her side, and intertwined her fingers with mine as rulers and consorts have always done, binding the public image together like cords on a ceremonial roast.

A body of soldiery marched up, opened out, and showed Nais among them.

They had clamped a metal collar around her neck with chains descending from it, held tight by two guards so she could not fling herself on a spear and steal a quick death. Yet she still wore her clothing. That small decency mattered. In Atlantis it was common for soldiers to strip the condemned and divide the garments like kitchen-scraps after a banquet. Someone had paid these guards to spare her that. I thought of Ylga—veiled somewhere, perhaps—and felt gratitude like a warm taste in the mouth amid bitterness.

The soldiers halted at the edge of the pavilion’s shade. Nais was ordered to prostrate herself, and she did so with wisdom, sparing herself the roughness of forced handling. Her face was pale, but there was neither fear nor defiance in it; her eyes were calm, as if she listened to something within herself that no one else could hear. She remembered what was due to Atlantis, and love and pride twisted in me like a knife.

Outwardly, I held myself like carved stone. Phorenice watched my face, and I let her see nothing but what I would have her see.

“Nais,” said the Empress, her voice carrying as cleanly as a bell across the square, “you have eaten from my platter when you were young and drank from my cup. I gave you what was yours. You should have learned gratitude—and knowledge—that the arm of the Empress is long and her hand heavy. Yet you have tried to take what I have set apart for myself. You were offered pardon on terms. You rejected it. Foolish. But to-day I am inclined to clemency. Presently, seated on that carved throne of granite, I shall take my Lord Deucalion to husband. Give me a plain word that you are sorry, girl, and name a man you would choose. I will remember the brightness of this occasion. You shall be pardoned and wed before we rise from these cushions.”

“I will not wed,” Nais said quietly.

Phorenice’s lips tightened, not quite into cruelty, but into the neatness of a blade being honed. “Think for the last time of what is the other choice. You will be taken warm and quick and beautiful as you stand there now, and laid in the hollow beneath the throne-stone. Deucalion—who is to be my husband—will lay you in that bed as a symbol that so shall perish all my enemies. Then he will release the rams and lower the upper stone into place, and the world shall see your face no more. Look at the bright sky, Nais. Fill your chest with the sweet warm air. Then think what this death will mean. Believe me, I do not wish to make you an example unless you force me.”

“I will not wed,” said the prisoner, in the same unbroken tone.

The Empress released my hand and lay back against the cushions. “If she presumes on old familiarity, or thinks that I jest, show her now, Deucalion, that I do not.”

I rose as though rising for a duty no more intimate than inspecting a granary. “The Empress is far from jesting,” I said. “I will do this thing because it is her wish, and because it is her command that a symbol of it remain for ever as an example. Lead your prisoner to the place.”

The soldiers wheeled. The guards tightened on the chains, ready to drag. But Nais walked willingly, colour unchanged, and I followed them after making obeisance to Phorenice.

Before ten thousand eyes, we showed no emotion. Not only for Atlantis’ sake, but because pride lived in both of us. We were not as Phorenice, who could flaunt endearments like jewels.

At the upper tier I ordered the collar removed. Then I clapped my arms around Nais with a roughness meant for witnesses, holding her as a captor holds his prey. The touch nearly unmanned me. I had never embraced a woman except with the arm of brutality; now I held the one I loved with tenderness so great it was like pain. Nais made no sign—no tremor, no glance that would betray us. She lay half-strangled in my grasp as any captive might, and the crowd below drank in the theatre.

We moved beneath the overhanging mass of the seat-stone. There, where Phorenice’s view did not reach, but where eyes on roofs and pyramid-sides could still see, I dragged Nais across the level and put her down into the hollow with deliberate brutality. She did not resist me even once. That, too, was courage.

To prevent the condemned from struggling up and being crushed—thus snatching an easier end—brazen clamps lay ready, fitted to grooves above the cavity. With one hand I held her down; with the other I fastened bar after bar. Each click of metal into stone sounded louder than any hymn. Sweat ran off me and spattered the thirsty granite. I could not stop that.

I locked the last two bars and lifted my hand away from her throat.

My fingers had left pale furrows in her skin like marks pressed into dough. I could not help looking at her face. She was stronger than I. She did not give me any last look. Her eyes stayed fixed on the cruel stone overhead, as if she studied it the way a housewife studies a ceiling beam for cracks before the rains. I left her then, because to linger would have broken me.

I stepped out from beneath the poised block and signalled the engineers. They drew away the fires that heated the rams. As the metal cooled and contracted, the vast throne-stone began to creep downward toward its bed.

Slowly. Too slowly. Every inch felt like a year.

I stood with a face as blank as an officer overseeing work done under his authority. I watched the descent without flinching until the stone settled with a final deep sound that rolled through the square and through the bones, a noise that seemed to swallow all other living sound—the crying of infants, the gossip of women, the very wing-beat of pigeons. The rams were removed. Then, by guild custom, the master architect came with plumb-line and level to test what calculation already assured.

It was useless formality, a performance for the middle-class to seem mysterious and impressive to the poor. Yet even that had to be endured. A man dragged around to verify the perfect level of the tomb where his love lies buried living may be forgiven bitterness.

I had gone up those steps full of grief, though outwardly unmoved. Coming down, I felt hollowed out, as if half my insides had been left behind in that cavity, and the space filled with an aching void. Still my face remained passive. Always, before private matters, stood the command of the High Council before the Ark of the Mysteries.

I returned to Phorenice and spoke the prescribed words that certified her wish had been carried out.

“Then now,” she said, “I will give myself to you as wife. We are not as others, you and I, Deucalion. There is law and form for common people; it is useless for our purposes. We will have neither priest nor scribe to join us. I am law in Atlantis, and you soon will be part of me. We will not be demeaned by profaner hands. We will make the ceremony for ourselves, with sufficient witnesses. Afterwards the record shall be cut deep in the granite throne and filled with gold, so it endures and remains bright always.”

“The Empress can do no wrong,” I said, and took her hand and helped her rise.

We walked from the awning into the harsh sunlight. She leaned on me, flushing, radiant. The people shouted, “A Goddess; our Goddess Phorenice.” For me they had no cheer. The grim set of my face frightened them, as if they sensed that beneath it something human had been sealed away like Nais.

We climbed the steps to the throne. I sat Phorenice in the royal seat beneath the serpent’s hooded head, and she drew me down beside her.

She raised a jewelled hand. Silence fell over the throng as if someone had closed a door on all sound.

“Hear me, O my people,” Phorenice proclaimed, “and hear me, O High Gods from whom I am come. I take this man Deucalion to be my husband, to share with me the prosperity of Atlantis and join me in guarding our great possession. May all our enemies perish as she is now perishing above whom we sit.” She put her arms around my neck and kissed me hotly on the mouth.

In turn I spoke the answering form, my voice steady by force alone: “Hear me, O most High Gods, whose servant I am, and hear me also, O ye people. I take this Empress, Phorenice, to wife, to help with her the prosperity of Atlantis, and join with her in guarding the welfare of that great possession. May all the enemies of this country perish as they have perished in the past.”

Then I kissed her—my first kiss given to any woman—while the taste of it turned to ash in my mouth because another mouth, the one I wanted, lay below stone.

But the rite demanded more.

“A woman is one, and man is one,” Phorenice said, taking up the ancient words, “but in marriage they merge, so that wife and husband are no more separate, but one conjointly. In token of this we will make the symbolic joining, that all may see and remember.” She drew her dagger and pricked the brawn of my forearm until a bead of blood rose. She pressed her red lips to it and took it into herself.

“Ah,” she breathed, eyes bright. “Now you are part of me indeed, Deucalion, and I feel you have strengthened me already. Let me make you my return.”

She pulled down the neck of her robe. I pricked the rounded whiteness of her shoulder. For a heartbeat, remembering who lay beneath us, I wanted to drive the blade through to her heart. Instead I bent and took the offered drop of blood.

My tongue went dry; my throat tightened; my face burned. I thought I would choke on the act.

Phorenice mistook it for passion. “It thrills you?” she cried. “It burns like living fire? I have just felt it. By my face, Deucalion, if I had known the pleasure of being made a wife, I should not have waited so long. With another man I should have had no thrill. They say this feeling comes to a woman but once, and I would not change it for the glory of all conquests.” She leaned close so her red curls brushed my cheek, her breath hot against my mouth. “Tasted you ever any sweet so delicious as this knowledge that we are one now, past all dissolving?”

My endurance ended there. I had lied with my face all morning; my tongue would not forge lover’s phrases over a living tomb. Yet I felt, too, a kind of pity: her passion had grown wild, and I had done nothing to check it. Her frankness was the habit of rulers who display what common folk would hide.

“Words are poor things,” I said. “I am a man unused to women, and have but a small stock of phrases except the driest. Remember, Phorenice, a week ago I did not know what love was, and now that I have learned it—somewhat suddenly—the language has yet to come. My inwards speak; indeed they are full of speech; but I cannot translate into bald cold words what they say.”

Perhaps the High Gods took pity on my bound tongue and misery, for an interruption tore through the square.

A man ran in shouting, blood dripping from a wound. The cry leapt from mouth to mouth, multiplying until the whole crowd surged in panic, hands groping for weapons.

“The rebels are in!”
“They have burrowed a path into the city!”
“They have killed the cave-tigers and taken a gate!”
“They are putting the place to storm!”
“They will leave none of us alive!”

With rebels merely biting at the walls, it had been easy for the city to pretend contempt and keep pageants turning like millstones. But rebels inside the city changed everything—sound, breath, thought. Phorenice’s lover-glow snapped into the hard glare of a fighter as the messenger shrieked his tidings.

I stood and drew in air as if I had been underwater. “Here,” I said, “is work I can understand. I will go and sweep this rabble back to their burrows, Phorenice.”

“But not alone. I come too. It is my city still. Nay, sir—we are too newly wed to be parted yet.”

“Have your will,” I said. Under my breath, as we descended the steps, I spoke a farewell to Nais that no one could hear.

Armour-bearers met us with weapons. We stepped into litters, and slaves took us off at speed. The wounded man who had brought the news collapsed into a faint, giving no more detail, but the growing din of fighting guided us. Soon we met knots of people fleeing from the breach, screaming, loaded with household goods—bundles of bedding, jars of oil, baskets of grain, even a cooking-pot still swinging from its handle, sloshing thin stew onto the stones. Panic trampled domestic life into the dust.

It was useless to stop them. They could not fight; if they stayed, they would die and clog the streets for our soldiers. So we drove the litters through them with little regard for their coward convenience.

When we reached the place of irruption, the rebels’ advantage proved small under a soldier’s eye. They had tunnelled from behind a covering mound beneath the walls and opened cleverly through the floor of a middle-class house. There they had gathered their men under shelter, hoping the Empress’s marriage would dull vigilance. But they had been discovered too soon; troops were barracked nearby. Their extermination would be only a matter of time even without us.

Phorenice saw it as quickly as I did. She laughed, sank back into the litter cushions, and handed her weapons back to an armour-bearer who came panting up. “We grow nervous with our married life, my Deucalion,” she said. “We fear this new-found happiness will be taken from us too suddenly.”

I would not surrender the breathing-space so easily. “Let me crave a wedding gift of you,” I said.

“It is yours before you name it.”

“Then give me troops,” I said, “and set me wide a city gate a mile away from here.”

“You can gather five hundred as you go,” she answered, already calculating, “taking two hundred of those here. If you want more, they must be fetched from other barracks along the walls. But where is your plan?”

It would weary any hearth-teller to set down, blow by blow, how a scant handful of drilled fighters beat apart a camp that swarmed fifteen to one; the marrow of it was not heroics, but timing, and the taste of fear spreading quicker than fire in dry thatch.

The rebels had trusted their mine as a cook trusts a hidden storehouse—dig under the walls, pour in their numbers, and let the city fall like an overripe gourd. But of all who crawled into that dark throat, none crawled out again. First came the clamor from within Atlantis—sharp, broken, ungoverned—then, as if some giant hand had turned the sound-stone of the world, the cries inside changed their pitch. Panic in the open leaguer heard triumph behind the walls. A rumor is a wind; it finds every crack.

And these rebels, for all their wrongs and their Atlantean courage, had no true leaders left to hold the line. Their belief in each man’s equal voice made them a chorus with no conductor: loud, fierce, and apt to fall into discord the moment the beat was lost. Their weapons were poor too—blades that bent, spearheads that sat loose on shafts, slings with fraying thongs—tools that would have shamed a kitchen boy setting out to dress a boar.

So when their courage soured, it soured at once. We did not stand and receive them; we hunted. Turn a flank, drive through the center, worry the other flank, and again cut a passage through their mass—never letting them settle, never letting them hear their own breathing long enough to remember bravery. A charge is like a hot pan: touch and withdraw and touch again, and the hand learns pain. Soon their outer ring began to peel away toward the forests and the trampled corn-lands, and those still pressed in close fought only to buy themselves a gap to flee through.

No practiced soldier would boast of such work as if it were a fine duel. Had they met us on chosen ground with their terror quieted, they would have fought like cornered beasts, and sheer weight of bodies would have dragged down any phalanx Atlantis could have sent. Oppression had salted them; hunger had sharpened them. But that hour belonged to disorder, and we took it.

By sunset the siege was lifted as cleanly as a pot is lifted off a fire before it boils over. It was plain the rebellion might be ended without the city pouring on needless cruelty—because harshness, like rancid oil, only makes the next meal harder to stomach and the next hunger more desperate.

We followed the fugitives far enough to keep their retreat from stiffening into another stand. That chase drew us well away from the walls, and the day’s heat had baked the men until even their curses came out dry. So I chose a halt among half-ruined houses—broken shells that could be made into a temporary fort, their fallen stones as good as breastwork. Night in those lands was never merely night: it carried teeth, and sometimes claws.

Luck, in the manner of luck, arrived on cloven hooves. A small drove of little horses—more goat than horse to the eye, with split hooves that clicked on stone—came blundering past, startled by the rout. We dropped five before the rest vanished into the dark. The men set fires at once, and the smell of blood and singed hair rose under the ruined eaves. They worked like men who had done this a hundred times: slit, skin, and joint; fat rendered in a shallow pot; strips laid across green sticks to roast; darker portions thrust into embers to char and soften. The meat was lean and iron-tasting, but hunger is its own spice, and the smoke made a roof against the night.

Sentries paced beyond the light, calling their short cries to one another—the old soldier-music that keeps the mind from imagining footsteps where none exist. The wounded sat near the coals, washing grit from cuts, binding torn flesh with linen, heating blade-tips to sear what would not stop bleeding. In the ring of firelight the officers talked over the day’s charges as men talk over a recipe they mean to perfect—this turn here, that feint there, what failed, what held. Such talk is a soldier’s privilege, and to outsiders it is as dull as listening to a butcher argue bone by bone.

Sleep began to gather. One by one the men’s heads dipped; snores rose like a rough chant. My officers’ eyes were red with weariness, and they kept the conversation going only because my rank demanded it. Yet I was afraid—feverishly afraid—of being left alone with what waited in my skull when the world fell quiet. I pressed them on, making them answer, making them speak, until at last a sentry came up and saluted, and the interruption saved them.

“My lord,” he said, “there is a woman come up from the city whom we have caught trying to come into the bivouac.”

“How is she named?”

“She will not say.”

“Has she business?”

“She will say none. She demands only to see my lord.”

“Bring her to the fire,” I began—then checked myself. A woman who came alone to such a place did not come for comfort. “Stay,” I said instead, and rose. “Take me to her.”

At the outer line the night smelled different—damp earth, crushed fern, the faint sour tang of those boiling springs that threaded the land like veins of scalding broth. The sentry led me to where two others held a muffled figure.

“My comrades are holding her,” the first sentry said. “She might be a wench belonging to these rebels, with designs to put a knife into my lord’s heart, and then we sentries would suffer. The Empress seems to set good store upon my lord at present,” he added in his plain way, “and we know the cleverness of her tormentors.”

“Your thoughtfulness is frank,” I told him, and looked at the woman. Hood and cloak hid her face, but grief and love teach their own recognition. Even wrapped in darkness and wool, that shape was Ylga—Nais’s twin, and so close a mirror that my heart always stumbled when I saw her.

I told the sentries to release her without demanding her name, and led her away beyond earshot. The camp’s sounds dulled behind us: the crack of resin in flames, the low murmur of men settling, the soft clink of a guard’s gear. Out here, the land itself spoke—distant surf, insects that sawed at the air, and now and again the deep, hollow belch of a boil-spring.

“It is something of the most pressing that has brought you out here, Ylga?”

“You know me, then?” Her voice had that same edge I remembered—pride sharpened into bitterness. “There must be something warmer than the ordinary between us two, Deucalion, if you can guess who walks beneath all these mufflings.”

I did not answer that hook. “What is your errand?”

“Aye,” she said, and the word came out like a slap. “There’s my reward. All your concern’s for the message, none for the carrier. Well, good my lord—husband to the dainty Phorenice no longer.”

“This is news.”

“And true enough. She will have no more of you—divorces you, spurns you, thrusts you from her. And after the first splutter of wrath is done, then come pains and penalties.”

“The Empress can do no wrong,” I said, from habit and necessity both. “Speak respectful words of the Empress.”

“Oh, be done with that old fable. It sickens me.” She leaned nearer, and though the night hid her face I could feel her eyes fixed on mine. “She was mad for love of you, and now she’s mad with jealousy. She knows you gave Nais some of your priest’s magic, and that she sleeps until you choose to come and claim her—even if the day be a century from this.

“And if you want the method of her enlightenment, it is simple. There is another airshaft next to the one down which you did your cooing and billing, and it leads to another cell with another prisoner. The wretch heard all, and thought to buy his freedom by telling it.”

Her words had their own stink—betrayal, stale and sweet, like meat kept too long in heat. She went on, sharp as a cook’s knife.

“His news came late. With the pressure of the morning’s ceremonies, they forgot his ration, and when his gaoler remembered him at last, it was rather too late—Phorenice had tied herself publicly to a husband, and Nais had doubtless eaten her green drug. Still, fools will always try to barter what they have, and as was natural they chopped his silly head off for the attempt. And after that your Phorenice behaved as you may guess.

“And now you may thank me, sir, for coming to warn you not to go back to Atlantis.”

“But I shall go back,” I said, and felt the old fatal calm settle over me like ash. “If the Empress chooses to cut my head from its proper column, that is as the High Gods will.”

“You are more sick of life than I thought. But I think our Phorenice judges your case very accurately.” Her laugh was brief and without mirth. “It was permitted me to hear her rage. ‘Shall I hew off his head?’ said she. ‘Pah! Shall I give him to my tormentors and stand by whilst they do their worst? He would not wrinkle his brow at their fiercest efforts. No. He must have a heavier punishment, and one that endures. I shall lop off his right hand and his left foot, so he may be a fighting man no longer, and then I shall drive him forth crippled into the dangerous lands, where he may learn Fear. Beasts shall hunt him; the fires of the ground shall spoil his rest. He shall know hunger; he shall breathe bad air. And all the while he shall remember that I have Nais near me, living and locked in her coffin of stone, to play with as I choose, and to give over to what insults may come to my fancy.’

“That is what she said, Deucalion. Now will you go back to meet her vengeance?”

“No,” I said, because the word was the only sane thing in that moment. “It is no part of my plan to be mutilated and left to live.”

“So.” Ylga’s breath eased, as if she had been holding it. “Being a woman of some sense, I judged as much. And having some small kindness left for you, I have made a plan for your further movement. Does the name of Tob come back to you?”

“One who was captain of Tatho’s navy.”

“That same Tob. Gruff, rude, smelling vile of tar and old hemp—but with a sturdy honesty. Tob sails away this night for parts unknown, to found a kingdom with Tob for king, no doubt. He can find little to earn in Atlantis now, and he has scruples at seeing his wife and young ones hungry.” She spoke that last word as if hunger were a living thing she had watched gnaw men down to bone. “He told me this at the harbour side when I put my neck under the axe by saying I wanted carriage for you. Having me under his thumb, he spoke more freely than usual. You made an impression on Tob. He said—you will forgive his hearty disrespect—you were just the recruit he wanted, and whether you joined him or not, he would go to the nether Gods to do you service.”

“With him,” I said, “I learned something of fighting the greater sea beasts.”

“Well, go and do it again. Believe me, sir, it is your only chance. It would grieve me to hear the searing-iron hiss on your stumps.” She stepped closer, fingers catching my sleeve. “I bargained with Tob to get clear of the harbour forts before the chain is up for the night, and he will come to a point of shore we agreed upon and await you. Come, Deucalion—let me lead you there.”

“My girl,” I said, and meant it, “I owe you thanks.”

“Oh, your thanks.” The words were scorn, but her grip tightened a fraction as if she feared to let go. “Keep them. I did not come out here in dark and danger for mere thanks, though I knew well enough there would be little else offered. Now show me your walking pace. They will begin to want your countenance in the camp directly, and we need not tempt questions.”

So we left the bivouac lights behind us and went into the black. I carried my weapons ready, because that land bred sudden teeth—beasts that moved without sound, men whose hunger made them quiet. Ylga led, swift and sure, choosing the firmer ground by instinct. The night had a thick, wet smell—bruised leaves, mineral steam, the salt pull of the estuary ahead. Insects stitched their thin music into the air, and from time to time the earth sighed with the deep, simmering breath of hidden heat.

We spoke little, only what was needed: where the ground dipped, where a thorn-brake waited, where a boil-spring’s white vapor drifted like a ghost across the path. Once only did we touch—at a rivulet of scalding water that ran from a boiling spring toward the sea, its surface dimly smoking, its sound a steady, wicked hiss like fat spitting in a pan.

A fallen tree-trunk bridged it, slick with moisture.

“Are you sure of footing?” I asked. In that darkness, one slip would strip flesh as if it were skin from a rabbit.

“No,” she said simply, and reached out to take my hand.

I steadied her across; her fingers were cold despite the steamed air. When she reached the far side I loosened my grip. She let her hand slip away slowly, and I heard—more than saw—her take in a long breath that was almost a sigh. Then she turned and went on, and I followed.

Hour after hour we walked, the night turning from black to a thinner black. At last we topped a rise, and through the trees the estuary showed itself—a long, faint gleam, like poured metal under starlight. The ground near it was soggy, sucking at our feet, and the vegetation grew armed: barbs and spines that caught at cloak and skin. Ylga’s breath came harder.

When I offered her my arm, she snapped away from me. “I am no poor weakling,” she said. “If that is your only reason for wanting to touch me.”

But even pride grows tired. Presently we came out onto open beach where the worst of the thorns ended, and there ahead rode a ship at anchor a mile away—low, old-fashioned, strange under the starlight, with no machine-hum, no easy mechanical chitter, only the creak of timber and the hush of tide. The shore’s soundscape widened: surf breathing, pebbles ticking as waves withdrew, and far off the dull bellows of the burning mountains that fenced Atlantis like watchful beasts.

We went along the level sands toward the ship. Tob kept sharp watch. We were challenged as soon as we came within stone-throw.

“Halt there! Speak your business!”

Ylga called back, and when Tob recognized the appointed voices he grew civil. He called a crew, slacked anchor-rope until the hull ground against shingle, and thrust out the two steering oars like rough ladders for us to climb.

I turned to Ylga. “I will not forget what you have done. If the High Gods ever bring me back to Atlantis and power, you shall taste my gratitude.”

“I do not want to return,” she said. “I am sick of this old life here.”

“You have your palace,” I reminded her, because it was the obvious argument—“your servants, your wealth. Phorenice will not disturb you.”

“Oh, I could go back and be fan-girl tomorrow if I wished. But I do not want to go back.”

“This is no time for a gently nurtured lady to go forward,” I said. “I have been viceroy of Yucatan. Founding a foothold in new lands is hardship—privation—suffering you cannot guess. Few survive who go first, and those only of the hardiest.”

“I do not care. And besides, I can share the work.” Her voice warmed with a fierce insistence that made her seem suddenly nearer to Nais than ever. “I can cook. I can shoot a good arrow. I can make garments—yes, though they are cut from beast-skins and sewn with backbone sinews. Because you despise fine clothes, and because you have seen me only decked as fan-girl, you think I am useless. Bah, Deucalion. You know less of a woman than a boy new from school.”

“I have learned all I care to know about one woman,” I said, and the name I did not speak filled my mouth like salt. “And because of the memory of her, I could not presume to ask her sister to come with me now.”

“Aye,” she said, and bitterness returned like smoke on a wind-change. “Kick my pride. I knew well enough it was only second place to Nais I could get, all the time I wanted to come. Yet no one but a boor would remind me of it. Gods—and to think half the men in Atlantis have courted me, and now I am arrived at this.”

“I must go alone,” I said. “I would have been happier to take your esteem with me. But as it is, I suppose I shall carry only your hate.”

“That is the most humiliating thing of all.” Her voice broke, not into tears but into honesty. “I cannot bring myself to hate you. I ought to, after the brutal way you have scorned me. But I do not. I grow the fonder of you. And if I thought there was a way of keeping you alive and unmutilated here in Atlantis, I do not think I should point out that Tob is tired of waiting and will probably be off without you.”

Then she flung her arms around my neck and kissed me—hotly, recklessly, with the taste of salt air and smoke on her lips, and the tremor of a woman who has decided she will not be remembered as careful. She pulled back at once, as if ashamed of needing anything, and ran into the edge of the trees.

“But this is foolishness,” I called after her. “I must take you back through the dangers to some gate of the city, and then return.”

“You need not fear for me,” her voice came back from the shadow. “The unhappy are always safe. And besides, I have a way. It is my solace to know you will remember me now. You will never forget that kiss.”

“Fare you well, Ylga,” I cried. “May the High Gods keep you entirely in their holy care.”

No answer came. The forest swallowed her footsteps, but the forest was never silent; it took her into its own rustle and whisper, into the old language of leaves that no palace ever translated.

I turned down to the water, splashed in, and climbed aboard by the steering oars. Tob gave the word to haul-to, and the crew heaved at the rope with grunts timed to the ship’s creaking complaint. Timber and tide argued, then agreed, and the hull began to slide.

“Greeting, my lord,” Tob said when I came on deck, “but I’d have been pleased to see you earlier. We’ve small force and slow heels in this vessel, and the sooner we’re away and beyond pursuit, the safer for my woman and brats in that hutch of an after-castle. It’s long since I sailed so small and old-fashioned a ship as this. She’s no machines, and not even a steering mannikin. Look at the meanness of her furniture—and in your ear, I’ve suspicions of rottenness in her bottom. But she’s the best I could buy. If she reaches the place at the farther end I’ve got my eye on, we must make home there or be content to die, for she’ll never have strength to carry us farther or back. She’s been in the Egypt trade, and you know what that means for worm and rot.”

“You had hands enough before I came?” I asked.

“Oh yes. Fifty stout lads and eight women packed in somehow, and trouble enough to get them away. That thief of a port-captain wellnigh skinned us before he could see it lawful that so many useful fighting men might go out. Times are not what they were, and the sea trade’s about done. Sailor men of any skill have taken a woman or two and gone out in companies to try their fortunes. Why, I had trouble enough to get half a score to work the ship. All the rest are landsmen—raw, simple. If I land half alive at the other end, we shall do well.”

“With luck and a few good winds,” I said, “it should not take long to cross to Europe.”

Tob slapped his leg, as if I had offered him thin broth when he craved meat. “No savage Europe for me, my lord. Now see the advantage of being a mariner. I found once some islands to the north of Europe, separated from the main by a strait. I called them the Tin Islands, seeing that tin ore litters many beaches. I was driven there by storm, and said no word of it when I got back. And now, you see, it comes in useful. There’s no one in all Atlantis but me knows of those Tin Islands today. We’ll go and fight honestly for our ground, and build a town and a kingdom on it.”

“With Tob for king?” I said.

He hesitated, and the night wind filled the pause with canvas-flutter and rope-squeal. “Well, I’ve figured it out as such many a day. But I know when I meet my better, and I’m content to serve under Deucalion.” Then, with the practical cruelty of men who have buried hopes before, he added, “My lord would have done wiser to have brought a wife, though. I thought it was understood by the good lady who spoke to me at the harbour, or I’d have mentioned it earlier. The savages in my Tin Islands go naked and stain themselves blue with woad—filthy and brutish to look upon. Sturdy enough, should make good slaves; but one would have to get blunted in the taste before one could wish to father their children.”

“I am still husband to Phorenice,” I said.

Tob grinned, the grin of a man who has watched tides erase footprints. “The Gods give you joy of her. But it is part of a mariner’s creed—and you’ll grow into it—that wedlock does not hold across the seas. However, that matter may rest. My Tin Islands will delight you. A kingdom will not be so hard to carve as Egypt, or as you found in Yucatan. Beasts there, of course, and no one who can hunt need ever go hungry. Greater beasts are few: cave-bears and cave-tigers in small numbers, some river-horses and great snakes. But the greater lizards seem to avoid the land. As for birds, rarely one that can hurt a grown man. Oh, it will be a most desirable kingdom.”

He spoke of it as a man speaks of bread he has kneaded in his mind for years: every fold remembered, every rise imagined. Then he sighed, and the sigh carried a small private mourning.

“In truth I did imagine myself king, and there’s no denying. And there is not another man living I would have broken this voyage for but Deucalion. But don’t think I regret it. This breeze and the ebb take the old ship finely. See those fire baskets on the harbour forts? We’re abreast of them now. We’ll have dropped them and the city out of sight by daylight, and the flood will not begin to run up till then. But unless the wind hardens with dawn we may have to anchor when the flood makes. Tides run hard in these narrow seas. Aye—and there are shrewd tide-rips round my Tin Islands, as you shall see.”

When day came and showed the waters and the burning mountains hemming them beyond the shores, fear looked back over every shoulder. The estuary lay behind like a wide mouth, and men expected at any moment to see Phorenice’s navy come surging out—oar-blades flashing, sails bellying—bringing them back to servitude and the thin, sour gruel of the city’s squalid wards. I confess I shared their dread. The very air seemed to listen for pursuit, and every creak of our own timbers sounded like another ship’s answer.

But their brave words found no test. For the full flow of the tide we hung nearly in place, creeping so slowly it seemed the land slid past us by its own will. Not an oar nor sail showed behind. When the tide turned, the ship leapt forward as if relieved, and men breathed again, mightily comforted.

Tob’s woman came up with drink and would have had all pour libations to her as a future queen—making ceremony where the sea demanded attention. Tob cuffed her back into the after-castle and slammed the hatch, bidding the crew put the liquor to honest use.

“We are done with that foolery,” he said. “My Lord Deucalion will be king of this new kingdom we shall build in the Tin Islands, and a right proper king he’ll make, as you untravelled ones would know if you’d sailed the outer seas with him as I have done.”

In his boasting I heard regret—the ghost of his own crown—yet I held my tongue. From the moment I stepped aboard, my mind had begun to lay its own course, quiet as a cook measuring salt.

So we went down the great estuary. The others took pleasure from the desolate seas—no sails, no rivals, no patrols. It saddened me. I remembered when those waters had been whitened with shipping, when the soundscape of the sea was crowded with creaking yards, shouted orders, clanking chain, and the deep, comforting moan of commerce. Now there was only wind and wave, and the occasional cry of a distant bird—thin and lonely.

They had begun with barely two days’ provision. So, once clear of the estuary, we were forced into a coastwise crawl, putting into every likely river and sheltered beach to gather victual. There were days when the air was rich with fish-smell as men dragged nets and clubbed what glittered in the shallows; days when smoke lay low because we had killed and butchered on the sand, and the fat sputtered as it rendered, and the meat—rubbed with whatever bitter herbs we could find—hung in strips to dry in the wind. Salt was scarce, and so we learned again the old arts Atlantis had begun to forget: smoking slow to drive out damp, drying high to keep off insects, boiling down bones for broth that thickened into jelly when cooled, saving every scrap because waste is a luxury of safe cities.

“And when winter comes,” Tob said one evening as we sat with our rough food—fish roasted skin-on over coals, its flesh flaking white and sweet beneath fingers blackened by ash—“as its gales will be heavier than this old ship can stomach, I had determined to haul up and make a permanent camp ashore, and get a crop of grain grown and threshed before setting sail again. It is the usual custom in these voyages. And I shall do it still, subject to my lord’s better opinion.”

By then, after two months of this slow edging along the coast—two months measured in tides, in smoke, in the changing calls of shore-birds and the different voices of rivers—I saw my opening to speak what I had carried like a stone in my belly.

“Tob,” I said, “I am a poor, weak, defenceless man, and quite at your mercy. But what if I do not voyage all the way to the Tin Islands and oust you of this kingship?”

He brightened, though he tried to hide it under roughness. “Aye,” he grunted, “you are very weak, my lord, and mighty defenceless. We know all about that. But what’s else? Tell your meaning plain. I’m a common mariner, and understand little of your fancy talk.”

“Why, this,” I said. “It is not my wish to leave the continent of Atlantis. If you will put me down on any part of this side that faces Europe, I will commend you strongly to the Gods. I would I could give you money—or better, articles useful for your colonising—but you see me destitute.”

In the first mornings of the world—so the old stories say, in the tongue that my mother’s mother still mouthed over her bread—when the Gods shaped man and stepped back to watch what would become of him, They left corners of the earth where a human life could not easily take root. Of all such places in Atlantis none was so stubbornly ill-suited as that steaming margin which faces the savage continents of Europe and Africa.

The common folk would not go near it. In their market-speech, in the half-remembered prayers they muttered while kneading dough, they called it cursed ground: a place where the evil dead walked in daylight and finished what bog and beast began. Men who fled there from justice—so the gossip ran from hearth to hearth—came back of their own choosing, begging for chains rather than those bright, fabulous terrors.

I had been taught to weigh such tales. Much of it was myth, yes—myth as a cook’s “pinch” is myth, a measure that changes with the hand—yet enough of the old dread proved real that every hour demanded caution. The mountains kept their bellies on the boil. Tremors were as common as the tick of a pestle in a mortar. Invisible vapours, sour as spoiled broth, slid low through the fern-water and struck without warning; more than once I only knew them by the way birds fell silent and insects stopped their thin fiddling, as if the whole air held its breath, before the stench seized my throat and I stumbled away choking. Rivers ran clear and innocent-looking, yet carried poisons that burned the belly. The ground split into sudden cracks that drank whole streams; later, those stolen waters were spat out again in explosions of steam and mud that could flay a man to the bone. At any moment the land might belch boiling mire or a tongue of molten rock.

And still—beneath that violence—stood forests so old their age felt like a word that had no translation. Their black trunks rose out of warm mist; their crowns knitted the sky to a dim green ceiling. Fires were rare under those trees. Everything below was water: ankle-deep, knee-deep, sometimes to the waist, always warm, always wet. Steam wrapped the air and made each breath a swallow of bathhouse heat. It mortified the strength, and strength was the first ingredient required there.

The second was cunning, for these dangerous lands were the kingdom of lizards—endless families of them, some small enough to face with a spear and a steady nerve, others so huge that the mind refused their shape until the eye had no choice. I saw one rise out of a lake after feeding, and the wet earth shook and pulsed with its tread as if the whole swamp had a heart. It could have taken Phorenice’s mammoth into its belly as a man swallows a fig. Horny plates armoured its head and body, and along its back and tail and limbs ran spines that tore slivers from the trees as it pushed through them.

Now and again such monsters were caught in some great fissure of the ground, but not often. They moved quickly, and their sagacity was keen. They seemed to know when the worst boilings of the mountains would come; then they found safety in deeper lakes or buried themselves in mud wallows. One danger, which drove men frantic, did them no harm at all: heat. They lay in pools where sudden steam-bursts made the water leap into boiling fountains. I saw one run across a flow of molten rock that threatened to cut it off and pass without so much as a singe.

Among such neighbours my new life was thrown, and from the outset it was hard and perilous. I came close to knowing what Fear truly is, and only a fervent trust in the most High Gods—only the belief that my life remained under Their fostering care—kept me from that black knowledge.

For long enough, until I learned something of the ways of this steaming, sweltering land, I was as miserable as even Phorenice could have wished to see me. Constant wet rotted my clothes from my back until I went near naked, like a savage from beyond the seas. Agues racked my limbs, and I could find no proper herbs for their easing; the plants I tried tasted of tar and resin so rank I could not force them down without gagging. For days my best food was tree-grubs—fat, pale, and sweet in a sickly way when you crushed them between the teeth—and bitter leaves that promised nourishment and delivered only more weakness. When I did kill beasts, I knew too little of their qualities; I ate flesh that turned my stomach and made my guts knot like bad twine. Hunger taught quickly, and pain taught more.

Yet a man is born adaptable. As the months dragged on, I learned the limits of this new life and gathered some knowledge of its resources. I found a great black tree with a hollow core and a hole near the roots that led into its middle. There I harboured—at first with gratitude, like a traveller finding a warm oven-mouth in winter—until one night a monstrous lizard came nosing at the trunk. Its sheer weight made the tree rock like a sapling. It set its mouth to the opening and tried to suck me out as a bird draws a worm from rotten wood. The tongue that probed for me was hard as horn and slick as boiled hide. I could no more have wounded it than I could have harmed a mountain. By the will of the Gods I escaped; by my own will I took warning.

So I cut myself a ladder of footholds up the inside of the trunk until I reached a point ten man-heights from the ground, and there I notched more steps. With branches I made a floor to lie upon. Later, for luxury, I carved arrow-slit windows, narrow as a knife cut in bread, through which I could watch the steaming world below without being seen. I carried up sand for a hearth, grain by grain in my hands and in a skin, and there I learned to cook my victuals above the dangers of the open ground.

Those first attempts were crude, but even in exile the body demands its small ceremonies. I discovered which woods smoked sweetly and which filled the mouth with bitterness. I learned to split fish along the spine and pin the flesh flat on green sticks, so that the heat would take it evenly. The large-scaled river fish, when you scraped them clean with a sharpened shell and rubbed their bellies with crushed sour berries, gave a savour that—if I closed my eyes—almost recalled the salted dishes of home. The lesser turtles were easier captured if you waited at dusk when the air cooled and their movements grew sluggish; their flesh, stewed with a handful of aromatic bark, made a broth that strengthened me. I could not find grains for gruel, and every green thing fit to chew reeked of tar, but at least I no longer felt my ribs threaten to break through my skin.

Still the peril remained: the greater lizards, the sudden scalding floods, the treacherous ground. And as months mounted into years and the brute part of my nature grew more satisfied, other longings rose—hungers no turtle soup could answer.

From the ivory of a river-horse’s tooth I tried to carve a likeness of Nais as last I had seen her. My fingers were loving, my will earnest, but my art was dull. Time and again the face came out clumsy and pitiful: a curve too heavy, a mouth too stiff, eyes without her living softness. Yet even in that rude work there were hints—an outline here, a tilt there—that set my heart burning anew. I would sit in my tree-house and hold the ivory by the hearth’s low glow, “eating” it with my eyes as if looking could nourish me. It did not satisfy; it sharpened the ache to know her fate, to be with her again, or at least to see the place where she lay.

Other men of the Priests’ Clan had come into these Dangerous Lands. By mortifying the flesh, by fasting and cold vigils and the hard disciplines, they won intimacy with the Higher Mysteries that carried them beyond mere learning and repetition. Some returned to the abodes of men with knowledge that the vulgar called magic and miracles. The common people, hungry for wonders and easily bribed by spectacle, chased such arts with increased ardour, and the priests—since all arts must be allowed that serve a holy cause—did not rebuke them overmuch.

But I could not free my mind for those abstruse studies. On the voyage home from Yucatan I had communed with the Mysteries with growing insight; here, in exile, my mind was not my own. Nais held it with a lien that could not be foreclosed. Her sweet trespass was my chief solace, and it made my thoughts circle always back to her name, a name whose sound in our old dialect carried shades the court-speech could never hold. In the common tongue she was merely “Nais.” In the older word she was—no, there is no clean rendering: the syllables meant tenderness braided with light, a vow spoken low at dawn. That untranslatable richness lived in me like a flavour only my own mouth remembered.

At last my longing could no further be denied. Through one of my narrow windows I could see, far away, a mountain-top whitened with perpetual snow. Each night our Lord the Sun dyed that snow with blood as He set. Night after night I watched that ruddy light until my eyes watered. Night after night I remembered how, in my days of entering the priesthood, it had been my duty to adore our Lord as He rose behind those same snows. Always the thought followed: from that distant crest I could see across the continent to the Sacred Mount—the place with the city below it, where I had buried my love alive.

So I gave way and set out.

It was a perilous journey. In heavy mists that clung to the lower ground, my way lay blind before me. I lost it continually. To say I traversed three times the direct distance is modest. In those swamps the great lizards hunted, and because the country was new to me I did not know the safe hollows, the deadfalls, the places where a man might crouch and become no more than another stump. A hundred times I was within an ace of being seen and devoured at a mouthful. Yet the High Gods still desired me for Their own purposes; more than once I saw a huge head swing my way and then, as if the beast’s eyes had filmed, pass on. I did not call it luck. I did not call it skill. I called it the will of the Gods.

Twice rivers of scalding water roared boiling across my path, sudden and furious. I could not ford them without being cooked alive. I waited, shivering in steam, until I had gathered enough black timber to lash together rafts that would give me dry ferriage. The timber stank of resin; the rope I twisted from grasses cut my palms; my arms burned with labour. In another life I would have judged such work by the neatness of the knot, the soundness of the binding—like judging a well-trussed bird. Here I judged it by whether it held.

The journey was infinitely tedious, yet to me—after years of waiting—time ran on winged feet. I had been separated from my love until the strain was unbearable. Let me but see from a distance the place where she lay. Let me fill my eyes with it for a while. Then I could go back to my tree and remain patiently, awaiting whatever the Gods willed.

As I rose above the region of trees, the air grew chill. The steam thinned; sound changed. Below, the swamp had been loud with constant dripping and the hiss of vapours, the ceaseless rasp of insects, the sudden plop of unseen bodies sliding into water. Up here, those wet noises fell away. The wind took their place, a clean, sharp voice that made the mind feel bare. I made buskins and a coat of woven grasses to protect my body. The grasses were harsh; I softened them by rubbing them between stones and warming them by my skin before weaving, as one softens stubborn fibre before it can be made into something serviceable.

Higher still, where snow lay eternal, blown into gullies and frozen into solid banks and bergs of ice, progress became a labour in itself. The chill numbed me and stole my strength. My breath rasped; my chest ached; each step sank and slid. Overhead a huge man-eating bird followed me with forbidding swoops, sometimes coming so close that I had to threaten it with my spear. It lived by pulling down creatures whose weakness it scented, and up here weakness had a scent as plain as blood.

Yet the lure ahead made these difficulties seem small. Though the mountain air agreed with me ill, bringing sickness and panting, I pressed on with what speed I could muster. Time after time I thought the next spurt would bring me to the view my soul yearned for; time after time another bank of snow and ice rose before me.

At last I reached the crest.

I leaned on my spear and gave thanks to the most High Gods for Their protection and favour. The world spread below me in hard clarity. Far, far away I could see the Sacred Mountain, its ring of fires burning pale under the day. The splendid city at its foot was too distant to be seen, but I knew its position as a man knows the place of a scar beneath cloth. I knew its plan, and my soul went out to that throne of granite in the square before the royal pyramid where, years before, I had buried Nais alive.

Had Phorenice left the tomb unviolated?

I stood and filled my eye with the prospect, warming even to the smoke of mountains that I recognised like old acquaintances. My love for Nais burned so fiercely it seemed to push everything else out of me. It was as if my whole being leaned forward toward her, leaving room for nothing beside.

For long enough a voice seemed dimly to be calling my name, but I gave it no regard. I had come to that hoary summit for communion with Nais alone, and I wanted no interruption. The wind’s clean howl, the thin squeak of snow under my feet, the distant crack of ice settling—these were the only sounds I would accept. Below, the swamp’s constant hiss and drip was gone; up here even my own heartbeat sounded loud, like a drum in an empty room.

But the voice grew louder, insistent, and at last I started as if struck. Since parting with Tob and his company, I had seen no other human being. I gripped my weapon and called out, asking who spoke.

The reply came clearly from up the slopes. I saw a man coming toward me over the snows.

He was old and feeble. His body bent; his hair and beard were white as the ground he trod. Yet he moved with incredible speed for such years and seeming frailty. In his hand he carried the glowing Symbol of our Lord the Sun, and I knew that holy strength could lend power beyond flesh.

As he came closer, recognition settled on me like a cloak. Zaemon.

He made the sign of the Seven. I returned it, completing the form with due ceremony. Then he saluted me as messenger appointed by the High Council of the Priests seated before the Ark of the Mysteries. I bowed in humble obeisance.

“In all things I will obey the orders that you put before me,” I said.

“Such is your duty, my brother.” His voice cut through the wind as if it owned the air. “The command is that you return immediately to the Sacred Mountain, so that if human means may still prevail, you—as the most skilful general Atlantis owns within her borders—may yet save the country from final wreck and punishment. The woman Phorenice persists in her infamies. The poor land groans under her heel. And now she has laid siege to our Sacred Mountain itself, and swears that not one soul shall be left alive in all Atlantis who does not bend humbly to her will.”

“It is a command and I obey it.” The words came readily—oath-trained words, the old liturgy of obedience. But another hunger rose behind them, stronger than discipline. “Yet let me ask of another matter that is intimate to both of us. What of Nais?”

“Nais rests where you left her, untouched. Phorenice knows by her arts—she has stolen nearly all the ancient knowledge now—that still you live. She keeps Nais unharmed beneath the granite throne in the hope that some time she may use her as a weapon against you. Little she knows the sternness of our Priests’ creed, my brother. Why, even I, who am the girl’s father, would sacrifice her blithely if her death or ruin might do a tittle of good to Atlantis.”

“You go beyond me with your devotion,” I said, and heard how my own voice had changed: the old cadences still there, but roughened by years of swamp and solitude.

Zaemon leaned toward me, brow glowering. “What!”

“Or my old blind adherence to the ancient dogma has been sapped and weakened by events.” I spoke plainly, tasting each word as if it were a foreign spice, measuring whether it would burn the tongue. “You must buy my full obedience, Zaemon, if you want it. Promise me Nais—and your arts, I know, can snatch her—and I will be true servant to the High Council of the Priests, and will die in the last ditch if need be for the carrying out of order. But let me see Nais given over to the fury of that wanton woman, and I shall have no inwards left except to take my vengeance and to see Atlantis piled up in ruins as her funeral-stone.”

Zaemon’s look turned bitter, as if I had served him rancid wine in a sacred cup.

“And you are the man the High Council thought to trust as they would trust one of themselves? Truly we are in an age of weak men and faithless now. But, my lord—nay, I must call you brother still: we cannot be too nice in our choosing to-day—you are the best there is, and we must have you. We little thought you would ask a price for your generalship, having once taken oath on the walls of the Ark of the Mysteries itself that always, come what might, you would be servant of the High Council of the Clan without fee and without hope of advancement. But this is the age of broken vows, and you are going no more than trim with the fashion. Indeed, brother, perhaps I should thank you for being no more greedy in your demands.”

“You may spare me your taunts.” The wind pressed cold against my teeth; my mouth was dry as old bread. “You, by self-denial and profound search into the highest of the Higher Mysteries, have made yourself something wiser than human. I have preserved my humanity, and with it its powers and frailties. It seems each of us has his proper uses, or you would not be come now here to me. Rather you would have done the generalling yourself.”

“You make a warm defence,” Zaemon said. “But I have no leisure to stand before you with argument. Come to the Sacred Mountain, fight me this wanton upstart Empress, and by my beard you shall have your Nais as you left her as a reward.”

“It is a command of the High Council which shall be obeyed,” I said. “I will come with my brother now, as soon as he is rested.”

“Nay,” said Zaemon, and there was something in the word—an edge like a knife slid free of its sheath. “I have no tiredness. And as for coming with me, there you will not be able. But follow at what pace you may.”

He turned and set off down the snowy slopes.

I followed.

Gradually he distanced me. His speed increased, though the incline was treacherous and the snow deep. Soon he passed out of my sight around the spur of an ice-cliff, and I found myself alone on the mountain side.

Alone—yes, truly alone. For his footmarks, once deep, grew shallower, less noticeable. I had to stoop to find them, as if searching for a lost word in a dead language. Presently even those faint impressions vanished entirely. The great flank of the mountain lay before me trackless, untrodden by the foot of man since time began.

I was not shaken by amazement. It was beyond my art to compass such a thing—I had spent my exile more with memories of Nais than with study of the uppermost recesses of the Higher Mysteries in which Zaemon was so prodigiously wise—but I had some inkling of his powers. Zaemon, I knew, would be back again in his dwelling on the Sacred Mountain, shaken and breathless, even before I had found the end of his tracks.

It behoved me to join him there in the quickest possible time.

I had his promise now for my reward, and I knew he would carry it into effect. Before, I had made an error. I had valued Atlantis most, and Nais—my private love—second. Now I meant to be honest with others even as with myself. Though all the world were hanging on my choice, I could but love my Nais most, and serve her first and foremost of all.

My crossing of Atlantis, though it dragged itself over leagues of thorn and hunger and fear, does not deserve the dignity of a full telling. The continent had learned to taste of ash. Even the wind seemed to have forgotten clean scents.

Only once did I stop long enough for a place to put its mark upon me.

I had run a stag down on open savannah, the kind of clean, wide grassland where a man can hear his own breath and measure danger by sound: the far-off cough of a cat, the grind of hooves, the low insect-drone that thickens when something large moves through it. When at last the animal went down, I worked quickly, because meat is a clock that starts the moment life stops. I rolled it, split the hide, and began to portion what I could carry—dense haunch, sweet backstrap, organs still warm and metallic. I salted nothing; I had no salt. I laid the strips on broad leaves to cool, and the air filled with that feral perfume of blood and crushed grass.

I took a moment to listen—an old habit from the Mountain days, where the world’s smallest noises could mean the difference between ritual and ruin. The savannah gave me nothing but wind and the thin clatter of seed heads.

Underfoot, though, the grass hid a thing which did not sing.

A great-legged worm—green as unripe fruit, glossy, obscene—lay coiled where my heel brushed it. It struck like a flash, a needle-thin head snapping forward. Pain bit into my lower thigh, sharp and clean as a knife’s point, then spread with a burning that felt as though the flesh itself had been salted with fire. I saw its tiny fangs withdraw, and with the same motion I drove my knife through its neck. It twisted, a hundred legs scrabbling like frantic fingers on a drumskin, writhing in loops that made a dry, sickening rustle in the grass.
