I did what a man must do with venom: I made the wound bleed. I scraped the blade clean in earth, then stabbed around the punctures—hard, deep—so the blood would run and carry what poison it could away. The red streamed warm down toward my heel. The worm’s body slackened at last.

I shouldered my bundle of meat and went on, thankful, for I believed I had paid the price and bought my escape.

By afternoon the price demanded more.

Tightness filled the leg, then a throbbing which matched my pulse, as if something inside it beat against the skin. Each hour swelled me further, until the flesh looked stretched like parchment drawn over a drum. Nausea rose in waves that emptied me, again and again, until there was nothing left but bile and the bitter taste of fear. The forest around me—darkening as the sun sank—had become a place of noises too slow and too large: the distant crack of a branch under weight, the hush of something sliding through leaves. Had any of the great hunters found me then, I would have been no fight at all, only a stagger and a fall and meat to be torn.

When darkness came, I forced myself into a tree, hauling my swollen leg with both hands, teeth clenched until my jaw ached. I wedged myself into the crutch of a limb and stayed there, awake and shivering, listening to night’s acoustic tyranny: the moan of wind through canopy, the wet cough of unseen beasts, the drip of sap like slow footsteps. Pain kept time without mercy.

At dawn, when the night-feeders withdrew to their dens and the smaller birds began to stitch the air with thin calls, I climbed down. I leaned on my spear and limped onward through sombre forest. In better days, moss on the north side of trunks was a steady compass. Here, in my fever, I saw moss everywhere, as though the world had turned and left me behind. My head swam; the forest spun its own language around me—leaf-whisper, insect-hum, the sudden silence that comes when something watches.

Still I drove myself on, because the last instinct left to me was obedience: the High Council’s command, and the promise they had hung before my eyes like a lamp—Nais enlarged, Nais restored.

My final clear memory was of a figure moving in the black trees toward me—someone human, quick, intent. A hand took my arm. A voice spoke, close and urgent. Then waking tore loose and flew.

When sense returned, it came in layers: first the smell of smoke and crushed herbs; then the taste of bitterness on my tongue; then the sound of fire licking sticks and the soft crackle of a cave’s breath. I lay on a bed of leaves in a cleft between two rocks. The shelter was crude but cunning: stakes and rough-built barricades bristled at the entrance against the larger marauders. My thigh was bound and smeared with a poultice dark as mud, sharp with plant oils. Across the cavern squatted a woman over a small fire, turning a mess of wood-grubs glazed in honey. The grubs popped as they heated; fat hissed; the honey caramelized at the edges, sending up a scent that was both sweet and animal, like a poor man’s feast.

“How came I here?” I asked. My voice sounded strange in that confined stone, too loud, as if the cave amplified every weakness.

“I brought you,” she said, without looking up.

“And who are you?”

“A nymph, they call me,” she replied, and there was pride in it, though her clothes were patched and her hair tangled. “I practise as such. I gather herbs. I cure the diseases of those who come. I tell fortunes. I make predictions. For that I take what they can afford. And if they do not pay according to their means”—she stirred the grubs with a stick, and her eyes flashed in the firelight—“I clap on a curse to make them wither. It’s lean enough living when wars and pestilence leave so few poor folk in the land.”

“Do you visit Atlantis?”

“Not I. Phorenice would have me boiled in brine alive if she could lay hands on me. Our dainty Empress tolerates no magic but her own. They say she is for pulling down the Priests off their Mountain now.”

“So you do get news of the city?”

“Assuredly. It is my trade to get news—good or bad—or else how could I sell fortunes to the vulgar? And I marked your quality by your speech, my lord. You are not one of those who come to me for love-philtres and dung-charms. I have no fear of speaking plain.”

“Tell me then. Phorenice still reigns?”

“Most vilely.”

“As a maiden?”

“As the mother of twin sons. Tatho’s her husband now, and has been these three years.”

“Tatho,” I said, and the name tasted like old iron. “Who followed him as viceroy of Yucatan?”

“There is no Yucatan.” She clicked her tongue, as if scolding a child for asking after a dead goat. “A vast nation of little hairy men—so the tale goes—came from the West and overran it. Clubs of wood tipped with stone, that was their only arm, but numbers were their chief weapon. They wanted no plunder, no slaves, no cities. To eat the flesh of Atlanteans was their only lust, and they followed it prodigiously. Their numbers were like bees in a swarm.

“They came to each city in turn. The colonists slew them in thousands, but the weight of them always prevailed. They ate clean each city they took—clean, mind you—and left the ruins for the beasts. Then on to the next. At last they reached the coast towns. Tatho and the few that survived took ship and sailed home. They even ate Tatho’s wife for him.” She shrugged as if the Gods had merely changed the weather. “Curious persevering things, these little hairy men. The Gods send they do not cross the seas to Atlantis, or they would be worse plague to this poor country than Phorenice.”

I had heard of those creatures before—neither fully human nor wholly beast, a link held in check by divine restraint in deep forests, loosed at times as punishment. I did not trouble to share such knowledge with this cave-nymph. Her craft lived by what men could be made to fear. But grief struck me, sharp and private, at the thought of Yucatan—of all the labour, the planning, the building, the hopes fed like bread to a future that was now chewed to nothing.

I kept my name quiet. The time was not yet full for it to be spoken aloud.

“What about Egypt?” I asked.

“In its accustomed darkness,” she said, and spat into the fire. “Who cares for Egypt these latter years? Who cares for anyone but himself and his own estate? Time was, the country folk and the hunters brought me offerings here for sheer piety. Now they come only if they can bargain. So instead of living fat and hearty, I make lean meals off honey and grubs.” She lifted the stick, letting a strand of honey stretch and snap. “A poor life, a nymph’s, in these latter years. It’s the fashion for all classes to believe in no kind of mystery now.”

“What manner of pestilence did you speak of?”

“I have not seen it.” For a moment, genuine relief softened her face. “Thank the Gods it has not come this way. But they say it has grown from the folk Phorenice has slain and left unburied. She is always slaying. The bodies lie thicker than birds and beasts can eat them. For which of our sins did the Gods let her reign? I wish she and her twins were boiled alive in brine before they came between an honest nymph and her living.

“They say she has put an image of herself in all the temples now, and ordered prayers and sacrifices night and morning. She has decreed all other Gods inferior to herself and forbidden their worship. Those that are not sufficiently devout have their hamstrings slit by her tormentors, to aid them constantly into a devotional attitude.” She looked at me then, as if remembering I was made of flesh. “Will you eat of my grubs and honey? There is nothing else. Your back was bloody with carrying meat when I found you, but you had lost your load. You must either taste this mess of mine now, or go without.”

Hunger is not proud. I ate.

The grubs were soft inside their browned skins, like marrow, and the honey lent them a sweetness that almost made them respectable. Smoke from the sticks gave a faint resinous bitterness. I chewed slowly, forcing my mouth to remember pleasure even in such fare, because a man who forgets taste forgets the world he fights for. Outside the cave, the forest’s noises thickened and thinned with the hours, and each change in its chorus seemed to tug at memory—voices of villages, lullabies in dialects already thinning like old cloth, the prayers of women spoken in half-forgotten words.

I stayed six days. She worked on my leg with herbs and with that half-theatrical certainty of the self-styled wise. Some of it was trick, no doubt, but not all. The poultices stung and cooled; the swelling drew back; the fever broke. When at last I could bear weight again, I paid her as best a man can who carries no coin: I hunted and brought her down a fat cloven-hoofed horse from the plains, and left it dressed, the meat portioned cleanly so it would not spoil. She watched my cuts with keen eyes, as if judging not just my skill but my respect for the animal’s gift.

Then I went on.

From there onward the land showed scars where once it had been sturdy. Here had been homesteads—some built in trees to escape cave-bear and cave-tiger, some built on the ground of ponderous logs clamped and dovetailed so no beast could pull them down. A man within those log walls could thrust a spear through the interstices and wound, sometimes kill, anything that came for him.

Now silence owned them. Fire-hearths before doors were choked with weeds. Vegetable patches had been taken back into scrub and jungle by the greedy fingers of the forest. Farther on, villages appeared with their strong walls—but the gateway logs lay strewn, half-swallowed by sprouting undergrowth. Only kitchen middens remained: heaps of broken bones and cracked potsherds, mute proof that once there were mouths here, and words spoken over meals.

Phorenice’s influence seemed to have spread like blight over what had once been smiling, easy-living land. Even the soundscape had changed. Where villages should have held the dawn-clatter of pestles, the chatter of children, the lowing of penned beasts, there was only wind and the harsh calls of opportunist birds.

So far, men had interfered little with me. Many fled with their women at the bare sight of me; some stood with threatening faces but did not attack, seeing I offered no insult. A few gave shelter and provision—thin soups, bitter roots, what little their fear allowed them to spare. But as I neared the city and began to cross muddy beaten paths, the density of people increased, and with it the chance of delay.

Each village had its watcher in the tower. If he spied me, he would cry, “Here is a masterless man,” and the people would rush out to spoil me of weapons and appoint me labourer, as though a free body were merely a tool to be seized.

I had no desire to slaughter these wretches. Pity weighed heavier than anger; they had been ground down until they could imagine no law but hunger. Often words served. Sometimes I let my voice go hard as iron, and they would stare at my fierceness and step aside. When words failed, I struck as lightly as I could, my object always to move forward and leave no unnecessary dead behind. Soon it became my custom to turn into the forest and make circuits whenever I came within smell of their garbage—sour refuse, stale smoke, the rank reek of crowded misery.

Among greater towns I kept beyond their walls, refusing to be snagged by any governor’s whim, any new edict that might turn a traveller into property. My progress became furtive, but pride did not bark at me too loudly. The fever of haste burned hot, and I had little space within me for other emotions.

At last, within half a day’s journey of Atlantis itself, I saw the Sacred Mountain rise with its ring of fires looming high beside the city, a constant blaze like a crown half-set into earth. Caution trebled. Everywhere the country showed it had been drained of fighting men. Everywhere women prayed—not for victory’s glory, but for an ending: the rout of the Priests or the killing of Phorenice, so the land might at last have peace enough to lick its wounds.

An army invested the Sacred Mountain, and its single approach was guarded narrow as a needle’s eye. Even after such a journey, it seemed I would have to sit down helpless, unable to fulfil the High Council’s command and earn the reward promised—Nais.

Force would be useless. I could have one good fight—a gorgeous fight—one man against an army, and then my usefulness would end in a heap of meat for crows. No. This was the occasion for guile.

I found covert on the outskirts of a wood and lay there a day and a night, brain aching with plans that broke like thin pottery. Across the plain stood the grim walls of Atlantis, and beyond them the heads of temples and palaces and pyramids. The step-sides of the royal pyramid caught the light strangely. Phorenice had overlaid their former whiteness with sheets of shining yellow metal. Gold—soft, hungry metal—turned architecture into proclamation.

But it was not the gaudy change that moved me. I remembered the square before that pyramid, and the granite throne carved with the snake and the outstretched hand. In the hollow beneath that throne lay Nais, my love, asleep these eight years because of the drug given to her—alive still, waiting, if only I could reach Zaemon where he defied the Empress and announce my coming.

In that wood’s edge I raged, not aloud—there were too many ears in the world now—but inwardly, furious at my own failure to find a way in. Then, on the morning that followed, a man came running toward me.

“You need not threaten me with your weapons,” he called. “I mean no harm. It seems you are Deucalion—though I should not have known you in those rags and skins, behind that tangle of hair and beard. You will give me your good word. Believe me, I have not loitered unduly.”

He was a lower priest I knew and held in little esteem: Ro, greedy and slick, a man who worshipped comfort as faithfully as any god. His breath rasped. Even his haste had a cautious sound, as if he feared the noise of his own footsteps.

“From whom do you come?” I asked.

“Zaemon laid a command on me. He came to my house—how he got there I cannot tell, seeing Phorenice’s army blocks all passage to and from the Mountain.” Ro licked his lips, and the gesture made him look more anxious than respectful. “I told him I wished to be mixed with none of his schemings. I am a peaceful man, Deucalion, and I have taken a wife who requires nourishment. I still serve in the same temple, though we have swept out the old Gods by order of the Empress and put her image in their place. The people are tidily pious nowadays, those that are left, and the living is consequently easy. Far more offerings now than in the old days. I had no wish to be mixed with matters that might deprive me of a snug post—and my head to boot.”

“I can believe it all of you, Ro.”

“But there was no denying Zaemon.” He thrust out his right hand. The fingers hung lifeless, the wrist slack. “You see that? Dead and paralysed from the wrist. A gift of Zaemon. He bestowed it, he said, to make me collect my attention.” His voice wavered, part fear, part indignation. “He called me apostate, would not let me put up a word in defence of how the change was forced upon me. Finally he said I might either do his bidding on a certain matter to the letter, or take the punishment my falling away had earned. ‘I shall not kill you,’ said he, ‘but I will cover all your limbs with paralysis, such as you have tasted already, and when at length death reaches you in some gutter, you will welcome it.’”

“If Zaemon said those words,” I told him, “he meant them. So you accepted the alternative.”

“Had I—having a wife depending on me—any other choice? I asked his pleasure. It was to find you when you came in from some distant part of the land, and deliver to you his message.

“‘Then tell me where is the meeting place,’ said I, ‘and when.’

“‘There is none appointed, nor is the day fixed,’ said he. ‘You must watch and search always for him. But when he comes, you will be guided to his place.’” Ro’s eyes darted toward the trees, as if the very leaves might be informers. “Well, Deucalion, I think I was guided, but how, I do not know. Now I have found you, and if there is such a thing as gratitude, I ask you to put in your word with Zaemon that this deadness be taken away. It is an awful thing for a man to go through life like this, for no real fault of his own. Zaemon could cure it from where he sat, if he was so minded.”

“You seem still to have very full faith in some of the old priests,” I said.

“I serve the one that gives me employment,” he muttered, then hurried on, eager to finish. “The message is simple. You are to make a great blaze. Fire the grasses of the plain in front of this wood if you choose. And on the night that follows, you are to go round to that flank of the Sacred Mountain away from the city where the rocks run down sheer. There they will lower a rope and haul you up to their hands above.”

“It seems easy. I thank you for your pains. I will ask Zaemon that your hand may be restored.”

“You shall have my prayers if it is.” His gaze crawled over my rags, my crude skins, my emptiness. “And look—Deucalion—it is a small matter. It would be less likely to slip your memory if you saw to it at once on your landing. Later you may be disturbed. Phorenice is bound to pull you down off your perch up there now she has made her mind to it. She never fails once she sets her hand to a thing. Indeed, if she was no Goddess at birth, she is making herself into one very rapidly. She has got all the ancient learning of our Priests, and more besides. She has discovered the Secret of Life these recent months—”

“She has found that?” The words tore out of me. For a moment even the wood seemed to hush. “How? Only the Three know that. It is beyond our knowledge even who are members of the Seven.”

“I know nothing of her means. But she has the secret, and now she is as good an immortal—so she says—as any of them. Dangerous for me to be missing from my temple overlong, so I will go. You will carry that matter in your mind? It means much to me.” He hesitated, shame and greed battling in his throat. “And if you think my service is of value to you—some small coin, or even a link of bronze? I am at great expense buying nourishment for my wife.”

“You see me poor,” I said. “Practically destitute.”

“If you have nothing, you cannot give.” His voice turned brisk with self-justification. “So I’ll bid you farewell.”

He went, and I was not sorry. Ro had always smelled to me of stale incense and fresh appetite.

I wasted no time. Almost before his timorous heels were clear, I put fire to the grasses.

The plain took flame with a roar like a great beast waking. Dry stalks caught in a bright rush; seed-heads exploded into sparks; smoke rolled thick and bitter, a dark tongue licking the sky. The crackle became a wall of sound, drowning the smaller life-noises—no birds, no insects, only the huge consuming hiss and pop. It was a signal, yes, but it was also a declaration: that the land could still be made to speak in sudden, ruthless language.

That night, under cover of darkness and smoke’s lingering stink, I went to the appointed place. The cliff on the Mountain’s far flank fell sheer and smooth, gaunt as bone. No rope hung there at first, and I waited, listening for any human sound above—boots on stone, murmured prayer—but heard only the distant, steady roar of the Mountain’s ring-fires, and far below, the faint dull undertone of the city’s unrest.

Then a rope slid down the face like a slender snake.

I made a loop, slipped it over one leg, and pulled hard as signal. Those above hauled. The rope bit into my flesh; stone scraped my palms; my muscles shook with the effort, but the ascent was clean. Phorenice’s troops had not thought to guard that seamless precipice.

So I went back to the Sacred Mountain after so many toilsome, warring years.

The men who hauled me up were old. Their breath came in harsh, wet pants, and I wondered, at first, why younger priests had not been set to such work. But with the Clan it is always taken for granted that an order is given for the best. Besides, these old ones did not offer themselves for questioning. They took me at once to Zaemon, and that was what I wished.

Zaemon greeted me with the royal sign. His voice cut through the constant fire-noise of the crest, steady as a bell.

“All hail to Deucalion,” he cried, “King of Atlantis, duly called thereto by the High Council of the priests.”

“Is Phorenice dead?” I asked.

“It remains for you to slay her and take your kingdom,” Zaemon said, “if, indeed, when all is done, there remains a man or a rood of land to govern. The sentence has gone out that she is to die, and it shall be carried into effect—even though we have to set loose the most dreadful powers that are stored in the Ark of the Mysteries and wreck this continent in our effort. We have borne with her infamies all these years by command sent down by the most High Gods; but now she has gone beyond endurance. They it is who have given the word for her cutting off.”

“You are one of the highest Three,” I said. “I am only one of the Seven. You best know the cost.”

“There can be no counting the cost now, my brother—and my king. It is an order.”

“It is an order,” I repeated, and the formality tasted like vow and chain. “So I obey.”

Zaemon’s face was hard, but beneath it lived a fury older than any one man. “If it were not impious to justify the Gods,” he said, “it would be easy. The woman usurped the throne—yet she was forgiven and bidden rule wisely. She tampered with our holy religion—yet she was forgiven. She killed the peoples of Atlantis in greedy useless wars and destroyed the country’s trade—yet she was forgiven. She desecrated the old temples and has set up images of herself to be worshipped as deity—yet she was forgiven. But at last her evil cleverness discovered the tremendous Secret of Life and Death, and there she overstepped the boundary of the High Gods’ forbearance.

“I myself went to carry a final warning and faced her in the great banqueting-hall. Solemnly I recited the edict. She chose to take it as a challenge. She would live eternally herself, and share her knowledge with those that pleased her. Tatho, her husband, should also be immortal. Indeed, if she thought fit, she would cry the secret aloud so even the common people might know it, and death from mere age would become a legend.

“She cared no wit how she might upset the laws of Nature. She was Phorenice, and the highest law of all. And finally she defied me there, and defied also the High Gods that stood behind my mouth. ‘My magic is as strong as yours, you pompous fool,’ she cried, ‘and presently you shall see the two stand side by side upon their trial.’

“She began to collect an army from that moment. We made our preparations. It was discovered by our arts that you still lived, and King of Atlantis you were made by solemn election. How you were summoned, you know as nearly as it is lawful one of your degree should know. How you came, you understand best. But here you are, my brother, and being King now, you must order all things as you see best for the preservation of your high estate—and we others live only to give you obedience.”

“Then being King,” I said, “I can speak without seeming to threaten. I must have my Queen first, or I am not strong enough to give my whole mind to this ruling.”

“She shall be brought here,” Zaemon said, and for the first time in that harsh place his voice softened a grain.

“So. Then I will be a general now,” I answered, “and see to the defences of this place, and view the men who are here to stand behind them.”

I went out, and Zaemon gave place and followed.

Night still lay over the world, but there is no darkness on the upper Sacred Mountain. The ring of fires—fed eternally from the earth-breath that wells from below—burns round one half of the crest, lighting it always as bright as day, and forming no small part of its fortification. The flames made their own weather of sound: a continuous roaring, broken by sudden gusting flares like shouted syllables. It was said that, in the early dawn of history, men first came to the Mountain as stronghold because of this natural defence—because fire is a wall that never sleeps, and its voice drowns the approach of enemies until they are already burning.

Under that ceaseless flame-song, with the city and its golden pyramid crouched beyond, I stood returned at last to the Mountain, and the siege tightened like a noose around all our fates.

From where we stood together just under the Sacred Mountain’s crest, the city lay open below us as though some careful steward had unrolled a chart upon a table. The harbour’s dark water held a faint sheen where the estuary broadened; beyond, the rim of hills gave off their habitual threads of smoke and sour vapour. In the city’s heart the Lord Sun’s stone circle rose bare and grim, and nearer still the royal pyramid shouldered up—its gilded faces catching what little light remained and throwing it back, new gold answering old. In the square before it, dwarfed by distance to a mere knob upon the paving, waited the granite throne. Nine years it had sat there with its secret—nine years my Nais had slept beneath that cold weight.

Old Zaemon watched the direction of my gaze as a man reads a kettle’s murmur before it boils. “Ay,” he said, and the sigh that left him was dry as husk. “I know where your chief interest lies. Deucalion, when he landed here new from Yucatan, was a strong man. The King we have chosen—and he is the best we have to choose—has his weakness.”

“It can be turned,” I said. “Give me Nais here, living and warm to fight for, and I am stronger than the cold viceroy and soldier you speak about.”

“I have passed my word to that already, and you shall have her,” he answered. “But at the cost of bruising this new kingdom of yours. Maybe too we may rid you of Phorenice and her brood. But I do not think it likely. She is too wily. Once we begin our play she will guess whence it comes and how it ends, and she will slip away before harm can reach her. The High Gods—who send trials for our refinement—have also seen fit to give her some knowledge of how these earth tremors may be set a-moving.”

“I have seen her juggle with them,” I said, tasting bile at the memory. “But may I hear your scheme?”

“It will be shown you in good time,” Zaemon said. “For the present, sleep. To-night it is your part to go into the city and take your woman—my daughter—when she is set free, and bring her here as best you can. For that you will need all a strong man’s strength.”

He stepped back and looked me up and down, weighing me like a butcher judging a carcass, not with cruelty but with the calm exactness of one who knows what the body can bear. “There are not many folk that would take you for the tidy, clean-chinned Deucalion now, my brother. Your appearance will be fine armour down yonder to-night when we wake the city with earth-shaking and terror. As you stand, you are hairy enough, shaggy enough, naked enough, and dirty enough for some wild savage newly landed out of Europe. Have a care no fine citizen takes a fancy to your thews and seizes on you as his servant.”

“I pity him in his household if he does.”

Zaemon’s laugh came quick and rare as a spark struck from flint. Then the gravity returned at once, as though laughter were a foreign tongue he could not comfortably speak. “Get you to bed, my King. Leave me to go into the Ark of Mysteries and prepare there with another of the Three the things that must be done. It is no light business to handle the tremendous powers we must put into movement this night. There is danger for us as for you. So if by chance we do not meet again till we stand behind the stars, giving account to the Gods—fare you well, Deucalion.”

I slept as a soldier sleeps: the body taking its ration of rest without bargaining, the mind refusing to pick at fear like a scab. Only the weak permit their sleep to be broken on such occasions. When the dark was well set I roused and fetched those who should attend the rope. Our Lady the Moon did not shine at that turn of the month; the air was thick with blackness, a palpable thing that seemed to press against the eyes. So I was out of sight all the while they lowered me.

At the deep foot of the cliff the rocks lay tumbled like bones, and I began to move with a caution as fine as a cook’s knife work, for Phorenice’s soldiers squatted uneasily round their camp-fires as though they already heard, in the smallest creak of stone, the prelude to disaster. Their voices were low; their armour made the wrong little clinks; even their coughing sounded afraid. I had no mind to stir that wakefulness. I slipped in the darkest seams of shadow, and at last came to the spot where the passage ended—the secret way I had used before to get beneath the city walls.

The lamp was in place. I made my way along the windings swiftly. The air down there was more noxious than I remembered: hot breath laced with mineral reek, the throat catching on it as on rancid smoke. It was as though the earth had begun already to simmer, brewing its poisons in unseen pots. Zaemon, I judged, had started to stir the internal activities that would shortly convulse the city.

But again I had trouble finding an exit—not because there were people moving above, but because the very set of the masonry had changed. In older times the Priests’ Clan oversaw all plans and ruled out anything that would clash with their passages and hidden chambers. In this modern day the Priests were of small account and had no say. The builders, with their proud new measures and their careless dominant speech, had blundered and sealed up outlets as a thoughtless hand spoils a family recipe by changing the old words it cannot understand.

So I got out of the net of tunnels where I could, not where I would, and in the event I found myself at the farther side of the city, near where the outer wall ran down toward the harbour. I came out unseen, and—even in that extremity—closed all traces behind me. Ordinances are a kind of memory; break them once, and the next breaking comes easier.

The earth under my feet had lost its dead solidity. It sprang like a ship’s deck in smooth water, alive with a subtle, sickening give. Yet the people slept on in their houses and shelters without alarm. They did not listen well. They had for years lived amid the ceaseless clang of Phorenice’s city—metal on stone, shouts in narrow streets, the coarse blare of spectacle—and their ears had grown dulled, as tongues grow dulled when a mother’s dialect is mocked into silence. The ground was speaking. They could not translate it.

Gods—should I be too late? Would Nais be laid bare to public gaze when fear drove people into open spaces and their rude hands seized whatever lay near? I saw in fancy their grasping fingers, the careless curiosity, the cruelty that comes when terror has made men forget reverence. My inwards dried at the thought.

I ran down and down the narrow winding streets with the single purpose of reaching the square before the royal pyramid before that could happen. With my own hands, nine years ago, I had been forced to place her alive in her burying-place beneath the granite throne. If thews and speed could do it, I would not miss the reward of taking her forth again with those same strong hands.

Few disturbed my hurry. At first, here and there, some gutter-wretch cried, “A thief! Throw a share or I pursue!” If any followed, I do not know. My speed would have outdistanced them. Soon, as the earth’s swing grew keener and the stonework of buildings began to grate and groan—dust sifting down in quick little showers—people started to run with scared cries from their doors. But none tried to stop the ragged, shaggy savage who ran so swiftly and flung the mud from his naked feet.

At last I came to the great square, none too soon. It was filling with people who fled the narrow streets, and it was full of darkness, noise, dust, and sickness. Beneath us the ground rippled in slow undulations like a sea whose swell is gathering. The terror in it was its patience: each wave more sure than the last.

Ever and again a house crashed down unseen in the gloom and added to the tumult. But the great pyramid had been planned for rude shocks. Its stones were dovetailed with marvellous cunning and clamped by tongues of metal. It was a boast that half its foundations could be dug away and still it would stand, square to heaven, more enduring than hills.

Flickering torches showed its great stone doors stood open, and now and again some frightened inmate scurried out and vanished into the dark. For the pyramid’s fate I had little concern then. I did not even care whether Phorenice was trapped or escaped to do further mischief. I crouched by the granite throne, and listened to its stones grate together like broken bone as it rocked upon the earth-waves.

In that dust-thick night it was hard to see the outline of one’s own hand. Yet I think the Gods, in some requital for love that had ached so long, gave me an edge of sight. As I watched, the great carved rock that formed the capstone moved—slightly, then again, then again. A tiny jerk for each pulse of earth; but there was shifting, and always to one side.

There was method in Zaemon’s desperate work, and in my blind panic I had overlooked it. I went up the throne’s steps on the side toward which the capstone crept, and clung there, all my blood afire with expectation.

The earth-swing grew more violent, though its increase came in such graduations one could not measure them. The din of falling houses and the shrieks of hurt and frightened people rose louder into the night. Dust thickened till breathing was choking, till coughs tore at the chest; the rare stars were blotted away. I crouched on the upper steps, one below the capstone, and could scarcely keep my place against the tremors.

Still the huge stone—carved with the snake and the outstretched hand—held my love locked in her living tomb. I could have bitten the granite in impotent rage. The square thronged fuller every moment, and their numbers made my case more desperate. “Phorenice, Goddess, aid us now!” some cried. When no instant relief came, they began to yammer out old confessions learned in childhood, turning back in their dread to Gods they had spurned through years of dishonour. Their faith, like their language, returned only when the dominant voice failed them.

The crash of masonry grew. From the pyramid—though its outline was lost in blackness—came grinding of stones and the crack of metal bars under strain. Zaemon’s old threat in the painted, perfumed banqueting-hall came back to me, ringing in memory like a gong: “You shall see this royal pyramid which you have polluted torn tier from tier, stone from stone, scattered as feathers before a wind!”

Heavier grew the surging of earth. The square’s paving gaped and heaved; people screamed as their feet were crushed by grinding blocks. Now even the pyramid began to split and topple. Roofs gave way; ponderous masonry shuttered down and filled splendid chambers. And then the sky itself joined the uproar: thunders roared above, and vivid lightning splashes lit the flying dust-clouds, turning the ruin into brief, stark pictures.

In one of those harsh illuminations I saw Phorenice safe in the square, standing not far from me.

It was perhaps natural she should be there, but it struck like a blow. She stood on the middle of a great flagstone, swaying her supple body to the shocks as a sailor sways on a deck. Her face was calm; its loveliness untouched by years. From time to time she brushed dust from the short red hair curled at her neck. There was no fear on her—only weariness, contempt, and, I thought, a tinge of amusement. It took more than the crumbling of her royal pyramid to impress Phorenice with powers arrayed against her.

The sight maddened me. I had it in me to strangle her if she came within reach. But she stood where she was, and beside her, crouched on the same flagstone and overcome with nausea, was Ylga—again made her fan-girl. In the lightning’s white bite of light, I remembered Ylga was twin sister to Nais, and that I owed her for an ancient kindness; but there was leisure for nothing. With each shock the capstone jarred farther from its bed. My love was coming nearer. She claimed all my service.

Once, in blind panic, a knot of people thought the granite throne too solid to be overturned and saw in it an oasis of safety. They flocked toward it, some dragging themselves up the steep steps on hands and knees because their feet had been injured by the billowing flagstones.

I was in no mood to have the place profaned by their trembling and staring. I beat at them with my hands, tore them away, hurled them back down the steps. They cried to know my title above theirs. I answered with blows and gnashing teeth. I did not care what they thought me. Only I wished them gone. And they went, wailing that I was a devil of the night; their spirit was too broken to defend themselves.

Farther and farther the great capstone slid, and its slowness maddened me. A life’s training in patience left me in that moment. I thrust at the block with shoulder and head, clawed at it with my hands until muscles stood out in ropes and knots. The High Gods must have laughed at my helplessness. All was ordered by the Three in Their good time. The work of the Gods may be slow, but it is sure.

At last—when the people were numbed with terror and incapable of more emotion, save Phorenice who still showed no concern—the threatened thing came to pass. The capstone slid until it reached balance, and the next shock threw it with a roar and clatter to the ground.

A strange tremor seized me then. What I had prayed for had come about. Yet my inwards sank at the thought of mounting again where I had mounted before, of reaching down into the hollow where my own hands had laid her. I knew Phorenice’s vengefulness; I valued her cleverness. Had she left Nais to lie in peace? Had she stolen her away to suffer indignities elsewhere? Had she ended her sleep with death and left the corpse for my finding as a grisly jest? I could not tell. I dared not guess. Never in a hard-fighting life had my emotions been so wrenched. Love for Nais had sapped my hardihood in a matter that was privately hers.

It came to me, too, that the infernal uproar was beginning to slacken, as though Zaemon knew he had done his promised work and meant to give the wretched city a breathing-space. I took my fortitude in hand and clambered up onto the flat of the fallen stone. The lightning had ceased; all was darkness again and stifling dust. At any moment the sky might flare once more, and if I were seen there—shaggy, changed though I was—Phorenice would not be slow to guess my name and errand.

So changed was I that I confess the idea of a fight was loathsome. I wanted my business done and to be gone.

With hands that shook, I fumbled over the stone’s face and found the clamps and bars of metal still where I had clenched them nine years before. I let my fingers pass between, and felt the curves of my love’s body at rest beneath. Exultation whirled in me. I did not know if she had been touched since I left her. I did not know if the drug would still do its office and bring her back to warmth and sight. But dead or alive, I had her there, and she was mine—mine—and I could have yelled aloud in joy at the mere fact of possession.

The earth still shook; masonry still roared and crashed. I had to cling with one hand while I unhasped the clamps with the other. At last I swung the upper half clear and left those that pinned her feet. I stooped, drew her soft body up onto the stone beside me, and pressed my lips again and again to the face I could not see.

Some mad thought took me that the heat and fierceness of kisses might bring her back to wakefulness. I will own it plainly: I did sorry credit to my training in calmness. She lay in my arms cold and nerveless as a corpse. By degrees sober wits returned.

This was no place for either of us. Let the tremors cease—as they threatened. Let daylight come. Let the panicked recover enough to think of names and gains. Then all the great cost would be wasted. We would be seen. Someone would put a name to Nais, and then it would be easy to guess Deucalion beneath beard and shaggy hair and browned nakedness. By the Gods, I was scared as the veriest trembler when that thought struck.

With ruin spread everywhere, it was hopeless to rely on any secret galleries under the ground; they would be broken, choked, or betrayed by collapse. The old means of passing under the walls was useless. But shouts from the frightened mob, carried through din and darkness, gave me another idea. “The city is accursed,” they cried. “If we stay here it will fall on us. Let us get outside the walls where there are no buildings to bury us.”

If they went, I could not see. But one gate lay nearest the pyramid, and in panic men do not go farther than needful. I slung Nais over my shoulder to leave my right arm free and blundered off through the stifling dark.

It was hard to keep direction. It was hard to walk over ground tossed like a frozen sea. As the earth still quaked, it was hard to keep footing. If I fell a score of times, my dear burden got no bruise. At last I reached the skirts of the square and found a street I knew. The worst of the shaking was done; no more buildings fell, but enough lay sprawled across the roadway to turn walking into climbing. Sweat rolled from me as I laboured.

There was no difficulty at the gate. There was no gate. There was no wall. The Gods had driven Their plough through it, and it lay flat. Proud Atlantis stood as defenceless as open country. Though I knew the cause, though I had in some measure incited it, I was almost sad at the ruthlessness. The pyramid might go; houses and palaces might be levelled—these I could spare. But when I saw the stately ramparts also filched away, the soldier in me woke and grieved at the humbling of the mighty city that once had been my only mistress.

It was a passing regret. I had a different love now, wrapped round me deeper and tighter. My duty was toward her first. Night would soon be past, and then danger would increase. None had interfered with us so far, though many had jostled me as I clambered over ruins. That forbearance could not last. The tremors were dying, and after panic and storm comes the time for spoiling.

The poor seize what lies nearest. Those of higher station—and any soldiers still true to command—strip and stop any man they see making off with plunder. I had no mind to clash with such guardians of law and property. I fled swiftly through the night with my burden, taking unfrequented ways, crying to the few who met me that the woman had the plague and would they lend shelter, since ours had fallen. The word “plague” was understood in any tongue; fear translates quickly.

So in time we came to the place where the rope dangled from the precipice. Nais was drawn up to the Sacred Mountain’s safety, and then I put my leg into the loop and followed.

Now came the keenest anxiety. We carried the girl and laid her upon a bed in one of the houses, and there, in a room lit cleanly for the first time, I saw her clearly.

Her beauty was drawn and pale. Her eyes were closed, but so thin and transparent had the lids grown that one could almost see the brown of the pupil beneath. Her hair had grown to inordinate thickness and length and lay as a cushion behind and beside her head, a dark, heavy abundance like stored grain left too long in a bin. There was no flicker of breath, no pulsing of life; and yet she did not look like ordinary death. The Nais I had placed nine years before beneath the stone had been a fine grown woman, full-bosomed and well boned. The Nais that remained was half her weight. The old Nais would have puzzled me to carry an hour; this was no burden to impede a man.

In other ways she had altered. The nails of her fingers had grown to great length and twisted in spirals, like vine tendrils seeking a trellis that was not there. Her fingers and hands were waxy and transparent so that the bony core showed beneath the flesh in dull outline. Her lips were clay-cold and so white that I sighed to remember their carmine. Her shoulders and neck had lost their comely curves; bony hollows had formed where dust of entombment lodged black and thick.

Reverently I set about preparing those things which, if all went well, should restore her. I heated water and filled a bath, and tinctured it heavily with those essences of the life of beasts which the Priests extract and store against urgent need and sickness—sharp, animal-sweet spirits that rose with the steam and struck the nostrils like the memory of fresh blood and warm hide, like broth made from marrow bones simmered until it gives up its strength. I laid her chin-deep in this bath and sat beside it to watch, keeping the water at a constant blood heat.

Nais herself sent me away from her side.

The first shouts of the assault came rolling through the house like surf in a shell, the sound thickened by stone corridors and the hollow organs of the cleft. Even in her weakness she turned her head to listen, as if the Mountain’s very breath had changed pitch.

“What is that?” she asked, and the words rasped softly, like a spoon in an empty bowl.

So I told her—shortly, because her eyelids were heavy and her mind only newly thawed—how she had been taken from me into that long, unnatural sleep, and how I had held off all barter and all peace until they gave me back the Queen I desired, safe and living, here upon the Sacred Mountain.

A flush rose in her cheeks, as though a hidden ember had been stirred. “My lord is the properest man in all the world to be King,” she whispered.

“I refused to touch the trade till they had given me the Queen I desired, safe and alive, here upon the Mountain.”

“How we poor women are made the chattels of you men!” There was a faint bite of laughter in it, and then a sigh. “But, for myself, I seem to like the traffic well enough. You should not have let me stand in the way of Atlantis’ good, Deucalion. Still, it is very sweet to know you were weak there for once, and that I was the cause of your weakness. What is that bath over yonder? Ah! I remember; my wits seem none of the clearest just now.”

“You have made the beginning. Your strength will return to you by quick degrees. But it will not bear hurrying. You must have a patience.”

“Your ear, sir, for one moment, and then I will rest in peace. My poor looks, are they all gone? You seem to have no mirror here. I had visions that I should wake up wrinkled and old.”

“You are as you were, dear, that first night I saw you—the most beautiful woman in all the world.”

“I am pleased you like me,” she said, and accepted the cup I held. The broth was plain—roots and a little dried flesh, boiled long until it yielded—yet in that chamber it smelled like a promise. I had salted it carefully, pinching the grains between finger and thumb the way the older priests did, with the reverence of men who know that a body returns by mouth as much as by prayer. She sipped, and the sound of it—small, steady swallows—cut cleanly through the distant clangor outside, a domestic rhythm set against the war-drum of the pass.

“My hair seems to have grown; but it needs combing sadly. I had a fancy, dear, once, that you liked ruddy hair best, and not a plain brown.”

“It is yours I like,” I told her, and she let her eyes close again. Sleep took her quickly, not the heavy, drugged fall of the nine-year enchantment, but a healthy sinking as when bread is left to rise in warmth and darkness. Smiles still played at the corners of her mouth. I drew the coverlet higher and kissed her lightly, taking care that my beard did not brush her cheek.

And then I went out.

That beard had become hateful to me in these last hours—an unkempt banner of the years I had been buried. In a side room I found instruments and shaved down to a bare chin. The scrape of the blade, the rinse of water, the clean sting of skin newly bared—these were small acts, but they restored something of order. A robe, too, I found and took in place of my squalid rags. If a man is in truth a king, he owes such decencies to the dignity of his office, even when the crown is only a word shouted over stones.

But if the sound of the fighting was any guide, mine was a kingdom narrowing by the hour.

Each minute the noise thickened and drew nearer: the clang of iron on stone, the ragged cheer of men forcing themselves to courage, the duller, wetter sounds that follow when bodies meet. It was plain that some of the gates in the cleft—gates men had called impregnable, as if a name were mortar—had yielded under the vehemence of Phorenice’s storm. Nor was it marvel. With all her genius spurred into fury by the blow we had struck in the city, she was no light adversary, and the Sacred Mountain was no longer strong.

Stone defenses, cunningly planned and mightily built, we still possessed. But walls do not fight alone. They need men to line them—abundance of men. In such a storm some desperate fellows spit at death and come to grips; slingers and archers slip a shot through; throwing-fire finds a seam; or some new device, like Phorenice’s fire-tubes, makes one wavering dart in a thousand find a throat. And so, though the general attacking loses his hundreds, the defenders are not without their dead.

The slaughter below had been prodigious.

As fast as the stormers came up, the Priests who held the lowest remaining gate rained down great rocks until the narrow stairway was paved with writhing bodies. But Phorenice stood on a spur of rock beneath, urging charge upon charge. Company after company marched up to hurl themselves hopelessly against the defences. They had no engines to batter the massive gates; their assault was as useless as a child hammering a wall with an orange, and all the while the stones from above mowed them down remorselessly.

It was no part of Phorenice’s policy that any should return. In her lust for the final conquest she meant to pour out troops until the passes were filled with the slain, and then march on to a level fight over the bridge of their bodies. She never counted cost. She set down the object to be gained, and it was her policy that the people of Atlantis existed to gain it for her.

Two gates she had carried in this dreadful fashion, slaughtering the priests behind them—those not already shot down. And then I came down from above to take my share.

There was no trumpet to announce me, no herald to proclaim quality. But the Priests, from sheer custom and need, snatched up my name as a cry. “Deucalion!” they shouted, and some called that with a King to lead there would be no further ground lost.

The name carried. Across the abyss of din I saw its effect below: a company of poor doomed wretches, hurrying up to their death, faltered in their charge. “Deucalion” was bandied from mouth to mouth and handed back down their line like a scrap of old song remembered at the lip. I took grim satisfaction in it; here was proof I had not been wholly forgotten.

Shouts came from behind them to press on, but they did not budge. Presently a glittering officer panted up, striking right and left with his sword to drive them forward. From where I stood on the high rampart above the gate I could see his face clearly, and knew him at once.

“It matters not what they use for their battle-cry,” he shouted. “You have the orders of your divine Empress, and that is enough. You should be proud to die for her wish, you cowards. And if you do not obey, you will die afterwards under the instruments of the tormentors, very painfully. As for Deucalion, he is dead any time these nine years.”

“There it seems you lie, my Lord Tatho,” I called down.

He started and looked up. For a moment the tumult seemed to thin around him, as if even the air wanted to listen.

“So you are there in real truth, then? Well, old comrade, I am sorry. But it is too late to make a composition now. You are on the side of these mangy Priests, and the Empress has made an edict that they are to be rooted out, and I am her most obedient servant.”

“You used to be skilful of fence,” I said. There was little enough to choose between us once. “If it please you to stop this pitiful killing, make yourself the champion of your side, and I will stand for mine, and we will fight out this quarrel in some fair place, and bind our parties to abide by the result.”

“It would be a grand fight between us two, old friend, and it goes hard with me to balk you of it. But I cannot pleasure you. I am general here under Phorenice, and she has given me the strongest orders not to peril myself. And besides, though you are a great man, Deucalion, you are not chief. You are not even one of the Three.”

“I am King.”

Tatho laughed. “Few but yourself would say so, my lord.”

“Few truly,” I answered, “but what there are, they are powerful. I was given the name for the first time yesterday; and as a first blow in the campaign there was some mischief done in the city. I was there myself, and saw how you took it.”

“You were in Atlantis!”

“I went for Nais. She is on the mountain now, and to-morrow will be my Queen. Tatho, as priest to priest, let me solemnly bring to your memory the infinite power you bite against on this Sacred Mountain. Your teaching has warned you of the weapons that are stored in the Ark of the Mysteries. If you persist in this attack, at the best you can merely lose; at the worst you can bring about a wreck over which even the High Gods will shudder as They order it.”

“You cannot scare us back now by words,” he said doggedly. “And as for magic, it will be met by magic. Phorenice has found by her own cleverness as many powers as were ever stored up in the Ark of the Mysteries.”

“Yet she looked on helplessly enough last night, when her royal pyramid was trundled into a rubbish heap. Zaemon had prophesied that this should be so, and for a witness—why I myself stood closer to her than we two stand now, and saw her.”

“I will own you took her by surprise somewhat there. I do not understand these matters myself; I was never more than one of the Seven in the old days; and now, quite rightly, Phorenice keeps the knowledge of her magic to herself. But it seems time is needed when one magic is to be met by another.”

“Well,” I said, “I know little about the business either. I leave these matters now to those who are higher above me in the priesthood. Indeed, having a liking for Nais, it seems I am debarred from ever being given understanding about the highest of the higher Mysteries. So I content myself with being a soldier, and when the appointed day comes, I shall fall and kiss my mother the Earth for the last time. You, so I am told, have ambition for longer life.”

He nodded once, hard. “Phorenice has found the Great Secret, and I am to be the first that will share it with her. We shall be as Gods upon the earth, seeing that Death will be powerless to touch us. And the twin sons she has borne me will be made immortal also.”

“Phorenice is headstrong. No, my lord—there is no need to shake your head and try to deny it. I have had some acquaintance with her. But the order has been made, and her immortality will be snatched from her very rudely. Now mark solemnly my words. I, Deucalion, have been appointed King of Atlantis by the High Council of the Priests who are the mouthpiece of the most High Gods, and if I do not have my reign, then there will be no Atlantis left to carry either King or Empress. You know me, Tatho, for a man that never lies.”

He nodded again.

“Then save yourself before it is too late. You shall have again your vice-royalty in Yucatan.”

His face tightened with a bitterness I had not expected. “But, man, there is no Yucatan. A great horde of little hairy creatures, that were something less than human and something more than beasts, swept down upon our cities and ate them out. Oh, you may sneer if you choose! Others sneered when I came home, till the Empress stopped them. But you know what a train of driver ants is, that you meet with in the forests? You may light fires across their path, and they will march into them in their blind bravery, and put them out with their bodies, and those that are left will march on in an unbroken column, and devour all that stands in their path. I tell you, my lord, those little hairy creatures were like the ants—aye, for numbers, and wooden bravery, as well as for appetite. As a result to-day, there is no Yucatan.”

“Then you shall have Egypt.”

He burst out hotly, loud enough that even the men below flinched at the edge of his voice. “I would not take seven Egypts and ten Yucatans. My lord, you think more poorly of me than is kind, when you ask me to become a traitor. In your place would you throw your Nais away, if the doing it would save you from a danger?”

“That is different.”

“In no degree. You have a kindness for her. I have all that and more for Phorenice, who is, besides, my wife and the mother of my children. If I have qualms—and I freely confess I know you are desperate men up there, and have dreadful powers at your command—my shiverings are for them and not for myself. But I think, my lord, this parley is leading to nothing, and though these common soldiers here will understand little enough of our talk, they may be picking up a word here and there, and I do not wish them to go on to their death (as you will see them do shortly) and carry evil reports about me to whatever Gods they chance to come before.”

He saluted me with his sword and drew back. At once the air filled again with motion and hurt. Missiles began to fly; the doomed wretches who had been halting under the steep rock-walls pressed forward. They had scaling-ladders, yes, but no chance to plant them. They could do nothing but fill the narrow way with their bodies. For that they had been sent, and to that end they humbly died.

Our Priests, stripped and sweating, worked with crow and lever, wrenching from their beds the great rocks that had been made ready. Each time a stone tore free there was a groan like an old tree giving up at last, then the crash and the sudden, high screams as it found flesh. It was a hideous music: rock on rock, bone on stone, men yelling to drown out the knowledge of what they did. There is an ecology of sound on a battlefield—how it crowds the mind, how it makes cruelty easier because thought is smothered in noise. I had known forests where the only great sound was wind and water, and a man could hear his own conscience. Here, in the cleft, nothing could breathe quietly. Even prayer felt battered into fragments before it reached the lips.

And always some arrow or sling-stone, or one thin dart from a fire-tube, would slip through and steal a life from our side. Phorenice could be lavish. For her, loss was a figure on a tally-stick; when one levy died, she could press others into service, for the whole breadth of the country lay under her rule. With us it was different. A man down was an arm irrecoverably missing.

I weighed our chances and saw the truth: if we kept fighting gate by gate, they would storm each one in turn, and by the time the last, uppermost gate was reached there would be no Priest alive to hold it.

So, borrowing from Phorenice’s newer doctrine—that a general should sometimes plot from safety rather than lead every rush—I left those at the gate with rough soldier’s cheer and withdrew up the narrow stair.

This approach to the Sacred Mountain had once been far more perilous when it was only the raw cleft the High Gods made. But men had bridged a chasm here, cut a shelf through solid rock there, built roadway on piers set out from distant crags, until what had been a goat’s terror became a uniform path. As I climbed, it came to me that if we destroyed the way itself, we might gain a breathing space.

It was a good thought, or at least no other offered relief. Yet though I had traversed that stair of a pass thousands of times and could have walked it blind, I had never looked on it with an eye to unmaking it. Even now, in this dreadful stress, I had to pace it three times, measuring by instinct and memory, until I fixed upon the best place for demolition.

Once the point was chosen there was little delay. I sent men to the storehouses among the Priests’ dwellings to fetch rams and crows, acids and hammers—whatever would bite stone and loosen the bones of the road. We put every pair of hands we could spare to the work, no matter their age or feebleness. Had Nais been able to walk so far, I would have pressed even her into it; for in a siege all tenderness is rationed.

Then the Mountain’s own handiwork began to die.

Carved balustrade and wayside statue, lettered wall-stones and foot-worn cobbles—marks of old feet, old processions, old hymns—were broken free and sent roaring into the gulf. The crash rose to join the yells and shrieks below. Gods, it was hateful, smashing down that splendid craft of the men of the past; like tearing the pages from an elder’s book because an enemy might read it and mock. Yet better the stones should fall into ruin than that Phorenice should profane them with her impious sandals.

At first I feared we would have to sacrifice the knot of brave men still holding the attacked gate. War often forces a general to leave some to fight to the finish without hope, and all he can do is commend them to the Gods. But as the path’s destruction neared completion, I saw a way to retrieve them.

We had not been content with breaking arches and throwing down piers. We had got our rams and levers under the living rock itself on which the whole fabric stood; fire stood ready to heat the rams so they would expand with resistless force. When the word was given, the remaining ledge could be torn away beyond repair.

All was prepared thus, and then I ordered them to hold.

A narrow strip of ledge still remained. Over this I crossed. The cut we had made lay immediately below the uppermost gate of all. Beneath it were three more massive gates still unviolated, besides the one being attacked. Their garrisons had already been retired. I passed through them unchallenged, closing each behind me with the sense of shutting doors upon old certainties, until I came to the busy rampart where twelve Priests still alive worked stripped to the waist, heaving down the murderous rocks.

For a while I worked with them, catching an occasional arrow or fire-tube dart on my shield, passing news in quick phrases. The attack grew fiercer by the minute. The enemy had packed the pass nearly full of their dead; our stones now had less distance to fall and so did less execution. The stormers pressed forward with their scaling-ladders more eagerly than ever. It was plain they would soon put the place to the storm.

Even in that short time, sling-stones and darts took three more lives from the twelve at my side.

So I called for one last furious avalanche. While the living crawled free of the new-killed and the next wave of reinforcements scrambled up over bodies, I sent my nine remaining men running up the steep stair, and followed. Gate after gate we passed, shutting them behind and breaking the bars and levers that moved them. Not until we were through the last did the roar below tell us the besiegers had found their prize deserted.

One by one we balanced across the narrow ledge left where the path had been destroyed. A Priest with a wound grew dizzy, lost his balance, and toppled down into the abyss before a hand could steady him. His cry was swallowed by the gulf; it is strange how quickly the Mountain eats sound when it wishes.

When we were all over, we put heat to the rams. They expanded and tore the remaining ledges from their hold. The last connecting link between our handful of the Sacred Clan above and the broad, estranged nation below was cut away. I think a pang went through us all at that sight—not only fear, but grief, as though a familiar dialect had gone suddenly silent in the mouth.

Here, at any rate, was a break in the fighting. There were no further preparations we could make; and high though I knew Phorenice’s genius, I did not see how she could do other than accept the check and retire. I set a guard upon the ramparts of the uppermost gate to watch for movement and sent the others to find the rest their bodies cried for.

For myself I tried first to find Zaemon. Dutifully I went as I ought, for little enough kingly state was observed upon the Sacred Mountain though the title had been given me. But Zaemon could not be come at. He was within the Ark of the Mysteries with another of the Three, and I, being only one of the Seven, had not rank enough to break in upon their workings.

So I turned where my likings would have led me first: back to the house that sheltered Nais.

She woke as I crossed the threshold, as though she had been listening for my foot. Her eyes filled with welcome. I went to her and knelt, laying my face on the pillow beside hers. She spoke in tender fragments, sweet endearments, and little household fancies that made me ache at what I had lost by not knowing her earlier—an infinity of delight, like a lifetime of meals eaten without tasting.

She teased me for my new-cleanness of chin and for the robe I had taken. She professed a pretty awe of my kingship and vowed that had she known of my coming dignities she would never have dared to discover love for me. But of my marriage with Phorenice she spoke with less lightness. She put out her thin white hand and drew my face close to her lips.

“It is weak of me to have a jealousy,” she murmured, “knowing how completely my lord is mine alone; but I cannot help it. You have said you were her husband for awhile. It gives me a pang to think that I shall not be the first to lie in your arms, Deucalion.”

“Then you may gaily throw your pang away,” I whispered back. “I was husband to Phorenice in mere word, for how long I do not precisely know. But in anything beyond, I was never her husband at all. She married me by a form she prescribed herself, ignoring all the old rites and ceremonies; and whether it would hold as legal or not, we need not trouble to inquire. She herself has most nicely and completely annulled that marriage, as I have told you. Tatho is her husband now, and father to her children, and he seems to have a fondness for her which does him credit.”

We said other things, too—those small repetitions lovers hoard like spices, the phrases that mean nothing to any ear but the one that has learned their taste. They are not for setting down here. Weightier subjects press their way into the historian’s index.

For Phorenice, it seemed, had more rage against the Priests’ Clan on the Mountain, and more bright genius to help her to vengeance, than I had credited.

Her troops stormed easily the gates we had left to them and swarmed up until they stood where the pathway was broken down. In the heat of their rush the foremost were thrust over the brink by those pressing behind before the advance could be checked, and they went screaming to a horrid death in the gulf. But no lavish spending of men could take a position like that. Presently all were drawn off save a half-score, left as outpost sentries, who dodged behind angles of rock out of arrow-shot.

It seems, too, that the Empress herself came to reconnoitre the break, using caution and quickness, taking the measure with her own eye rather than trusting another’s report. And with extraordinary nimbleness she must have planned the engine she required and given orders for its making; for even with her vast force and resources, the speed with which it rose was prodigious.

Very little noise betrayed what was afoot. Below on the plain at the foot of the cleft, skilled craftsmen cut the wood and tongued it; metal was forged and fitted; each balk, each crosspiece, each plank was finished to its purpose before it ever began the climb. And when at last the ponderous pieces were dragged up the steep pass through the conquered gates, each came ready at once for its appointed place in the machine that Phorenice meant to set against our broken road.

A tottering old Priest came up through the press and laid a hand upon my shoulder.

“Well?” I snapped, for my blood was up and I had small relish for any voice that was not the ring of steel.

“News has been carried to the Three, my King, of what is threatened.”

“Then they know I stand here,” I said, and kept my eyes on the pass where Phorenice’s tower crouched like a carrion-bird, “to take the finest fight of my life. When it is done I shall go to the Gods, and stand behind the stars to welcome them when they arrive. Tell them I regret they are too old and too feeble to die properly and see a clean killing.”

“I have commands from them, my King,” the old man said, and there was pity in his cracked voice, “which I fear you will like but slenderly. You are forbidden to find your death here. They have further use for you yet.”

I turned on him so sharply the bronze rings on my corslet bit my skin.

“I take no such order. I will not believe it was given. You have misunderstood. If I am man, Priest, soldier, King—then no enemy passes this gate whilst I live. Go back and throw that message at their teeth.”

He smiled, not mocking, but with the sour envy of an old fighter whose arm has withered.

“I told them you would not easily believe. I asked for a sign. They bore with me, and gave me one. I was to give you this jewel, my King.”

He put the thing into my palm. Even through sweat and grime I knew the weight of it: a bracelet, worked with the old spirals and the sun-disks, warmed by a woman’s flesh so long that it seemed to hold a faint echo of her scent—salt, clean skin, and that sharp sweetness of the mountain herbs Nais loved to bruise between her fingers.

“How came they by that?” I demanded. “It is from the elbow of Nais.”

“They must have stripped her of it. I did not know. The word I was to bring you said the owner of the jewel is inside the Ark of the Mysteries, and waits you there. The use the Three have for you concerns her also.”

The gate behind me shook with the rhythm of siege. Every blow carried its own note: stone groaning, wood splitting, iron ringing; and underneath it, like a drum too vast for human hands, the mountain’s own mutter. Yet it was the bracelet that unseated me. A man can make his peace with dying. It is harder to make peace with leaving the living unguarded.

I will not pretend obedience came easy. The promise of that battle was a wine strong enough to make a man forget his vows. But love for Nais—love and that stubborn old habit of discipline that had been kneaded into me since boyhood like dough under a hard palm—prevailed. I gave the salute that showed I had heard, and would obey.

To the knot of Priests left to hold the gate, I turned and made my farewells.

“You will have what I shall miss, my brothers,” I said. “I envy you that fight. But though I am King of Atlantis, I am only one of the Seven and the servant of the Three. They speak the will of the most High Gods. You will stand behind the stars before I come. Commend me to Those you meet there. It is not my will that I shall not be by your side.”

They smiled, courteous even in the teeth of ruin, and saluted me with their weapons. Then I left them to their glory.

I did not see the fight, but I know it was good from the time that passed before Phorenice’s hordes broke out upon the crest of the Mountain. Those last lesser Priests of Atlantis died hard.

With bitterness on my tongue I went up to the head of the pass, through groves where the leaves trembled though there was no wind, and between temples and colleges and houses perched upon the upper slopes of the Sacred Mountain. In gentler days that boundary meant death to any unprivileged foot that crossed it. But the world was shedding its old rules like a snake its skin, and my temper was too hot for reverence.

Yet I was stopped.

In the temple that marked the boundary stood old Zaemon, waiting as if the mountain itself had sent him there. He would not grant me passage until he had spoken the due words and performed the whole of some ancient ritual to win dispensation from the High Gods for my trespass. His voice, thin and ceremonial, sounded oddly beside the clangour below, like a prayer recited in a kitchen while the roof catches fire.

Already Phorenice’s tower and bridge were set, and the clash and yelling told the handful of Priests at the last gate were trading their lives for the highest tally they could earn. They were trained, but old; the odds too monstrous to be stemmed for long. Peace, if such a word could still live, was shrinking by the breath.

But Zaemon would not hurry. He led me to the Ark of the Mysteries, chiding my impatience, and waited until I had given it the reverential kiss. The Ark’s metal was cool beneath my lips, and tasted faintly of old incense ground into its seams by generations of hands. Then Zaemon called aloud, and from the opening at the top of the Ark another old man appeared—hoary, frail, emaciated—climbing down by the battens fixed to its sides.

I had never seen him before. He and Zaemon were the only two remaining Priests raised to the highest degree of our Clan, the only two who held the last keys to the highest secrets, powers, and mysteries.

“Look!” Zaemon cried, and swept a trembling finger over the shattered city and the wide spread of sea and country below.

I followed his pointing, and a chill crawled through me.

The Lord Sun burned high in a sky of cloudless blue; day shimmered in His heat. From that height, the world should have looked calm—fields, roofs, water, the tidy geometry of a kingdom that had fed itself for ages: grain carried in baskets, fish gutted on stones, bread baked in dome ovens whose smoke once rose sweet with fennel-stalk and olivewood. But peace was a lie.

The mountains smoked more than ordinary, some spouted fires. The sea boiled with a strange disorder. Far out, surf surged against the rocks in rolls too huge for any tide. Up the great estuary—at whose head Atlantis stood—the sea gushed in successive waves of enormous height that never returned.

Already the lower lands were blotted out. Harbour walls drowned. The flood crept into the lower wards of the city itself. Where once the air had been salted with drying nets and spice and hot bread, there was only a raw reek of churned mud and brine, and a sound that made the mind go thin: a continual roar as if the whole world were one vast kettle coming to boil.

“You have seen?” Zaemon asked.

“I have seen.”

“You understand?”

“In part.”

“Then I will tell you all. This is the beginning, and the end will follow swiftly. The most High Gods who sit behind the stars have a limit to even Their sublime patience. That has been passed. Atlantis, the great continent beyond, and all that are in them are doomed to unutterable destruction. Of old it was foreseen this wiping-out would come through the sins of men, and for this the Ark of the Mysteries was built under direction of the Gods.

“No mortal implement can scratch its surface. No waves or rocks can wreck it. Inside is stored on sheets of the ancient writing all that is known in the world of learning not shared by the common people. Also there is grain in store, and sweet water in tanks sufficient for two persons for the space of four years, together with seeds, weapons, and all other matters deemed fit.”

He spoke of grain as if it were a prayer. In my mind I saw the vaults: baskets lined with resin, sealed jars, the careful choosing of kernels—wheat and barley, the hard little suns of millet—cleaned of chaff, dried to the snap, tucked away against damp and rot. It was strange, in that hour, to feel comfort in the thought of honest food, measured and husbanded. As if, while continents died, two mouths might still keep time with the old human ritual—hand to bowl, bowl to lips.

“Out of all this vast country,” Zaemon went on, “it has been decreed that two shall not perish. Two shall be chosen: a man and a woman, fit and proper to carry away the ancient learning and dispose of it as they see best, and afterwards to rear up a race who shall in time build another kingdom and do honour to our Lord the Sun and the other Gods in another place. The woman is within the Ark already, seated in the place appointed for her, and though she is a daughter of mine, the burden of her choosing is with you. For the man, the choice has fallen upon yourself.”

The words struck like a blow from the flat of a sword. I stood half numb.

“I do not know that I care to be a survivor.”

“You are not asked for your wishes,” Zaemon said. “You are given an order from the High Gods, who know you to be Their faithful servant.”

Habit rode me as surely as harness. I made the salutation in required form and said I heard and would obey.

“Then it remains,” he said, “to raise you to the sublime degree of the Three. If your learning is small and you will not understand the keys to many of the Powers and the highest Mysteries when they are handed to you, that fault cannot be remedied now.”

Time was short. The fight still raged down at the pass, though it was a wonder those few had held so long. Ocean rolled in upon the land in an ever-increasing flood. Mountains belched volleys of rock as weight and water worried their foundations. Soon even Phorenice’s besiegers must see their doom, and then panic would drive them like fire through dry grass.

But there was no hurrying Zaemon or the other sage. There was no temple for the ceremony, no sacrifice, no incense—only sky for a roof, the Gods for witnesses, and the roar of destruction as accompaniment. Yet they went through the raising with all the grand and majestic form age upon age had dignified, and did not curtail one sentence.

A burning mountain burst with a bellowing roar as incoming waters met its fires, flinging up steam like scalding breath. Gravely they continued, voices quavering but unbroken. Phorenice’s troops broke the last resistance and poured among the groves and temples, a frenzied stream. Still the ritual did not falter.

They say the ceremony of raising to the Three is the grandest of all connected with our holy religion. It was so. Even the tremendous circumstances could not make those frail men forget the deference due to the highest order of the Clan.

For myself, I was less rapt. I stood bareheaded in the heat, trying to concentrate, torn by foreign emotions. A continent was in the act of dying, and if Zaemon could give his whole mind to priestly syllables while the world drowned, I could not. My thoughts kept returning to Nais—her hands, her voice, the particular words she used when she was glad or angry, the way she called certain herbs by names that belonged only to our mountain dialect and would taste wrong on a stranger’s tongue.

Below, the soundscape changed minute by minute. The clean clangour of battle grew swallowed by water’s rage. The mountain’s old murmurs rose into harsh, broken thunder. It was as if the world’s familiar music—birdcalls in the groves, temple bells, the kitchen-chorus of mortars pounding grain—was being drowned beneath one vast industrial roar of ruin, without rhythm or mercy.
